Regarding This Volume

Volume 2 records uprisings, protests, regime overthrows, civil wars, and coups in 32 countries of Asia-Pacific. The regional composition and country order follow the Country Analysis Series (2026-10-07). The method of description, dataset definitions, rules for connecting countries, axioms, and assessment formulas are Volume 1.

The figures in the table below represent the number of records linked to this country in each dataset, as counted by the program from the raw data. A value of 0 indicates that there are no records in the dataset; the magnitude of the figures varies depending on the definitions, thresholds, and reporting scope of the datasets (Volume 1, Part 4).

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Camenaça Assembly and the Cailaco Struggle Modern Era (Portuguese Colonization Period) · 1719–1728

In the early 18th century, Portugal dispatched a governor to Timor and imposed in-kind taxes called finta and head taxes on the allied kingdoms (reinos). According to Durand (2011), after Governor Melo e Castro left in 1718, leaders of local kingdoms gathered at Camenaça, a southern coastal settlement, and decided to reject Portuguese authority. In 15 kingdoms from Oecusse to Ermera, an alliance was formed in 1726, using the mountain fortress of Cailaco as their base for resistance; Portuguese commander Joaquim de Matos mobilized 5,500 loyal troops and laid siege to the fortress from October 23 to December 8, approximately six weeks. De Matos's report (cited in Sá 1949, Hägerdal 2012) recorded over 700 deaths on the opposing side and 39 of his own soldiers killed. Durand describes the fighting as ending without a clear victor with the onset of the rainy season, while Damaledo (2018) states that the fortress fell but resistance continued until 1728, showing conflicting accounts of the outcome. In 1733, more than 40 kingdoms agreed to pay the finta and recognize Portuguese royal authority (Durand 2011).

  1. Durand, Frédéric (2011). Three centuries of violence and struggle in East Timor (1726-2008). Sciences Po Mass Violence & Resistance · Academic paper (online encyclopedia) — https://www.sciencespo.fr/mass-violence-war-massacre-resistance/en/document/three-centuries-violence-and-struggle-east-timor-1726-2008.html
  2. Damaledo, Andrey (2018). Divided Loyalties, ch.2. ANU Press (citing Sá 1949·Hägerdal 2012) · Monograph · Orientation: the source text is a report by a Portuguese commander — https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/n4398/pdf/ch02.pdf

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Topasses Offensive and Withdrawal to Lifau (Transfer of Capital to Dili) 1761–1769

The Portuguese-Timorese mixed-race group, the Topasses, whose Da Costa and Ornaia families alternated between alliance and opposition with Portugal. According to Durand (2011), in 1761 Dutch commander von Plüskow was killed by Francisco da Costa and António Ornaia, and in 1766 Governor Dionísio de Gusmão Galvão was killed in an attack by the Topasses. As the attacks made it difficult to maintain the Lifau outpost, on August 11, 1769, 1,200 Portuguese loyalists were transported by ship to Dili, and the Oecusse region remained under Topass influence. Damaledo (2018) describes this process as a trajectory in which continuous Timorese resistance led to the governor's assassination (1766) and the transfer of the capital to Dili (1769).

  1. Durand (2011), Sciences Po Mass Violence & Resistance · Academic journal article (online encyclopedia) — https://www.sciencespo.fr/mass-violence-war-massacre-resistance/en/document/three-centuries-violence-and-struggle-east-timor-1726-2008.html
  2. Damaledo (2018). Divided Loyalties, ch.2. ANU Press · Monograph — https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/n4398/pdf/ch02.pdf

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Laclo and Ulmera Kingdoms Uprising Uprising · 1861

After Portugal raised head taxes and introduced forced labor following the transfer of the capital to Dili, the Laclo and Ulmera kingdoms on the eastern coast rebelled. In 1861, the warriors of these kingdoms marched on Dili, established forward bases, and cut off food supplies to the administrative center. The Portuguese government secretly allied with other kingdom rulers—Rikissa, Ermera, Kailako, Maubara—and suppressed the rebels (who had joined with Hera and Manatuto), and the victorious side recorded seizing more than 1,000 cattle (Damaledo 2018, citing Portuguese records).

Data record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or UCDP (checked in data/TLS.json of this pipeline).

  1. Damaledo (2018). Divided Loyalties, ch.2. ANU Press · Monograph · Orientation: the source text is a colonial authority record — https://press-files.anu.edu.au/downloads/press/n4398/pdf/ch02.pdf

Manufai Rebellion (Rebellion of Eastern Boaventura) Uprising · 1911–1912

The uprising led by Boaventura in Luroai East, Manufahi, gained support across the territory against increased Portuguese taxation and control (CAVVR 『Chega!』 summary). According to Durand (2011), Boaventura retreated to Leo Laco with more than 12,000 people; on August 10, 1912, he broke the siege with several thousand followers, and the remaining forces dispersed or surrendered on August 11. Some Teutun residents from southern Suai and Kamenas fled to the Dutch South Belu of West Timor, and the Kamenas Luroai group of 750 obtained permission to cross (Damaledo 2018, citing La Lau 1912).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Timorese FatalitiesUnconfirmed (original Portuguese authorities' tally unverified)15,000–25,000, more than 5% of the population at the time (Durand 2011)
West Timor Refugees750 from Kamenas Luroai (recorded by Dutch border authorities, cited in La Lau 1912)More than 2,200 Teutun 'returnees' (Damaledo 2018)

Data record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 includes some colonial wars from 1816 onward, but this pipeline's TLS collection does not contain an entry for this uprising.

Viqueque Uprising Uprising · 1959

A anti-colonial uprising took place in the Viqueque area of Portuguese Timor from June 7 to June 20, 1959. The authorities arrested 65 Timorese suspects, and the leaders were exiled to Angola (Durand 2011).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
FatalitiesUnconfirmedEstimated at 500–1,000 (Durand 2011)

Data record: Not included in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO (checked in data/TLS.json of this pipeline).

  1. Durand (2011), Sciences Po Mass Violence & Resistance · Academic monograph (online encyclopedia) — https://www.sciencespo.fr/mass-violence-war-massacre-resistance/en/document/three-centuries-violence-and-struggle-east-timor-1726-2008.html

UDT–FRETILIN Civil War Civil War · 1975

On August 11, 1975, the Timorese Democratic Union (UDT) initiated armed action (a coup) in the process of Portuguese decolonization, and FRETILIN counterattacked to seize Dili on August 27 (Durand 2011). CAVR 『Chega!』 reports that in the internal armed conflict of August–September, UDT forces killed FRETILIN supporters in Liquiça, Dili, Ermera, and Manufahi, while FRETILIN forces also killed approximately 40 people in an attack on the Maubisse (Maubisse) village and executed hundreds of detainees in Aileu, Maubisse, and Same between December 1975 and January 1976. The committee recorded illegal killings by both sides.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
FatalitiesUnconfirmed (CAVR summary does not present a single total for the civil war)1,500–3,000 (Durand 2011)
West Timor RefugeesUnconfirmedApproximately 10,000 (Durand 2011)

Data record: Not included in the TLS collections of COW, UCDP, or Powell & Thyne. The UDT action on August 11, 1975, is labeled 'coup' by Durand but is not included in Powell & Thyne because it occurred in territory prior to independence.

  1. CAVR (2005). Chega!   2. Durand (2011), Sciences Po Mass Violence & Resistance · Academic monograph (online encyclopedia) — https://www.sciencespo.fr/mass-violence-war-massacre-resistance/en/document/three-centuries-violence-and-struggle-east-timor-1726-2008.html

Indonesian Invasion and Armed Resistance under Occupation Uprising · 1975–1999

Indonesia attacked and occupied Dili on December 7, 1975; the UN General Assembly (Resolution 3485, 1975.12.12) and the Security Council (Resolution 384, 1975.12.22) demanded withdrawal (Durand 2011). Resistance continued under FRETILIN's armed wing Falintil. CAVR 『Chega!』 reported that approximately 70% of the estimated 18,600 illegal killings and forced disappearances of non-combatants between 1974 and 1999 were committed by Indonesian security forces and Timorese auxiliaries, and it also recorded illegal killings by the resistance. Deaths from starvation and disease concentrated during the period of large-scale military operations in 1977–1978 and during the 1979 internment camp period. Following the Kraras (Viqueque) incident in 1983, more than 200 civilian executions were also recorded in September–October.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Conflict-Related Deaths (1974–1999)At least 102,800 (±12,000): 18,600 killed or disappeared (±1,000) + 84,200 excess deaths from starvation and disease (±11,000), upper bound 183,000 (CAVR 『Chega!』 2005, HRDAG statistical analysis)Indonesian sources: at least 150,000 (80,000 military operations · 70,000 famine); independent sources: approximately 250,000 (Durand 2011 compilation)
Indonesian combatant fatalities (COW)5,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 'East Timorese War phase 3 of 1976-1979', Indonesia vs FRETILIN; pipeline IDN collection)

Data record: COW records this war under the Indonesia (IDN) state entry; not present in the pipeline TLS collection. NAVCO 1.3: 'Freitilin' 1975–1979 violence and failure (TLS collection).

Santa Cruz Massacre (Dili Massacre) Demonstration · 1991

On November 12, 1991, when approximately 3,000 demonstrators gathered at Santa Cruz Cemetery in Dili to march to the grave of Sebastião Gomes, an activist killed during the October 28 attack on the Motael Church, Indonesian security forces fired automatic weapons for several minutes (ICJ 1992). The National Commission of Inquiry appointed by the government issued a preliminary report on December 26, 1991. In 1992, ten soldiers were tried at the Bali Military Court; the maximum sentence was 18 months' imprisonment, and Gregório Saldanha, charged as the 'instigator' of the demonstration, received life imprisonment (HRW 1993). The number of deaths varies widely among the counting bodies.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
Deaths'Approximately 50' (Indonesian authorities' figure; Durand 2011); government 'missing persons' approximately 90 → revised downward to 66 (HRW 1993)50–200 (ICJ 1992); at least 75 (HRW 1993; CAVR 『Chega!』 2005 'at least 75, almost certainly higher'); human rights group list: 271 dead, 382 wounded, 250 missing (Durand 2011)

Data record: Mass Mobilization collected from 2002 onward (TLS collection) — the 1991 event is not included. Included in NAVCO 1.3 'Timorese resistance' period 1989–1999.

1999 Referendum and Militia Violence Uprising · 1999

In the August 30, 1999 referendum supervised by the UN, more than 98% of eligible voters participated, and the UN announced on September 4 that 78.5% had chosen independence (Durand 2011). CAVR's 『Chega!』 reported that in this year there was an organized violence campaign aimed at securing pro-Indonesian results and threatening the independence movement, with 1,400–1,500 deaths or disappearances and forced displacement of hundreds of thousands. Lethal violations concentrated in April, before the May 5 agreement, and in September–October, after the announcement of the vote results. The multinational force INTERFET landed in Dili on September 20.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
Deaths · Disappearances1,400–1,500 (CAVR 『Chega!』 2005); more than 1,400 civilian killings by Indonesian security forces and militias (same report)More than 1,400 (Durand 2011, citing the truth commission)
Forced displacementApproximately 250,000 forcibly displaced to West Timor, approximately 300,000 forcibly displaced within territory (CAVR 『Chega!』)Approximately 300,000 forcibly displaced to West Timor (Durand 2011)

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Timorese resistance' 1989–1999 nonviolent and successful (TLS collection). Not present in UCDP or COW TLS collections.

2006 Political and Security Crisis and the Resignation of Prime Minister Alkatiri Regime overthrow · 2006

The crisis began in January 2006 when approximately 600 out of 1,600 soldiers submitted a petition complaining of discrimination (Durand 2011), and following their dismissal, clashes and riots between military and police factions continued in April–May. The UN Independent Special Commission of Inquiry report (2006.10.2) identified the vulnerability of state institutions and the weakness of the rule of law as causes and recommended investigations into some former and serving senior officials (UN News 2006.10.5). Under daily demands for his resignation and pressure on the president to step down, Prime Minister Mari Alkatiri resigned on June 26, 2006 (AFP report).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsAt least 37 (UN News report related to UN Independent Special Commission of Inquiry, 2006.10.05)21 (counted from late May; reported by AFP 2006.06.26); 37 (Durand 2011)
Displaced PersonsApproximately 155,000, about 15% of the population (UN News 2006.10.05)Approximately 150,000 (AFP 2006.06.26; Durand 2011)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Anti-Alkatiri' 2006 nonviolent and successful. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 2 events in 2006. No record of an irregular transfer or coup in the Archigos · Powell & Thyne TLS collections.

2008 Attack on the President and Prime Minister Coup · 2008

On February 11, 2008, an attack took place against President José Ramos-Horta and Prime Minister Rui Maria Araújo. Ramos-Horta was seriously wounded but recovered, and rebel leader Alfredo Reinado was killed. Subsequently, approximately 100,000 displaced persons returned following an amnesty, and the last refugee camp closed in 2009 (Durand 2011). Whether the event constituted a coup attempt was not adjudicated according to the dataset criteria; this series classifies it under the coup entry for convenience but records it as an 'attack.'

Data Record: Powell & Thyne (pipeline collection) TLS: 0 coups and attempts — this event not included. Mass Mobilization: 1 event in 2008.

  1. Durand (2011), Sciences Po Mass Violence & Resistance · Academic paper (online encyclopedia) — https://www.sciencespo.fr/mass-violence-war-massacre-resistance/en/document/three-centuries-violence-and-struggle-east-timor-1726-2008.html

6. Related Research

  1. Commission for Reception, Truth and Reconciliation in East Timor (CAVR) (2005). Chega! Final Report — Executive Summary. Link — The official report of the Commission for Reception, Truth and Reconciliation, which uses multiple system estimation (MSE) to consolidate deaths, disappearances, and forced displacement from 1974 to 1999.
  2. Durand, Frédéric (2011). Three centuries of violence and struggle in East Timor (1726-2008). Online Encyclopedia of Mass Violence, Sciences Po. Link — A chronology of violence and resistance from 1726 (Kailako) to 2008 and an aggregation of death estimates.
  3. Hägerdal, Hans (2012). Lords of the Land, Lords of the Sea: Conflict and Adaptation in Early Colonial Timor, 1600-1800. Leiden: KITLV Press (Verhandelingen 273). Link — Reconstructs the conflict between the Kingdom of Timor and colonial powers in 1600–1800 using Dutch and Portuguese primary sources and oral accounts (open access).
  4. Damaledo, Andrey (2018). Divided Loyalties: Displacement, belonging and citizenship among East Timorese in West Timor. Canberra: ANU Press. Link — Chapter 2 describes colonial-era migration and resistance, including Kailako (1726), the 1861 uprising, and the 1912 flight.
  5. Kammen, Douglas (2015). Three Centuries of Conflict in East Timor. New Brunswick: Rutgers University Press. Link — Traces the dominant families of the Mau Barra region and recurring uprisings and pacifications from 1712 to 2012 through documents and oral accounts.
  6. Robinson, Geoffrey (2010). "If You Leave Us Here, We Will Die": How Genocide Was Stopped in East Timor. Princeton University Press. Link — The claim that the 1999 militia violence was a calculation of the Indonesian military leadership (the author was a UN political officer in 1999).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War

Struggle for the Lan Xang Throne and the Division into Three Kingdoms Late Lan Xang Kingdom · 1690s–1710s

The Lan Xang kingdom maintained its independence under the Pha Ngone dynasty in the 16th and 17th centuries, repelling Burmese invasions in 1571–1621; however, as competing claimants to the throne emerged toward the end of the 17th century, it fragmented into three kingdoms—Luang Phrabang, Vientiane, and Champasak (the U.S. Congressional Research Service describes the point of fragmentation as 1690). Following the fragmentation, the three kingdoms competed with one another, setting the stage for growing influence by Siam and Vietnam. The specific circumstances of succession after King Sulinyabot's death (a usurpation by a chief minister, struggles among his grandsons, Luang Phrabang's independence in 1707, the founding of Champasak in 1713, etc.) and the dates vary across sources; this narrative records only the range verified.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Laos (1994) — Lan Xang (reprinted from countrystudies.us) · Government report · Orientation: compiled by the U.S. government (Congressional Research Service), point of 1994 — http://countrystudies.us/laos/6.htm

5. Modern Events through Literature

Chao Anou's Anti-Siamese Uprising Uprising · 1826–1828

King Chao Anou of the Lao kingdom of Vientiane, a vassal state of Siam, took military action against Siam; in retaliation, Siam destroyed Vientiane in 1827–28. Anou fled to Huế in Vietnam, where Emperor Minh Mạng lent him two companies of troops to escort him back to Vientiane. In mid-October 1828, Anou fought again against a stronger Siamese force and retreated to Muang Phuan when the Mekong blocked the Siamese advance on Nakhon Phanom; when a Siamese commander demanded surrender after warning of an attack on Xiangkhouang, the Muang Phuan leadership complied, and Anou was taken to Bangkok, where he died in detention. The Siamese side (Rama III) claimed that Anou had refused to submit and had resumed hostilities. Most primary Lao sources from this period have been lost, so later accounts in Siamese and Vietnamese records diverge.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Scale of Deaths and Forced DisplacementUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: events after 1816 are not recorded in systematic datasets such as COW or UCDP (pipeline-collected data/LAO.json). The original COW Intra-State/Extra-State master list has not been checked.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Laos (1994) — Siam · Government report · Orientation: compiled by the US government; relies on Siamese and Vietnamese sources for primary records — http://countrystudies.us/laos/8.htm

Southern uprising of the 'holy man' Phu Mi Bun and resistance in the Bolaven Plateau (Ông Kẹo, Ông Khammada) Uprising · 1901–1930s

An uprising took place on both banks of the Mekong (southern French Laos and northeastern Siam), led by a revered holy man (Phu Mi Bun). In 1900, leaders including Ong Man, Ong Kẹo, and Pho Kaduat met in Komarat and agreed on a resistance plan for the Savannakhet area; in early May 1901 an attack on Songkhone marked the beginning of the Savannakhet uprising. On March 30, 1902, Ong Man's followers attacked and burned Komarat, and the number of rebels swelled to more than 2,500. On April 4, 1902, in Sapho village northwest of Ubon, Siamese artillery fire killed more than 200 uprising fighters; later that month, an attack on Savannakhet was repulsed by colonial security forces (Garde Indigène). Ong Kẹo of the Bolaven Plateau surrendered in October 1907 but was executed in November 1910, and Ong Khammada continued to go into hiding until 1924, when he circulated a declaration written in his own script and proclaimed himself 'King of Khome' in 1926. The researcher (Baird) describes the uprising as arising from discontent over the replacement of corvée with cash taxes and the possible involvement of the Champasak royal house. Characterizations of the uprising (minority resistance vs. regional elite involvement) coexist between French colonial authorities' records and the researcher's interpretation.

Bachay (Pachaï Boue) Mon uprising Uprising · 1918–1921

A rebellion led by the Messianic Mon leader Bachay (Pa Chay Vue), described as a four-year revolt against Tai local power and French colonial authority spanning Tonkin (northern Vietnam) and Laos. Michaud (2021) suggests that a photograph taken in Tonkin in 1918 may show Bachay, who had met with colonial authorities just before the uprising, and notes that French military records are incomplete and concealment by administrative authorities is possible. Bachay was killed during the suppression of the uprising in Laos in 1921–22.

Data record: not recorded in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO (pipeline collection).

  1. Michaud, J. (2021). 'Is This Pa Chay Vue? A Study in Three Frames'. Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society 31(2), 363–391 · Journal article · Orientation: academic paper (critical review of colonial military records) — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-the-royal-asiatic-society/article/is-this-pa-chay-vue-a-study-in-three-frames/6249A1D5507E3823D1F5C0E7AA88A1F5

Lao Issara and the 1945 Declaration of Independence, French Reoccupation Uprising · 1945–1946

After the Japanese military detained French administrators in early April 1945, King Sisavang Vong proclaimed the end of French protectorate status on April 8. Following Japan's surrender, Prince Phetsarath cabled local governors on August 28 that independence would not be affected and announced on September 15 the unification of Luang Prabang Kingdom with four southern provinces. The king stated on September 7 that the protectorate would continue and dismissed Phetsarath from his posts as prime minister and prince father on October 10. The provisional revolutionary government in Vientiane (Lao Issara) adopted a provisional constitution on October 12, and after crowds entered the Luang Prabang royal palace on November 10, the king declared himself a commoner. In 1946, French and Lao guerrillas reoccupied Xieng Khouang in January and Savannakhet in March; at the Battle of Takhèk on March 21, hundreds of defenders and civilians died within a day. On April 23, the king accepted the constitution and declared void the acts he had signed under duress since April 4, 1945; the Lao Issara leadership established a government-in-exile in Bangkok.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other

Pathet Lao and the Laotian Civil War Civil war · 1950–1975

A congress held in August 1950 in Vietnam formed the Neo Lao Issara and a government with Souphanouvong as leader, and on April 19, 1953, Souphanouvong officially established the Pathet Lao government in Houaphanh Province. In spring 1953, Viet Minh forces controlled almost all of Houaphanh Province and parts of Phongsali, Xieng Khouang, and Luang Prabang. The April 24, 1960 election is described by a Library of Congress Country Study as a fraudulent election, and on May 23, imprisoned members of the Patriotic Front (LPF) escaped. Following the Kong Le coup in August and the Battle of Vientiane in December of the same year, from early 1961 onward the Phoumi-Vong government (Vientiane), the Souphanouvong government (Kangkae), and the Pathet Lao supported by North Vietnamese forces coexisted. U.S. aircraft began attacking ground targets in Laos from 1964.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
Combat fatalities10,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, 'First Laotian War of 1960-1968', war_num 749)Unconfirmed

The 1960 Kong Le coup and the April 1964 coup Coup · 1960–1964

On the morning of August 9, 1960, Captain Kong Le, commander of the 2nd Parachute Battalion of the Royal Lao Army, staged a virtually bloodless coup in Vientiane. At the time, the entire cabinet was in Luang Prabang discussing King Sisavang Vong's funeral arrangements with King Vatthana. The insurgents declared support for the constitution and the monarchy, as well as for the safety of foreigners, and demanded the withdrawal of a U.S. military mission (24 men) (U.S. Department of State FRUS editor's note). On August 15, Prince Boun Oum Norsaovan proclaimed a counter-coup committee, and on August 31 a government headed by Prime Minister Souphanouvong and Deputy Prime Minister Boun Oum took office; however, on September 10, Prince Boun Oum announced the abolition of the constitution in the name of the Revolutionary Council. On December 11, 40 lawmakers assembled in Savannakhet voted to censure, and on December 12 King's Decree No. 282 dismissed the Souphanouvong government; from December 13 to 16, Boun Oum's troops attacked and entered Vientiane. After the three-way summit talks broke down on April 18, 1964, and Boun Oum announced his resignation, on the morning of April 19, General Kouprasith, commander of the Fifth Military Region, and General Sithou, commander of the Vientiane security forces, seized power in the name of the National Revolutionary Committee and arrested neutral figures including Boun Oum. The United States issued a statement supporting a coalition government and demanding the immediate release of the neutrals.

Data record: Powell & Thyne (V2026.08.29): 8 coups in Laos — successful on 1960-08-09, 1960-12-08, and 1964-04-19; failed on 1965-01-31, 1965-03-28, 1965-04-16, 1966-10-21, and 1973-08-20. Archigos 4.1: Sannanikone ousted by the military on 1959-12-31; Souphanouvong ousted by the military on 1960-08-09; Boun Oum ousted by the military on 1960-12-10 and 1964-04-19. The date of the successful coup on 1960-12-08 in P&T differs from the dates of the parliamentary vote of no confidence (12.11), the royal decree dismissing the government (12.12), and the attack on Vientiane (12.13).

  1. FRUS 1958–1960, Vol. XVI, Doc. 355 Editorial Note (Kong Le coup) · Government report · Tone: U.S. government diplomatic record — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1958-60v16/d355
  2. Library of Congress Country Study: Laos — A Deepening Split · Government report · Tone: compiled by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/laos/27.htm
  3. FRUS 1964–1968, Vol. XXVIII, Doc. 22 Situation Report No. 1 (1964.04.19) · Government report · Tone: U.S. government diplomatic record (the party opposing the coup) — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1964-68v28/d22

Abolition of the Monarchy and the Establishment of the Lao People's Democratic Republic in 1975 Regime Overthrow · 1975

On March 27, 1975, North Vietnamese and Pathet Lao forces attacked Hmong defenders at Pha Bao. In May, protests over prices and other issues grew in Vientiane; on May 10, Vang Pao and 12 Hmong leaders signed a document stating that they were leaving Laos and would not return, after which Vang Pao departed on May 14. Protesters occupied the compound of U.S. aid agencies, bringing aid projects to an end, and four right-wing cabinet ministers, including Defense Minister Nga Jampasack, left the country. On August 23, the Revolutionary Council took over administration of Vientiane; in November, local elections were held in former royal government areas, with voters choosing from lists distributed in advance. On November 28, protesters demanded the dissolution of the provisional coalition government and the National Political Consultative Council; on November 29, Boun Oum and Souphanouvong persuaded the king to abdicate in Luang Prabang. A national congress attended by 264 people was held on December 1, and on December 2 the congress unanimously decided to establish the Lao People's Democratic Republic and elected Souphanouvong as president. Non-communist parties disappeared, and a one-party system took shape.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Population fleeing abroad in the years after 1975UnconfirmedApproximately 350,000 (Library of Congress Country Study, 1994); more than 300,000, mostly Hmong (Radio Free Asia 2008.02.08)

Data record: Archigos 4.1: Boun Oum Norsaovan ousted on 1975-12-02 by 'rebels supported by foreign powers'; Kaysone Phomvihane entered irregularly on 1975-12-02. NAVCO 1.3: 'Second Pathet Lao' successful, 1959–1975. Not included in Powell & Thyne coup data.

Hmong armed resistance and jungle hideouts since 1975 (Chao Pha, etc.) Civil War · 1976–2000s

Some Hmong who fought on the pro-American government side during the Vietnam War and received advice from the US Central Intelligence Agency (CIA) left the country after 1975; thousands remained in Laos. Some of those remaining continued armed resistance or hid in the jungle. In a report dated March 23, 2007, Amnesty International described 'thousands' of Hmong scattered in the Laotian jungle hiding from the military and demanded an end to armed attacks against them and access for UN agencies. Radio Free Asia (RFA), a US government-funded broadcaster, reported in 2004 that thousands had surrendered after being promised land and building materials but were subsequently unaccounted for, and in February 2008 it cited anonymous military sources who said that orders had been issued to shoot Hmong insurgents on sight, offering 600,000 kip (about $600), party membership, and a one-grade promotion. The Laotian Ministry of Foreign Affairs spokesperson Yong Chanthalaengsi denied this as 'a complete fabrication' and stated that the military was protecting roads and public security.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Scale of Hmong in Jungle HideoutsUnconfirmed (government denies both the existence of rebels and punitive operations)'Thousands' (Amnesty International ASA 26/003/2007)
Bounties for Killing (Reports)Denied (Laos Ministry of Foreign Affairs, cited by RFA 2008.02.08)600,000 kip ($600), party membership, one-grade promotion (anonymous military source, RFA 2008.02.08)

Student democratization protests of October 26, 1999 Protest · 1999

On October 26, 1999, in Vientiane, students and teachers were arrested by the police while peacefully posting posters demanding economic, political, and social change. According to Amnesty International, Thongsavanh Khaokhoun, Souphanouvong Phengphandith, and Bouavanh Chanmanivong were arrested along with 'several other students and teachers', and a student arrested with them, Kamphouvieng Sithasath, died in prison in September 2001. Amnesty learned in 2003 that the individuals had received sentences of up to ten years, but the Laotian authorities stated in 2009 that it was twenty years. Amnesty described the information provided by the authorities as 'inadequate and contradictory'. Another Amnesty document states that the authorities have consistently denied both the occurrence of the protests and the arrests (original text of the document unverified).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Sentences for Leaders20 years (Laotian authorities, 2009; cited by Amnesty International 2012)Up to 10 years (information obtained by Amnesty International in 2003)
Number of ArrestsUnconfirmed3 named individuals + 'several other students and teachers' (Amnesty International 2012)

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Kelantan Uprising (To' Janggut's Rebellion) Uprising · 1915

In 1915, an uprising against British rule broke out in rural areas of the British protectorate of Kelantan, led by 'To' Janggut' (the 'old long-bearded man'). Cheah Boon Kheng (2006) notes that oral traditions, newspaper reports, and academic studies differ on whether the event was a tax rebellion or an anti-colonial uprising, and on whether To' Janggut opposed the sultan or was used by him. This series records only that these interpretations coexist without adjudicating the nature of the event.

Data Record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or UCDP (checked in data/MYS.json for this pipeline).

  1. Cheah Boon Kheng (2006). To' Janggut: Legends, Histories, and Perceptions of the 1915 Rebellion in Kelantan. NUS Press · Monograph · Orientation: Publisher's blurb (academic monograph) — https://nuspress.nus.edu.sg/collections/malaysia/products/to-janggut

Malayan Emergency (Malayan Communist Party Armed Struggle) Uprising · 1948–1960

In June 1948, the British colonial authorities declared a state of emergency in response to activities by armed elements of the Malayan Communist Party (CPM), and the emergency was lifted in 1960. According to the National Army Museum (NAM) in the UK, more than 400,000 people had been resettled into 500 'New Villages' by late 1951, and in Batang Kali in December 1948, around 20 unarmed civilians were killed. The UCDP records the period 1948–1957 as a territorial conflict between the British government and the CPM (an external colonial-type conflict) and the period from 1958 onward as a conflict between the Malaysian government and the CPM.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Security Forces DeathsOver 500 military personnel, over 1,300 police (National Army Museum NAM)Over 500 military personnel, over 1,300 police (The Star 2024.08.31; source institution unindicated)
Communist Armed ForcesOver 6,000 dead, 1,200 captured (NAM estimate)Unconfirmed
Civilian DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict 229 'Government of United Kingdom vs CPM' 1948–1957 (extrasystemic, 4 war years with over 1,000 deaths); conflict 261 'Government of Malaysia vs CPM' 1958–1981. NAVCO 1.3: 'Malayan Emergency' 1948–1960 violence · partial success. Not included in the COW collection (MYS).

  1. National Army Museum (UK) — Malayan Emergency · Public institution · Orientation: UK military museum (state agency on the suppression side) — https://www.nam.ac.uk/explore/malayan-emergency
  2. The Star (2024.08.31) Rightful honour for all who sacrificed · Media · Orientation: Major Malaysian daily (history of ownership by ruling coalition) — https://thestar.com.my/news/nation/2024/08/31/rightful-honour-for-all-who-sacrificed
  3. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/MYS.json) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/

Indonesia–Malaysia Confrontation (Konfrontasi) and North Borneo Communist Organizations Civil War · 1963–1966

Territorial disputes between Indonesia and Malaysia continued before and after the formation of the Federation of Malaysia (1963), and during the same period there were armed clashes between communist organizations (CCO) and the Malaysian government in Sarawak. This entry records only dataset records; no narrative literature verified from the original text in this round.

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict 280 'Government of Malaysia vs CCO' 1963–1966 (internationalized civil war, territory); conflict 281 'Indonesia vs Malaysia' 1963–1966 (inter-state, territory). No war year with over 1,000 deaths.

  1. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/MYS.json) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/

May 13 Incident (1969 Kuala Lumpur Racial Riots) Protest · 1969

Racial riots broke out in Kuala Lumpur on May 13, immediately after the general election of 1969. On May 15, a state of emergency was declared, parliament was suspended, elections in Sabah and Sarawak were halted, and Deputy Prime Minister Abdul Razak was appointed operational head of the National Operations Council (NOC). The NOC published its White Paper in October 1969; this was the only official document of the National Archives of Malaysia until 2001. The New Economic Policy (NEP) was introduced in 1971 (Asia Times 2019). The gap between the official death toll and estimates by foreign diplomatic sources is large, and interpretations surrounding declassified British documents remain contested.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths196 (National Operations Council NOC report, cited in Asia Times 2019)Up to approximately 800 (estimates by foreign and diplomatic sources, Asia Times 2019)

Data Record: Not present in UCDP, COW, or NAVCO collections (MYS). Mass Mobilization has recorded entries from the 1990s onward (the 1969 event is not included).

  1. Asia Times (2019.05.29, Nile Bowie) Fifty years on, fateful race riots still haunt Malaysia · Media · Lean: Hong Kong-based online outlet — https://asiatimes.com/2019/05/fifty-years-on-fateful-race-riots-still-haunt-malaysia/

Second Armed Struggle of the Communist Party of Malaysia Civil War · 1968–1989

Armed clashes between the Malaysian government and the Malayan Communist Party (CPM) after 1958 are recorded in the UCDP database up to 1981, and the Malaysian account refers to 1968–1989 as the period of the Second Armed Struggle. The Star (2024) reported that an additional 155 security personnel died during this period.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Security Personnel DeathsUnconfirmed155 (The Star 2024.08.31, source agency unindicated)

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1 conflict 261 'Government of Malaysia vs CPM' 1958–1981, active years recorded: 6; no war years with over 1,000 fatalities — the Malaysian account's period designation (1968–1989) and termination year differ.

  1. The Star (2024.08.31) Rightful honour for all who sacrificed · Media · Lean: Major Malaysian daily — https://thestar.com.my/news/nation/2024/08/31/rightful-honour-for-all-who-sacrificed
  2. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/MYS.json) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/

Lahad Datu Armed Conflict (Sulu Succession Claimants) Civil War · 2013

In Lahad Datu, Sabah, in 2013, armed conflict occurred between forces claiming succession to the Sultanate of Sulu and the Malaysian government over territory. Because no narrative literature was verified in the original text for this round, only the dataset record is noted.

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict 11487 'Government of Malaysia vs Sultanate of Sulu' 2013 (civil war, territory). Conflict zone records are location records and not political judgments (Volume 1, Part 3).

  1. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/MYS.json) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Mon and Kachin uprisings in Pegu (Bago) and the restoration of the Hanthawaddy Kingdom, fall of Ava Late Toungoo Dynasty · 1740–1752

In the first half of the 18th century, the population of Hpaung (Hpu Burma) was predominantly Mon, but the uprising that brought down the Toungoo Dynasty began with a Burman governor appointed by the king. The governor was killed by his own troops, and local Mon and Kachin (some scholars identify them as Karen) elevated a monk from Kachin, Shyem Maw Buddhakitti, to kingship (GlobalSecurity compilation; US Library of Congress Country Studies series). Another paragraph in the same source states that in 1740 the Mon 'rose again' and elected a Shan monk king of Pegu. The new king's army seized the ports of Syriam and Martaban and reached Prome and Toungoo in the north by 1743. In 1747, the Shan leader Binnya Dala deposed Shyem Maw Buddhakitti, advanced on Ava in 1751, captured the capital, and deposed the last king of the Toungoo Dynasty. Ma Yi Yi (1965) describes the annual raids of the Mon as ending with the '1751 capture of Ava', which brought the Toungoo Dynasty to an end. The GlobalSecurity list of kings gives the fall of Ava as March 11, 1752, so the year of the fall is given as 1751 in some sources and 1752 in others. In this civil war, Burmans, Mons, Shans, and Kachins fought on both sides, according to the account.

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Myanmar: Yadanabon era / Konbaung Dynasty (1752-1878) · Public DB · Tone: compiled by a US private security research site (including verbatim passages from US Library of Congress Country Studies and colonial gazetteers) — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/myanmar/history-konbaung.htm
  2. GlobalSecurity.org — Myanmar: Nyaung Yan Dynasty (A.D 1599 - 1752) · Public DB · Tone: compiled by a US private security research site — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/myanmar/history-nyaung-yan.htm
  3. Ma Yi Yi (1965). Burmese Sources for the History of the Konbaung Period 1752–1885. Journal of Southeast Asian History 6(1), 48–66 · Journal article · Tone: academic journal article (only abstract reviewed) — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-southeast-asian-history/article/burmese-sources-for-the-history-of-the-konbaung-period-17521885/7D086F4B750FBBD52BA7F870E5E22E98

Alaungpaya's rebellion at Shwebo and the restoration of Hanthawaddy Founding period of the Konbaung Dynasty · 1752–1757

After the fall of Ava, Sagaing fell into disorder. Alaungpaya, a former village headman, led a local uprising against Mon forces; when the uprising succeeded, many joined his band (GlobalSecurity account). Ma Yi Yi (1965) notes that Alaungpaya gathered a following in Shwebo, also known as Konbaung. According to the same GlobalSecurity account, he seized Ava in 1754, moved south into the delta and occupied Bassein, and in 1755 assumed the title of king of Burma and Pegu and adopted the name 'Alaungpaya'. In 1757 Binnya Dala was defeated by Alaungpaya and taken prisoner; he was publicly executed in 1775 during the reign of King Singu Min. The same source states that Alaungpaya referred to the Mon as 'Thalay gyi' (crushed), a description of how the conquerors designated them, drawn from colonial-era accounts. Accounts differ on the year of the recapture of Ava (1753 and 1754).

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Myanmar: Yadanabon era / Konbaung Dynasty (1752-1878) · Public DB · Tone: compiled by a US private security research site; the relevant passage is written in the style of British colonial-era accounts — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/myanmar/history-konbaung.htm
  2. Ma Yi Yi (1965). Journal of Southeast Asian History 6(1), 48–66 · Journal article · Tone: academic journal article (only abstract reviewed) — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-southeast-asian-history/article/burmese-sources-for-the-history-of-the-konbaung-period-17521885/7D086F4B750FBBD52BA7F870E5E22E98

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Chin Byan's uprising in Arakan (Rakhine) 1811–1815

King Bodawpaya conquered Arakan in 1784 and annexed it to the Ava Kingdom (Human Rights Watch 2000). Following the conquest, policies such as conscripting thousands into forced labor on Sagaing led many refugees to cross the Naf River into British territory (Bengal), and anti-government insurgents used the border area in Bengal as a base for raids against Burmese garrisons (GlobalSecurity; US Library of Congress Country Studies series). In 1811, Chin Byan, leader of the Arakanese insurgents, assembled a large force among refugees in British territory and crossed the border to seize Mrauk-U (Mrauk U), the former capital. Human Rights Watch states that he 'controlled most of Arakan.' He was driven out of Mrauk-U and retreated across the Naf River, but continued his raids until his death in 1815. The Burmese side protested that Britain had failed to stop him, and the insurgents' use of the border and the Burmese army's 'pursuit crossings' are described as factors of tension with British India. Designations of the Arakanese ethnic group (Rakhine, Arakanese, Rohingya) differ across sources.

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Konbaung Kingdom: Expansion and Confrontation, 1784-1826 · Public DB · Tone: compiled by a US private security research site (US Library of Congress Country Studies series) — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/myanmar/history-konbaung-3.htm
  2. Human Rights Watch (2000). Burmese Refugees in Bangladesh — Historical Background · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (pro-Rohingya refugee protection perspective) — https://www.hrw.org/reports/2000/burma/burm005-01.htm

Sponsored by the U.S. government-supported Political Instability Task Force (PITF) (stated in user guide).

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Say San (Galon) Rebellion Uprising · 1930–1932

Amid the Great Depression and falling rice prices, farmers burdened by heavy taxes and land loss to Indian moneylenders rose up under the leadership of Say San, a traditional healer, in the Bago region's Thayawaddy District in 1930, and the uprising spread to several southern regions (The Irrawaddy 2019). Herbert (1982) notes that the outbreak in December 1930 was a complete surprise to British authorities, describes large areas as being in 'anarchy' in March–April 1931, and recounts that the authorities requested martial law from the Indian government in July of the same year but were refused. The colonial government brought two infantry divisions armed with machine guns from India, and it took nearly two years to suppress the uprising (The Irrawaddy). Say San was sentenced to death by a special tribunal on August 28, 1931, and hanged in Thayawaddy Prison on November 28. Regarding the government's report The Origin and Causes of the Burma Rebellion (1930–32) (1934), Herbert evaluates it as the most complete statement of the government's position but notes that it relies on selective excerpts from trial records.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Rebel deathsUnconfirmed (original 1934 government report not consulted)Over 1,300 (many wounded; Herbert 1982); approximately 3,000 dead and wounded (The Irrawaddy 2019)
Surrendered · CapturedUnconfirmed9,000 (Herbert 1982); over 8,000 arrested (The Irrawaddy 2019)
ImprisonmentUnconfirmed126 including Saya San (Herbert 1982: 'Saya San and 125 others'); 78 (The Irrawaddy 2019)
Imprisonment and hard laborUnconfirmed1,389 imprisonment and hard labor (Herbert 1982); 270 life sentences (The Irrawaddy 2019)
Government-Side CasualtiesUnconfirmed58 dead, 96 wounded; costs of 2.5 million kyats; 10,000–11,000 troops (The Irrawaddy 2019)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3: "Saya San's rebellion" 1930–1932, target British occupation, goal expulsion of foreign power and self-determination, violence, failure (pipeline collection data/MMR.json). COW Intra-State·Extra-State collection contains no corresponding entry.

  1. Herbert, Patricia (1982). The Hsaya San Rebellion (1930–1932) Reappraised. Monash University Centre of Southeast Asian Studies Working Papers No. 27 · Research report · Orientation: academic reassessment (critical review of colonial government reports) — https://burmastudiesgroup.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/06/herbert_the-hsaya-san-rebellion-1930-1032-reappraised.pdf
  2. The Irrawaddy (2019.08.28). On This Day: The Day the British Sentenced Peasant Rebel Leader Saya San to Death · Media · Orientation: independent outlet formerly based in Burmese exile media (anti-military stance) — https://www.irrawaddy.com/specials/on-this-day/day-british-sentenced-peasant-rebel-leader-saya-san-death.html

Communist Party, Karen, and People's Volunteer Army armed uprisings immediately after independence (beginning of civil war) Civil War · 1948–1951

The Union of Burma gained independence on January 4, 1948. The Red Flag Communist Party faction led by Thakin Soe had already been operating underground in the Arakan Yoma mountains, and in northern Arakan a Mujahid rebel group that sought to establish an Islamic state was active. The White Flag Communist Party led by Thakin Than Tun criticized the British-Burmese agreement as 'fake independence'; after peace talks in March 1948 failed, it moved to Prome and rose up, reaching a peak of 25,000 troops. In July 1948, a People's Volunteer Army (PVO) White Band faction and parts of the 1st and 3rd Battalions of the Burma Rifles joined the rebellion. The Karen National Union (KNU) had demanded a federal state with autonomy for the Karen region in 1947, and the armed organization Karen National Defense Organization (KNDO) rose up in January 1949, seizing Insein, Bassein, Pyapon, and Taungoo. In February–April 1949, rebels controlled most of the countryside and parts of Rangoon fell temporarily into rebel hands. Government forces recaptured Mandalay on April 24 of the same year, and by 1950 they had pushed the KNDO east of the Salween River and into part of Tenasserim Division. In 1951, a minister acknowledged that less than half of the territory was under effective government control.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Maximum strength of the White Flag Communist uprisingUnconfirmed25,000 (GlobalSecurity, US Congressional Research Service narrative lineage)
Combat fatalitiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no COW entry for the 1948–1951 interval)

Data record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: Conflict 222 (government vs CPB, PVO White Band, etc., targeting the government) 1948–2025, active 56 years · over 1,000 war deaths in 26 years; conflict 221 (government vs KNU, etc., territorial) 1949–2025, active 66 years · war years 8; conflict 223 (Arakan Mujahid, etc.) 1948–2025. The first civil war entry in the COW Intra-State v5.1 collection for Myanmar is 'Second Burmese War of 1958-1960' (1,500 dead), and there is no entry for 1948–51. Instead, 'Costa Rica War of 1948' (1,600 dead) is linked to cow_state 'Myanmar' (possible linking error; anomaly recorded).

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Myanmar: Parliamentary Government, 1948-62 · Public DB · Orientation: compiled by a US private security research site (US Congressional Research Service narrative lineage) — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/myanmar/government-1948-62.htm
  2. GlobalSecurity.org — 1962 Military Coup in Burma (describes independence date as 1948.1.4) · Public DB · Orientation: compiled by a US private security research site — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/war/myanmar1.htm

Military transitional government of 1958 and Ne Win coup of 1962 Coup · 1958–1962

In 1958 Prime Minister U Nu brought in the military temporarily to restore political order, and civilian rule was restored 18 months later. The sources describe this transitional government as having been established at the invitation of parliament and with the approval of U Nu. The 1961 declaration of Buddhism as the state religion provoked opposition from ethnic minorities, and on the morning of March 2, 1962, just as a national federal seminar on federalism was underway, police and the military seized control of Rangoon; at 8:50 AM General Ne Win announced that the military had taken power. The coup is described as bloodless. Ne Win established the Revolutionary Council of the Union, dissolved parliament, and ruled by decree; subsequently, the Burma Socialist Programme Party (BSPP) became the sole legal party. Among the reasons Ne Win gave for the coup was 'preventing the disintegration of Burma.'

Data records: Powell & Thyne (V2026.08.29): coded as success on 1958-09-26 and 1962-03-02, failure on 1976-07-02. The 1958 transfer is described in the sources as having taken place at the invitation of parliament and with the approval of the prime minister, but P&T classifies it as a coup (difference in definitions; anomaly recorded). Archigos 4.1: U Nu ousted on 1962-03-02 by the military without foreign support; imprisoned; Ne Win entered irregularly on 1962-03-02.

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — 1962 Military Coup in Burma · Public DB · Orientation: compiled by a US private security research site (including reproductions of documents reflecting the views of the US government at the time) — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/war/myanmar1.htm
  2. Powell & Thyne coup data (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://www.uky.edu/~clthyn2/coup_data/home.htm

The 8888 Democratic Struggle and the Military Takeover of September 18 Protests · 1988

On July 23, 1988, Ne Win stepped down from the presidency and the chairmanship of the BSPP, to be succeeded by Sein Lwin. Martial law was declared again in Rangoon on August 3, and a general strike and massive rallies began on August 8, as had been rumored beforehand. The crackdown continued until August 11; Sein Lwin resigned on August 12, and the military returned to its barracks on August 13 (Egreteau 2009). On September 18, the military seized power again in a coup. There is a large discrepancy between official statements and independent estimates of the death toll. The regime's first statement reported four wounded and 822 detained; on August 10, state media reported 41 deaths from 'the incident'; and General Soe Myint mentioned 'six' casualties for the first night in an interview in 1989. Most observers estimated that at least 100 to 200 people died alone in Rangoon on the first night.

ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths4 wounded, 822 detained (regime's first statement); 41 dead (state media 8.10); 6 on the first night (Soe Myint 1989 interview) — cited by Egreteau 2009Approximately 3,000 nationwide (Amnesty International 1990, US Congress 1990, Steinberg 2001, cited by Egreteau as 'the most credible estimate'); 4,000–5,000 (Aung Zaw); 3,000–10,000 (exile groups, NGOs, foreign diplomats)

Data record: Powell & Thyne: successful coup on 1988-09-18. Archigos 4.1: Ne Win regular exit on 1988-07-25; Sein Lwin 1988-07-25 to 08-12; Maung Maung ousted by the military on 1988-09-18; Soe Myint entered irregularly on 1988-09-18. NAVCO 1.3: 'Pro-Democracy movement in Burma' 1988–2019, method 'violence', failure. The Mass Mobilization v5.1 collection begins in 1990 and does not include the 1988 protests. Archigos's date for Ne Win's exit (7.25) differs from Egreteau's (7.23).

  2. Powell & Thyne coup data (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://www.uky.edu/~clthyn2/coup_data/home.htm

Saffron Revolution (August–September 2007 protests) Protests · 2007

On August 15, 2007, the Burmese government raised retail fuel prices by up to 500%, and monks joined protests against this. The report of the Special Rapporteur of the UN Human Rights Council, Paulo Sérgio Pinheiro (A/HRC/6/14, 2007.12.7), focused on the crackdown of September 26–29. The government acknowledged 15 deaths during the protests; the Special Rapporteur received information on 16 additional deaths; and a list of 653 detainees, 74 missing persons, and 16 deaths was presented separately from the government's list of 15 deaths. In a December press conference, Police Chief Kin Ye stated that '10 people died and 14 were injured during the monk protests of September 26–30' (cited by Human Rights Watch). Human Rights Watch recorded 20 killings in Rangoon and believed the actual death toll to be higher.

ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

Detention and DisappearancesUnconfirmedDetained: 653; enforced disappearances: at least 74 cases (A/HRC/6/14, paras. 53 and 69)

  2. Human Rights Watch (2007.12.07). Burma: Crackdown Bloodier Than Government Admits · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (based on interviews with witnesses) — https://www.hrw.org/news/2007/12/07/burma-crackdown-bloodier-government-admits

Rakhine State 'clearance operations' and large-scale Rohingya exodus Civil War · 2017

On August 25, 2017, the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army (ARSA) attacked police outposts and military bases in Rakhine State; the military announced a 'clearance operation' against 'extremist terrorists' (Reuters, reprinted by The Irrawaddy 2017.9.1). In the same report, the military stated that approximately 370 Rohingya insurgents, 13 security personnel, 2 civil servants, and 14 civilians had been killed. Rohingya refugees described the operation as an effort to drive them out through arson and murder. The UN Independent International Fact-Finding Mission on Myanmar (A/HRC/39/64, 2018) characterized the violence between ARSA in Rakhine State and security forces as a non-international armed conflict at least from August 25, 2017 onward, described the 'clearance operations' as a human rights catastrophe, and recorded that approximately 725,000 Rohingya had fled to Bangladesh by mid-August 2018, with at least 392 villages (40% of northern Rakhine settlements) partially or completely destroyed.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
FatalitiesApproximately 400 (about 370 insurgents, 13 security personnel, 2 civil servants, 14 civilians; Myanmar military announcement, 2017.8.31, reported by Reuters)At least 6,700 (August 25–September 24, 2017, including at least 730 children under five; Médecins Sans Frontières survey, 2017.12.12); estimates of up to 10,000 are also conservative (UN Fact-Finding Mission A/HRC/39/64)
Refugees in BangladeshUnconfirmedApproximately 725,000 (as of mid-August 2018, A/HRC/39/64); 38,000 as of 2017.8.31 (UN sources, Reuters)

Because many of the independent estimates of fatalities are non-combatant civilians, they differ in definition from datasets based on combat deaths (outlier recorded).

February 1, 2021 coup and the 'Spring Revolution' Coup · 2021–2026

On February 1, 2021, the military ousted civilian leader Aung San Suu Kyi and seized power, citing alleged fraud in the November 2020 general election (National League for Democracy landslide) as justification (The Jakarta Post 2021.4.9, citing AFP and Reuters). Protests continued daily across the country thereafter, and UN human rights officials stated that the military had increased its use of heavy weapons, including assault rifles, tear gas grenades, machine guns, and snipers. On April 9, junta spokesperson Zaw Min Tun stated at a televised press conference that participation in protests and strikes was declining. On April 19, state media reported that 258 civilians had died in anti-military protests since February 1, and that other tallies were exaggerated; it designated the Assistance Association for Political Prisoners (AAPP) as an 'illegal organization based in Thailand' (Reuters). On the same day, AAPP's tally stood at 737. As of August 14, 2026, AAPP recorded that 8,272 people had died and 31,759 had been arrested since the coup at the hands of the junta and pro-military groups; it stated that the actual figures were likely higher.

Cumulative Deaths Since the CoupUnconfirmed8,272 (AAPP 2026.08.14 daily briefing, confirmed identities)

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Uprising of the Trưng Sisters (Trưng Trắc and Trưng Nhị) Ancient (Period of Chinese Dynastic Rule) · 40–43

In 40 AD, Trưng Trắc, a noblewoman of Lạc descent, and her younger sister Trưng Nhị rebelled against new taxes imposed by the Eastern Han authorities, leading the nobility in revolt. More than 65 villages and districts in the Red River Delta supported them. The Eastern Han History (Hou Hanshu) states that Trưng Trắc's husband was alive at the time of the uprising, but later court chroniclers described it as a revenge for his death, showing conflicting accounts in the records. The Eastern Han dispatched more than 10,000 troops and generals; suppression took more than two years, and the Eastern Han forces achieved victory in 43. Accounts of how the sisters died coexist: some hold that they committed suicide, while others maintain that they were beheaded after being captured. After the suppression, the Eastern Han exercised direct military rule over the region, and the Vietnamese territory remained under Chinese dynastic rule until 939. In Vietnam, the sisters are commemorated on the 6th day of the 2nd lunar month.

  1. National Geographic History, 'Warriors for independence: the Trung sisters' · Media · Orientation: Popular history magazine (secondary narrative). The article text refers to the Eastern Han general as 'Ma Huan' — https://www.nationalgeographic.com/history/history-magazine/article/warriors-for-independence-the-trung-sisters

The Lam Sơn Uprising (Le Lai's Dai Hong Province Rebellion) Middle Ages (Ming Rule) · 1418–1427

On January 2, 1418 (the Year of the Dog), Le Lai rebelled in Lam Son, Thanh Hoa and proclaimed himself 'King Binh Dinh Vuong'. This uprising was an armed rebellion against Ming rule that lasted for approximately nine years. Early in December 1427, Le Lai negotiated a truce with the Ming general Wang Tong at Dong Quan; they agreed to repair roads and bridges and provide food to allow the Ming forces to withdraw. In early 1428, Le Lai ascended the throne, founding the Later Lê dynasty, establishing the era name Thuận Thiên, setting Thang Long as the capital, and restoring the national title Dai Viet.

  1. Báo Nghệ An (Communist Party organ of Nghe An Province), 'Lam Son Historical Site and King Le Temple Festival' · Media · Orientation: Communist Party organ of a Vietnamese provincial party (official state narrative) — https://baonghean.vn/en/khu-di-tich-lam-son-va-hoi-den-vua-le-10000038.html

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The Tay Son Uprising c. 1771–1802

In 1771, three brothers—Nguyễn Nhạc, Nguyễn Luận, and Nguyễn Huệ—rose in revolt against heavy taxes in the mountainous western region of Quy Nhơn, near the village of Thiện Sanh. The first major victory, according to the sources reviewed, was Nguyễn Nhạc's capture of Quy Nhơn in 1773; this became the rebels' base (the year of the uprising's outbreak is not specified in the reviewed sources and is therefore noted as 'circa 1771'). In 1777, the Tây Sơn forces attacked Thiện Sanh District (near modern Ho Chi Minh City), killing most of the southern Nguyễn ruling class but sparing Nguyễn Phúc Án. In June 1786, Nguyễn Huệ captured Phú Xuân (Huế), and on July 21 he occupied Thăng Long, bringing down the northern Trịnh regime. At the request of the Lê royal family, the Qing Qianlong Emperor dispatched approximately 200,000 troops in November 1788; they seized Thăng Long on December 17. Nguyễn Huệ was proclaimed Emperor Quang Trung on November 22 and launched an offensive during the lunar New Year period, entering Thăng Long on January 30. Quang Trung died on September 16, 1792, and Nguyễn Phúc Án weakened the Tây Sơn before ascending the throne as Emperor Gia Long in 1802. The nature of the uprising (social uprising, regional uprising, ethnic minority elements) is debated among scholars; Tana Li characterizes it as 'a regional rather than a social uprising,' while Dutton sees economic hardship rather than political discontent as the cause. Estimates of Qing troop numbers in Vietnamese popular sources vary widely, from 290,000 to 20,000.

  1. EBSCO Research Starters, 'Nguyen Hue' (R. C. Lutz, 2024) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Academic database overview (secondary narrative) — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/nguyen-hue
  2. Dutton, George (2006). The Tây Sơn Uprising. University of Hawai'i Press (UBC Press cataloging) · Monograph — https://www.ubcpress.ca/the-tay-son-uprising

4. Modern Data (1816–)

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Yên Bái Uprising (Vietnamese Nationalist Party) Uprising · 1930

On the night of February 9, 1930, the Vietnamese Nationalist Party (VNQDĐ) staged an uprising against French colonial authorities in Yên Bái; during the first week (February 9–15), detachments briefly seized some barracks and government offices in Hưng Hóa, Phú Thọ, Sơn Tây, Hà Tĩnh, and Thái Bình. The French authorities suppressed the uprising within about a week. On June 17, 1930, in Yên Bái, the leader Nguyễn Thái Học and 13 party members are reported to have been beheaded (whether the leader is included in the count is unclear from the sentence in the paper). The same paper notes that 37 party members were beheaded and approximately 1,000 were imprisoned in Kon Dao (Poulo Condor) and elsewhere in Tonkin between July 1929 and January 1931.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Executions (1929.7–1931.1, Tonkin)Unconfirmed (original French colonial authorities' tally unverified)37 VNQDĐ members beheaded, approx. 1,000 imprisoned (Nguyen V.K., 2021, Russian Journal of Vietnamese Studies)

Data Record: Not recorded in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or Powell & Thyne (occurs after 1816 but is a short armed uprising during the colonial period and outside the scope of each dataset).

  1. Nguyen, Van Khanh (2021). The Vietnam Nationalist Party and the National Liberation of Vietnam. The Russian Journal of Vietnamese Studies 5(3): 90–108 · Paper · Affiliation: Researcher at Hanoi National University — https://vietnamjournal.ru/2618-9453/article/view/87096

Nguyên Thanh Soviet Movement Uprising · 1930–1931

Beginning on May 1, 1930, with worker and peasant protests in Vinh-Bình Trị, the movement continued with peasant protests led by the Indochinese Communist Party in two provinces, Nghệ An and Hà Tĩnh. On September 12, 1930, approximately 8,000 peasants from three communes (社) in Hưng Yên Province raised red flags and protested; the French colonial authorities ordered an aerial bombardment. 'Soviet' regimes were reported in September in Thanh Trì, Nam Đàn, and other districts, and the French authorities continued harsh suppression after September 1930. The number of fatalities from the Hưng Yên bombing varies even within Vietnamese state media: 217, 'over 200', and 171.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Fatalities from the Hưng Yên Bombing (1930.9.12)217 dead, 125 injured (Báo Nghệ An 2020, local party organ of Vietnam)'Over 200' (Nguyễn Ý Khiêm letter to the Comintern, September 29, 1930) / 171 (same person's letter to the International Peasant Congress, November 5, 1930) — both cited by Báo Nghệ An
Protest fatalities in Nghệ An Province (based on the November 5, 1930 letter)Unconfirmed (French authorities' tally unverified)393 dead across seven protests (Nguyễn Ý Khiêm letter, cited by Báo Nghệ An)
Political prisoners (1931.1)UnconfirmedNghệ An 900, Quang Nam 400, Hanoi 201, Thái Bình 187, Thanh Hoa 70 (cited by Báo Nghệ An in an article on 'white terror')

Data Record: Not recorded in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO.

  1. Báo Nghệ An, 'The Nghe-Tinh Soviet: 90 Years of Enduring History for the Nation' · Media · Affiliation: Local party organ of Vietnam (official communist narrative) — https://baonghean.vn/en/xo-viet-nghe-tinh-90-nam-truong-ton-cung-lich-su-dan-toc-10217554.html
  2. Báo Nghệ An, 'The Nghệ Tĩnh Soviet Uprising' (citing Nguyễn Ý Khiêm's letter) · Media · Affiliation: Local party organ of Vietnam; primary source is a letter by a movement leader — https://baonghean.vn/en/chu-tich-ho-chi-minh-voi-xo-viet-nghe-tinh-10283811.html

August Revolution Overthrow of the Regime · 1945

On August 13, 1945, the National Committee for the General Uprising issued Military Order No. 1, and an uprising order was issued after the national congress of the Việt Minh on August 14–15. On August 19, the Việt Minh took power in Hanoi; uprisings continued in Huế and Haiphong on August 23 and in Saigon on August 25. In the afternoon of August 30, the abdication ceremony of Emperor Bảo Đại was held in Huế, which the Provisional Revolutionary Government accepted, bringing the Nguyễn dynasty to an end.

Data Record: Overlaps with the start year of NAVCO 1.3 'Indochina revolt' (1945–1954, target: French occupation, violence, success). UCDP records it from 1946 as the French–Viet Minh conflict (conflict_id 216). Archigos 4.1: Ho Chi Minh 1945-09-02 irregular rule.

  1. Nhân Dân (organ of the Communist Party of Vietnam), English edition, 'A Timeline of the August Revolution' · Media · Affiliation: Party organ (state official narrative) — https://en.nhandan.vn/megastory/2025/cm08/
  2. Nhân Dân English edition, 'August 30, 1945: King Bao Dai abdicates' · Media · Affiliation: Party organ (state official narrative) — https://en.nhandan.vn/august-30-1945-king-bao-dai-abdicates-post152325.html

Cư M'nông Uprising (Resistance to Land Reform) Uprising · 1956

During the land reform process of the Democratic Republic of Vietnam (North Vietnam) (1953–1956), residents in the area around Khuin Ruu District, Yen An Province, rose up in November 1956 against land reform and land redistribution policies. A note in the U.S. Department of State Foreign Relations of the United States (FRUS) describes this as a 'popular uprising in Yen An Province in early November,' and a dispatch from the U.S. Ambassador to Vietnam (Saigon), Reinhardt, dated November 23, 1956, proposed that the South Vietnamese government send a letter to the International Control Commission requesting the resumption of free movement between the North and the South. The scale of casualties is not recorded in the primary documents reviewed; Moïse's study on land reform (1983) includes a section on 'Khuin Ruu unrest' in Chapter 12. In his errata, Moïse stated that he had raised the overall estimate of executions during land reform from approximately 5,000 to approximately 10,000 (this is not a figure limited to the Khuin Ruu incident).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Executions during Land Reform (not limited to Khuin Ruu)Unconfirmed (official figures of the Vietnamese government unconfirmed)Approximately 5,000 (original estimate by Moïse 1983) → Approximately 10,000 (revised estimate in Moïse's errata, explicitly stated as an estimate because exact figures cannot be provided)

Data Record: Not included in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization.

  1. FRUS 1955–57 Vol. I, Doc. 353: Telegram From the Ambassador in Vietnam (Reinhardt), 1956-11-23 · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. government diplomatic document (South Vietnamese ally perspective) — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1955-57v01/d353
  2. Moïse, Edwin E. (1983). Land Reform in China and North Vietnam. UNC Press — Author's page (table of contents and errata) · Monograph — https://edmoise.sites.clemson.edu/landbook.html

The Buddhist crisis of 1963 and the November coup (South Vietnam) Coup · 1963

On May 8, 1963, in Hue, protests by Buddhists broke out over a flag-hanging regulation during religious ceremonies; at least eight people died when security forces dispersed the crowd. A CIA memorandum dated June 3, 1963, notes that although the evidence pointed to government artillery fire, President Ngô Đình Diệm claimed that it was a Viet Cong grenade. The protests spread to other cities. On November 1 of the same year, a military coup brought down the Diem government; the U.S. Department of State Foreign Relations series groups the October 23–November 2 sections as 'A Coup Against the Diem Government' and covers 'The Death of Ngô Đình Nhu.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Hue Deaths (1963.5.8)South Vietnamese government: claimed to be caused by Viet Cong grenades (described in CIA memo)At least 8 (CIA 1963.6.3 memo)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 '1963 Buddhist Protests' (nonviolent, successful). Powell & Thyne: South Vietnam 1963-11-01 successful. Archigos 4.1: Diem 1963-11-01 military ouster without foreign support, outcome 'deaths'; successor 'Minh' took power irregularly on the same day. South Vietnam recorded 7 coups between 1960 and 1965 (4 successful, 3 failed) in P&T.

  1. FRUS 1961–63 Vol. III, Doc. 145: CIA Current Intelligence Memorandum, 1963-06-03 · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. intelligence agency (assessment of the government of an ally at the time) — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1961-63v03/d145
  2. FRUS 1961–63 Vol. IV, Chapter III: The Coup Against the Diem Government, Oct 23–Nov 2, 1963 · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. government diplomatic document — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1961-63v04/ch3

Tây Ninh Rural Unrest Protest · 1997

From May 1997 onward, thousands of farmers in Tây Ninh Province began open protests over corrupt local officials, tax burdens, and land disputes. On November 7 of the same year, violent protests broke out in Thuận Nghĩa District, Đồng Nai Province, where many residents are Catholic, triggered by local authorities' expropriation of church land. Human Rights Watch noted that it could not obtain accurate information on arrests, prosecutions, and trials because of press censorship.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Arrests and ProsecutionsUnconfirmed (official figures of the Vietnamese government unconfirmed)No accurate information (HRW 1997, citing press censorship)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1: 2 protests in Vietnam in 1997 (whether this event is included not verified). Not recorded in NAVCO or UCDP.

  1. Human Rights Watch/Asia (1997). Rural Unrest in Vietnam, Vol. 9 No. 11(C) · Human rights organization · Orientation: International human rights organization (critical of government) — https://www.hrw.org/legacy/reports/1997/vietnm/Vietn97d.htm

Central Highlands Montagnard (Đak Lak) Protests Protest · 2001, 2004

In 2001, thousands of Montagnard (central highlands ethnic minority) residents marched to the cities of Dak Lak, Zaraï, and Kontum demanding religious freedom and the return of ancestral lands; the government dispatched tanks and elite troops to disperse the protests quickly and arrested dozens of organizers (HRW account). Protests broke out again on April 10, 2004 (attempted entry into Buôn Ma Thuột) and on Easter Sunday, April 11 (Zaraï districts); police responded with tear gas, water cannons, and checkpoints. HRW reported a protestor's death from beating, one death in Dak Doa from police gunfire, and 40 injured at the Dak Lak City Hospital on the night of April 10, citing witness testimony; it also noted unverified reports of police injuries and deaths. The HRW documents reviewed do not cite an official position of the Vietnamese government.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
2004 DeathsUnconfirmed (official Vietnamese government announcement unconfirmed)1 death reported in Dak Doa, eyewitness testimony of a beating death in Buôn Ma Thuột (HRW 2004.4.14, explicitly unverified)
2004 InjuriesUnconfirmed40 at Dak Lak City Hospital (night of April 10, HRW 2004.4.14)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1: 1 event in 2001, 1 event in 2004 (whether this event is included has not been cross-checked). Not recorded in UCDP or NAVCO. ACLED records for Vietnam begin in 2010.

  1. Human Rights Watch (2004.4.14). Vietnam: Violence Against Montagnards During Easter Week Protests · Human rights organization · Orientation: International human rights organization (critical of government), based on eyewitness testimony — https://www.hrw.org/news/2004/04/14/vietnam-violence-against-montagnards-during-easter-week-protests

Anti-China protests and riots, May 2014 Protest · 2014

After China deployed oil drilling equipment near the Paracel (Hoang Sa) Islands on May 1, 2014, several thousand people set fire to and looted foreign-owned factories in industrial zones in Vinh Phuc and Dong Nai Province on May 13 (Tuesday), and a worker clash broke out at a Taiwanese steel mill in the Bung Ang Economic Zone of Ha Tinh Province on May 14 (Wednesday) night. The reported death tolls vary widely by reporting body. A provincial public security official in Vinh Phuc stated that approximately 600 people had been arrested on charges of looting and incitement to local media, while Ha Tinh announced the arrest of 76 people. In Vinh Phuc, 460 companies reported factory damage.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths1 (Vietnamese Foreign Ministry spokesman Le Hai Binh; Deputy Chairman of the Ha Tinh Provincial People's Committee Đặng Quốc Khánh) / 2 Chinese (Xinhua, Chinese state-run) / 1 Chinese worker (Formosa Plastics, factory owner company)Up to 21 — 5 Vietnamese, 16 described as Chinese (Ha Tinh General Hospital doctor, Reuters 2014.5.15)
Injuries149 (Ha Tinh Provincial People's Committee) / 90 (Formosa) / Over 100 hospitalized (Xinhua)Approximately 100 transported (Ha Tinh General Hospital doctor, Reuters)
ArrestsApprox. 600 in Vinh Phuc (Provincial Public Security Director, local media) / 76 in Ha Tinh (Provincial People's Committee)Unconfirmed
