Nationwide protests against the Economic Zone Law and Cybersecurity Law, June 2018 Protest · 2018

From June 9, 2018, protests opposing a draft Economic Zone Law that allows foreign investors 99-year land leases and a Cybersecurity Law took place in Ho Chi Minh City, Hanoi, Nha Trang, Vinh Phuc, and Vinh Phuc. The most violent clashes occurred in Vinh Phuc Province, where police used tear gas, smoke grenades, and water cannons. The National Assembly announced on June 11 that it had postponed the vote on the Economic Zone Law until year-end after revisions, and the Cybersecurity Law was passed on June 12 and took effect on January 1, 2019. The Deputy Minister of Public Security instructed Vinh Phuc authorities to 'severely punish the ringleaders.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Arrests and Detentions107 detained over two days in Vinh Phuc (Vietnamese police announcement, cited by HRW)Hundreds arrested reported after June 9 (HRW 2018.6.15)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1: 1 event in 2018. ACLED: 45 protests and 11 political violence events in 2018.

  1. Human Rights Watch (2018.6.15). Vietnam: Investigate Police Response to Mass Protests · Human rights organization · Orientation: International human rights organization (critical of government); figures from police announcements cited alongside — https://www.hrw.org/news/2018/06/15/vietnam-investigate-police-response-mass-protests

Suppression of the Dong Tam Land Dispute Protest · 2020

Near Hanoi, in Dong Tam village of My Duc district, on the morning of January 9, 2020, approximately 3,000 security forces entered the village following a long land dispute over military airport construction land. In the process, Le Dinh Kinh, an 84-year-old village elder, was shot dead by police gunfire, and three police officers died after falling into a concrete trench between two houses during petrol bomb attacks. Twenty-nine residents were brought to trial; on September 14, 2020, Le Dinh Cuong and Le Dinh Cong, Kinh's two sons, were sentenced to death for murder, Le Dinh Joan to life imprisonment, and 17 others to suspended sentences. Regarding the circumstances of Kinh's death, Vietnamese state media reported that he was killed while carrying a grenade and inciting resistance, conflicting with other accounts. The deputy director for Asia at Human Rights Watch commented that the sentences were 'not surprising.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths3 police officers · 1 resident (Le Dinh Kinh) — state media and court recordsIdentical (RFA 2020.9.14); conflicting accounts of Kinh's death
SentencesDeath sentences: 2; life imprisonment: 1; 12–16 years: 3; suspended sentences: 17, etc. (Hanoi People's Court; RFA report)HRW: Criticized the fairness of the trial

Data Record: ACLED: 4 deaths in Vietnam in 2020 (matches the 4 deaths in this event; event response unverified). Within the scope of Mass Mobilization v5.1 (~2020.3), but no 2020 records.

  1. Radio Free Asia (2020.9.14). Two Get Death as Dong Tam Violent Land Dispute Trial Ends in Vietnam · Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded broadcaster (tendency to criticize the Vietnamese government) — https://www.rfa.org/english/news/vietnam/trial-09142020170954.html
  2. VietNamNet (2020.9.17). Dong Tam case: justice observed, consciences awakened · Media · Orientation: Vietnamese state media (official government narrative) — https://vietnamnet.vn/en/society/dong-tam-case-justice-observed-consciences-awakened-674429.html

Attack on Eathay and Ekchut in Dak Lak Province Uprising · 2023

On the morning of June 11, 2023, two armed groups attacked the People's Committee buildings and police stations of the two communes of Eathay and Ekchut in Kuk Tin District, Dak Lak Province, killing nine people and injuring two. The People's Court of Dak Lak Province issued sentences on January 20, 2024, to 100 defendants (including six who had fled abroad), sentencing 10 to life imprisonment for 'terrorism,' five to 20 years' imprisonment for 'terrorism with the purpose of opposing the people's regime,' two to 19 years, and the remainder to 9 months to 18 years. On January 25, 2024, Foreign Ministry spokesperson Pham Thu Hang rejected at a regular briefing the claim that ethnic discrimination lay behind the incident and stated that it had been investigated and tried in accordance with Vietnamese law (response to foreign criticism).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths9 dead, 2 injured (Vietnam Law Magazine, under the Ministry of Justice)Unconfirmed (figures from independent investigations unverified)

Data Record: ACLED: 11 deaths in Vietnam in 2023 (discrepancy with official announcement of 9 deaths; whether attackers are included unverified). Not recorded in UCDP or NAVCO.

  1. Vietnam Law Magazine (under the Ministry of Justice). Ten sentenced to life imprisonment in trial of Dak Lak terrorist attack · Media · Orientation: Vietnamese state-run media outlet (official state narrative, 'terrorism' is government terminology) — https://vietnamlawmagazine.vn/ten-sentenced-to-life-imprisonment-in-trial-of-dak-lak-terrorist-attack-71163.html
  2. Tuoi Tre News, 'Dak Lak deadly terror case handled in line with Vietnamese laws: foreign ministry' · Media · Orientation: Vietnamese domestic media (state-controlled press environment) — https://tuoitrenews.vn/news/society/20240126/dak-lak-deadly-terror-case-handled-in-line-with-vietnamese-laws-foreign-ministry/78036.html

6. Related Research

  1. Dutton, George E. (2006). The Tây Sơn Uprising: Society and Rebellion in Eighteenth-Century Vietnam. Honolulu: University of Hawai'i Press. Link — The first detailed Western-language study to characterize the Thayer uprising as an uprising arising from economic hardship and to reinterpret its leaders as political opportunists (publisher's description).
  2. Moïse, Edwin E. (1983). Land Reform in China and North Vietnam: Consolidating the Revolution at the Village Level. Chapel Hill: University of North Carolina Press. Link — A study of land reform in North Vietnam and the Kuinruu unrest (Chapter 12); the author corrected the estimated execution figures in a corrigendum.
  3. Nguyen, Van Khanh (2021). The Vietnam Nationalist Party and the National Liberation of Vietnam. The Russian Journal of Vietnamese Studies, 5(3), 90–108. DOI 10.24412/2618-9453-2021-3-90-108. Link — An account of the scale of the 1930 Yen Bai uprising and its suppression by the Vietnamese Communist Party.
  4. Human Rights Watch/Asia (1997). Rural Unrest in Vietnam. Vol. 9, No. 11(C). Link — A report on the causes and human rights issues of the 1997 rural unrest in Thai Binh and Dong Nai.
  5. Kerkvliet, Benedict J. Tria (2019). Speaking Out in Vietnam: Public Political Criticism in a Communist Party-Ruled Nation. Ithaca: Cornell University Press. Link — An analysis of open criticism by workers, farmers in land disputes, anti-Chinese protesters, and democracy activists since 1990 and the Party-state's response.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War

Brunei Civil War (Pulau Cermin War) Recent Times (Sultanate) · 1660–1673

Following a dispute over a cockfight between Pengiran Muda Roffah, the son of Sultan Muhammad Ali, and Pengiran Muda Alam, the son of Pengiran Muda Abdul Hakim Mu'in, Roffah killed Alam. In retaliation, Mu'in assassinated Sultan Muhammad Ali in his palace and proclaimed himself sultan (sources differ on the regnal number, listing either the 13th or 14th reign). Mu'in seated Muhyiddin, the grandson of the late king, as Bendahara; under pressure from Muhyiddin's faction, Mu'in relocated to Pulau Cermin at the mouth of the Brunei River on the recommendation of his advisors, and in the meantime Muhyiddin proclaimed himself sultan, establishing a dual monarchy. Mu'in retreated to present-day Kota Kinabalu in Sabah, where he held out for about ten years with the help of residents of Bazaar and Dusun; he later returned to Cermin and seized control of the river mouth, blocking food and fisheries from the mainland. It is reported that Muhyiddin promised eastern North Borneo to the Sultan of Sulu in exchange for support, but the existence of this promise is not acknowledged by Brunei, making it an origin of the modern territorial dispute over Sabah. The civil war ended in 1673 when Muhyiddin's faction occupied Cermin; accounts of Mu'in's end include the claim that he threw himself into the sea with his crown.

  1. KajoMag (2020.10.21), 'The Brunei Civil War and how it led to Sulu's claim over Sabah' · Media · Orientation: Sarawak-based online popular history outlet (secondary narrative, sparse primary source citations; author not credited) — https://kajomag.com/the-brunei-civil-war-and-how-it-led-to-sulus-claim-over-sabah/

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Brunei Uprising (North Kalimantan National Army · Brunei People's Party) Uprising · 1962–1963

The armed wing of the Brunei People's Party (PRB), the North Kalimantan National Army (TNKU), rose up at 2:00 a.m. on December 8, 1962, and read an independence declaration (a condensed version prepared by A. M. Azahari) in Bukit Semuwar. Party leader A. M. Azahari was abroad in Manila after departing Singapore on December 7, and Yassin Affendi directed operations on the ground. The uprising began in Brunei Town and spread to Limbang, Lawas, and Miri in Sarawak and to Western and Sipitang in North Borneo; the rebels' main targets were the Sultan's palace, the Chief Minister's residence, and the Central Police Station. British troops flown in from Singapore and Malaya suppressed the uprising, and the Chief Minister banned the PRB. The Sultan invoked emergency powers under a 1962 constitutional provision, and U.S. Department of State reports indicate that these have been renewed every two years since and continue to the present. Under emergency powers, public assemblies of ten or more people require government permission (U.S. Department of State 2022). Harun cites Kahin's assessment that the uprising, though small in scale and duration, served as a catalyst for Indonesia and the Philippines' opposition to Malaysia (confrontation policy).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Maria Hertogh Riot Uprising · 1950

A riot broke out on December 11–12, 1950, triggered by a custody dispute over Maria Hertogh, a Dutch girl raised in a Malay Muslim household. The Singapore Police Annual (2013) records that 18 people died (including one police sergeant) and 173 were injured, and notes that this experience led to the recommendation in June 1952 to establish three special riot squads. Stockwell (1986), a scholar, examines the event from the perspective of British imperial authorities' response to Muslim assertiveness, while Aljunied (2012) examines it from the perspective of its regional and global reverberations and aftermath rather than its causes.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths · Injuries18 deaths (including 1 police sergeant) · 173 injured (Singapore Police Annual 2013)Unconfirmed

1964 Communal (Racial) Riots Uprising · 1964

On July 21, 1964, approximately 20,000 Malays gathered in Padang for a procession marking the birth of the Prophet Muhammad, and clashes began after a bottle was thrown at the crowd during the procession. The government subsequently linked the subsequent events to UMNO activists led by UMNO Secretary-General Zahir Albakri, then serving as secretary-general; the person who threw the bottle was never identified (Government SG101). About one month later, widespread communal riots broke out between Malays and Chinese, subsiding on September 14. Linking the events to UMNO activists is a narrative of the Singapore government (People's Action Party); the narrative of the Malaysian federal government could not be verified in the original text.

1969 Communal Conflict Uprising · 1969

Discontent over the results of the 1969 Malaysian general election led to clashes in Kuala Lumpur, and rumors of these clashes spread; on May 31, Malay crowds and Chinese violent organizations (triads) also began attacking each other in Singapore. The clashes lasted for seven days (Government SG101). The Singapore Police Annual (2013) describes the Little India riots of 2013 as 'the worst riots since 1969,' using 1969 as a benchmark for the previous major riot.

Fatalities and Injuries4 dead, approximately 80 injured (Singapore Government SG101)Unconfirmed

Little India Riot Uprising · 2013

Shortly after a traffic accident in Little India on the night of December 8, 2013, in which a bus struck and killed a pedestrian, an angry crowd gathered within minutes to attack the bus driver and conductor. The Singapore Police Annual (2013) records that approximately 400 people in the crowd (foreign workers) turned violent, describing it as 'the worst riots since 1969' and 'the first major public riot in 40 years.' The first responding police officers were met with bottles and stones; they recovered the body of a victim, rescued the driver and conductor, and some officers were injured. After the Police Tactical Unit (PTU) arrived, the crowd dispersed within about an hour, and arrests and scene clearance were completed two hours after the start of the incident. Subsequently, under strengthened measures under the Public Order (Preservation) Act, restrictions on liquor sales and drinking and increased CCTV installation were implemented. An inquiry committee (COI) appointed by the Minister for Home Affairs began public hearings on February 19, 2014; Central Narcotics Bureau investigators took statements from approximately 300 witnesses, and the committee also visited foreign worker dormitories to hear views on living conditions.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Number of ParticipantsApproximately 400 (Singapore Police Annual 2013)Unconfirmed
Police DamageInjuries to 'scores' of police officers and damaged vehicles (description by Tanglin Police Station, Police Annual 2013)Unconfirmed

Data records: Mass Mobilization v5.1 collection: Singapore, 2 events in 2012 and 5 in 2014, with no entry for 2013. The ACLED aggregate includes only data from 2020 onward. The police annual mentions unrest on Lavender Street during the 2001 general election; this overlaps in time with the MM collection's single 2001 event (1 violent protester), though whether it is the same incident is unconfirmed.

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Rebellion of Trunajaya (Trunajaya) Early Modern (Mataram Kingdom) · 1674–1680

This was a rebellion raised by Trunajaya (Raden Trunojoyo), a nobleman from Madura, who sought to oust King Amangkurat I of Mataram from the throne. Imania et al. (2022) describe Amangkurat I's rule as having been influenced by close cooperation with the Dutch East India Company (VOC), and they cite as background that his rule was characterized by unfair practices that caused hardship to the people, against which anti-VOC sentiment was expressed in Islamic language. In the 1674 rebellion, the capital Pleret was sacked and the king fled (Atlas of Mutual Heritage). Two princes contested the succession; the elder, Anom (later Amangkurat II), supported by the VOC, defeated his younger brother Prince Puger and made Kartasura the new capital. The rebellion was suppressed when Mataram and VOC forces defeated Trunajaya's troops. This series records that this rebellion is not adjudicated as either an internal dynastic rebellion or anti-VOC resistance, but that both accounts coexist.

  1. Imania, Huda & Wasid (2022). Pergolakan Keagamaan dan Pemberontakan Raden Trunojoyo Terhadap Mataram Tahun 1674-1680 M. Qurthuba 5(2): 129–142 · Academic article · Orientation: Research of the Indonesian Islamic University (emphasizing religious resistance) — https://jurnalfahum.uinsa.ac.id/index.php/qurthuba/article/view/802
  2. Atlas of Mutual Heritage — Kraton Pleret · Public DB · Orientation: Database developed in cooperation with the Dutch National Archive for Cultural Heritage (VOC heritage perspective) — https://www.atlasofmutualheritage.nl/en/id/4989

Massacre of Chinese in Batavia (Geger Pacinan) Early Modern (VOC rule) · 1740

From October 9 to 11, 1740, riots and massacres targeting the Chinese took place in Batavia, the VOC headquarters, and its surroundings, resulting in more than 10,000 deaths among the Chinese (Sanalin et al., 2024). Studies cite as causes the vigorous commercial activities of Chinese merchants in Tanah Abang and Glodok, Chinese discontent with VOC policies reaching a peak immediately prior to the event, political tensions within the VOC, and conflicting interests between Governor Adrian van der Goes and Council President Gustaaf Willem van Imhoff. The subsequent unrest in the Javanese interior known as the 'Chinese disturbances (Geger Pacinan)' and the rebellion of Mas Garendi are described as the background to the division of Mataram (Mastingah, 2011).

  1. Sanalin, Putri, Lestari & Putra (2024). Exploring the Historical Background of the Massacre of Ethnic Chinese in Batavia in 1740. Journal of Indonesian History 12(1) · Academic journal · Orientation: Research from an Indonesian university — https://journal.unnes.ac.id/journals/jih/article/view/5981
  2. Mastingah (2011). Sekitar Perjanjian Giyanti 1755 M. Undergraduate thesis, UIN Sunan Kalijaga · Thesis · Orientation: Undergraduate thesis (based on abstract) — https://digilib.uin-suka.ac.id/id/eprint/5859/

The Third Javanese Succession War and the Giyanti Treaty Recent Modern (Mataram Partition) · 1746–1755

The third Javanese succession war (1746–), in which Prince Mangkubumi and Mas Said participated, ended with the Giyanti Treaty of February 13, 1755, after the VOC mediated negotiations exploiting fissures on both sides. Under the treaty, Mataram was divided into the Surakarta Kingdom (Kasunanan) and the Yogyakarta Sultanate (Kasultanan), and Mangkubumi ruled Yogyakarta as Hamengkubuwono I. Subsequently, Mangkunegaran split from Surakarta in 1757 and Pakualaman from Yogyakarta in 1813 (Mastingah, 2011 abstract). The abstract identifies the Surakarta monarch as Pakubuwono II, but this could not be checked against other consulted sources, so the monarch's name is not used in the text.

  1. Mastingah (2011). Sekitar Perjanjian Giyanti 1755 M (Pecahnya Menjadi Kasunanan Surakarta dan Kasultanan Yogyakarta). UIN Sunan Kalijaga · Thesis · Orientation: Undergraduate thesis (based on abstract) — https://digilib.uin-suka.ac.id/id/eprint/5859/

5. Modern Events in the Literature

Javanese War (Diponegoro War) Uprising · 1825–1830

An armed uprising against the Dutch colonial government led by Prince Diponegoro, a Yogyakarta royal, that lasted approximately five years across the former Mataram domain. The war ended on March 28, 1830, when the Dutch summoned Diponegoro to Magelang under the pretext of negotiations and arrested him; about one-quarter of the cultivated land was damaged, and Dutch military expenditures are described as approximately 20 million guilders (Mubarok, 2011 abstract). Peter Carey's research treats this war as a more complex event than a mere war to expel colonial forces, arguing that it reconfigured the old order of Javanese society (Kyoto Review review).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original Dutch colonial government tally unverified)Approx. 200,000 in Java · 15,000 Dutch soldiers (Mubarok 2011 abstract, cited as 'historical data estimates')
Military ExpenditureUnconfirmedApprox. 20 million guilders (Mubarok 2011 abstract)

Data Record: An event after 1816, but the COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 collection in data/IDN.json does not include an entry for Indonesia as a state unit (colonial period; not a member of the state system).

  1. Mubarok, M. Isa Akbar (2011). Perang Jawa 1825-1830 (Tinjauan Politik dan Strategi Militer). Undergraduate thesis, UIN Sunan Kalijaga · Thesis · Orientation: undergraduate thesis (based on abstract) — https://digilib.uin-suka.ac.id/id/eprint/5425/
  2. Udiani, C. M. (2019). Review – Life is Flame, 70th Anniversary Festschrift for Professor Peter Carey. Kyoto Review of Southeast Asia 26 · Academic article · Orientation: review — https://kyotoreview.org/?p=18719

Aceh War Uprising · 1873–1913

In March 1873, a Dutch expeditionary force of approximately 3,000 men led by Colonel Kühner attacked the palace of the Sultanate of Aceh and was repulsed; Kühner was killed (56 dead and 438 wounded in the first expedition). Later that year, Lieutenant General van Swieten occupied Banda Aceh with a force of more than 8,500 troops. The Islamic scholar Snouck Hurgronje argued for a shift to military suppression on the basis of local surveys conducted in Aceh in 1891–1893, and in 1898 van Rees was appointed governor-general and military governor of Aceh. The sultan surrendered on February 10, 1903, but resistance continued until 1904–1913. In the Gayo expedition of 1900–1903, approximately 3,000 people died, more than one-third of them women and children (Encyclopedia of Western Colonialism since 1450).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Casualties in the 1873 First Expedition56 dead, 438 wounded (cited from source literature; original tallying agency unconfirmed)Unconfirmed
Deaths in the 1900–1903 Gayo ExpeditionUnconfirmedApprox. 3,000, more than one-third women and children (Encyclopedia of Western Colonialism since 1450)
Total War DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Not included in COW or UCDP state-unit collections (data/IDN.json) (colonial period).

  1. Aceh War — Encyclopedia of Western Colonialism since 1450 (Encyclopedia.com) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: English-language academic encyclopedia (author not specified) — https://encyclopedia.com/history/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/aceh-war

Communist Party (PKI) Uprising and the Boven Digoel Concentration Camp, 1926–27 Uprising · 1926–1927

On November 12, 1926, the Indonesian Communist Party (PKI) staged an uprising against the colonial government in Batavia and other locations, targeting police stations and Dutch officials. On November 18, the Council of the Indies (Raad van Nederlandsch-Indië) decided at a special session to build a place of exile for the ringleaders of the uprising upstream on the Digul River in southern Papua. After 1927, the number of inmates at the Boven Digoel (Tanamera) detention camp exceeded 1,200, and many participants were Muslims from Banten and West Sumatra. The camp operated until the inmates were transferred to Australia just before the Pacific War; subsequently, nationalists such as Hatta and Syarif also were exiled there (Tirto.id, 2016).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Boven Digoel InmatesUnconfirmed (original colonial government tally unverified)More than 1,200 (Tirto.id 2016, Petrik Matanasi)

Data Record: No separate entry in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO collections. NAVCO 1.3 'Anti-Dutch colonial struggle' (1911–1943) records the location as West Papua (conflict zone marked), so it is unclear whether this directly corresponds to the uprising.

  1. Tirto.id (2016.03.27) Digoel, Tempat Buangan Para Pembangkang (Petrik Matanasi) · Media · Orientation: Indonesian online outlet (historical series) — https://tirto.id/digoel-tempat-buangan-para-pembangkang-uzn

Indonesian War of Independence (Revolution) Uprising · 1945–1949

On August 17, 1945, Sukarno proclaimed the independence of the Republic of Indonesia, but the Netherlands refused to recognize it and responded with force; negotiations and fighting continued for four years. The Dutch government took the position in 1969 that 'with the exception of some excesses, the military acted correctly,' but in 2005 it stated that the Netherlands should not have waged this war. A joint study conducted by KITLV, NIMH, and NIOD at the request of the government (begun in 2017, published February 17, 2022) concluded that extreme violence by Dutch troops was widespread and often deliberate, was tacitly condoned at all political, military, and judicial levels, and was concealed for a long time both during and after the war. The researchers stated that exact figures for crimes and victims could not be established.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original official tallies of Dutch and Indonesian governments unverified)Not confirmable (joint KITLV·NIMH·NIOD study, reported in The Low Countries 2022)

Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict 204 'Government of Netherlands vs Indonesian People's Army' 1946–1949 (extrasystemic, territorial). NAVCO 1.3: 'Indonesian Revolt' 1945–1949, violence, success. Not included in the COW collection of civil wars within states.

  1. Oostindie et al. (2022). Beyond the Pale: Dutch Extreme Violence in the Indonesian War of Independence, 1945-1949. Amsterdam University Press · Monograph · Orientation: Research funded by the Dutch government (KITLV·NIMH·NIOD) — https://www.aup.nl/en/book/9789048557172
  2. The Low Countries (2022.02.17) Dutch army guilty of systematic extreme violence in Indonesia · Media · Orientation: Dutch and Flemish cultural outlet — https://the-low-countries.com/article/dutch-army-guilty-of-systematic-extreme-violence-in-indonesia

Regional Rebellions of the 1950s (South Moluccas, Darul Islam, PRRI/Pemesta) Civil War · 1950–1962

Immediately after independence, the Indonesian government clashed with armed rebellions in several regions. Systematic datasets record the South Moluccan Republic (RMS) secession war of 1950, the Darul Islam rebellion of 1951–1962, and the Pemesta rebellion of 1958–1961 as civil wars that ended in government victory. Because the course of individual events could not be verified from the original text in this round, only the scope of the dataset records is described.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Combat deaths (South Moluccas 1950)3,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 estimate)Unconfirmed
Combat deaths (Darul Islam 1951–1962)20,000(COW v5.1)Unconfirmed
Combat deaths (Pemesta 1958–1961)6,000(COW v5.1)Unconfirmed

Data Record: COW v5.1 war_num 734·736·742. UCDP v26.1: conflict 237 (South Moluccas, 1950), conflict 243 (Darul Islam, Pemesta, PRRI, 1953–1961, against the government). The periods of Darul Islam differ among COW (1951–1962), UCDP (1953–1961), and NAVCO (1953).

  1. COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/IDN.json) · Public DB — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/
  2. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/downloads/

The September 30th Incident and the 1965–66 Massacres, Suharto's Rule Coup · 1965–1966

On the night of September 30, 1965, left-wing officers kidnapped and killed six army generals and one lieutenant. This was followed by large-scale killings of members of the Communist Party (PKI) and those identified as associates. On March 11, 1966, Sukarno signed the March 11 decree (Supersemar), granting Suharto authority to restore order, and Suharto banned the PKI the following day. Approximately one year later, the Provisional People's Consultative Assembly (MPRS) stripped Sukarno of his powers and installed Suharto as acting president. The National Commission on Human Rights (Komnas HAM) investigated the testimonies of approximately 349 individuals over about three years and concluded in 2012 that there were 'serious human rights violations' in 1965–66, submitting a report to the Attorney General and attributing responsibility to security forces under Suharto. On January 11, 2023, President Joko Widodo officially acknowledged 12 serious human rights violations of the past, including PKI-related events of 1965–66, and expressed regret. The government has never produced an official tally of fatalities that has been finalized.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
FatalitiesNo official count (The Jakarta Post 2016); remarks by a senior government official: 'it will not exceed 1,000' (Luhut Binsar Pandjaitan, Coordinating Minister for Maritime Affairs, 2016); the fact-finding team established by Sukarno estimated approximately 80,000 (account of retired general Sintong Panjaitan, 2016)Approximately 500,000 (Komnas HAM report-related Human Rights First 2012; Chandra 2017 'consensus estimate'); estimated range 500,000–3,000,000 (The Jakarta Post 2016)

Data records: Powell & Thyne (V2026.08.29): coup failure on 1965-10-01, coup success on 1966-03-12. Archigos 4.1: Sukarno's irregular departure from office by the military on 1966-03-12 (no foreign support), Suharto's irregular inauguration on the same day. UCDP and COW do not include the 1965–66 massacres because they fall outside their definitions of armed conflict (organized hostilities between two or more parties).

  1. Indonesia Investments — Today in Indonesian History (11 March 1966): Supersemar · Media · Orientation: investment information site historical column — https://www.indonesia-investments.com/culture/culture-columns/today-in-indonesian-history-11-march-1966-supersemar/item5383

  4. Chandra, S. (2017). New Findings on the Indonesian Killings of 1965–66. Journal of Asian Studies 76(4): 1059–1086 · Academic journal article — https://www.cambridge.org/core/product/A53ACF4B79AD100D81C0324F0834C7EB

Papuan Conflict Uprising · 1965–present

Armed clashes between the Free Papua Movement (OPM) and the Indonesian government have continued intermittently since 1965. The 12 past serious human rights violations acknowledged by the president in January 2023 include the Wasior incident (2001–2002) and the Wamena incident (2003) in Papua. The designation of West Papua is a geographical record, not a political judgment (Volume 1, Part 3).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Combat Fatalities (1967–1968)3,000 (COW v5.1 estimate for the 'First West Papua War')Unconfirmed

Data records: UCDP v26.1: conflict 291 'Government of Indonesia vs OPM' 1965–2025, active 17 years (over 1,000 in 4 years). COW v5.1: war recorded only for 1967–1968. NAVCO 1.3: 'West Papua rebels' 1965–1978 (violence, failure), 'West Papua self-determination struggle' 1979–2019 (non-violence, failure) — NAVCO locates the conflict in the conflict zone (West Papua). The periods covered by the datasets differ.

  1. UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset v26.1 (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://ucdp.uu.se/downloads/
  2. Medcom.id (2023.01.11) 12-case list · Media · Orientation: Indonesian media group — https://www.medcom.id/english/national/eN4qy1yb-govt-acknowledges-12-serious-human-rights-violations-jokowi

Aceh Separatist Armed Struggle (GAM) and the Helsinki Memorandum of Understanding Civil War · 1976–2005

Armed clashes between the Free Aceh Movement (GAM) and the Indonesian government progressed through negotiations after the Indian Ocean tsunami on December 26, 2004, and concluded with the signing of the Helsinki Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) on August 15, 2005 (The Jakarta Post). Among the 12 serious human rights violations acknowledged by the government in 2023, the Lume Jeda and Pos Satui incidents (1989), the Simpang KKA incident (1999), and the Jambo Krueng Kluet incident (2003) in Aceh were included.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Conflict FatalitiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data records: COW v5.1: First Aceh War 1990–1991 (combat deaths 1,500), Second Aceh War 1999–2002 (designated 1999–2004, combat deaths 5,500). UCDP v26.1: conflict 366 1990–2005 (active 9 years, 0 years with over 1,000 fatalities). NAVCO 1.3: GAM partial success 1975–2005. Start dates differ across datasets (NAVCO 1975, COW·UCDP 1990).

  1. The Jakarta Post (2014.12.22) The tsunami brought peace (Ati Nurbaiti) · Media · Lean: Indonesian English-language daily — https://www.thejakartapost.com/news/2014/12/22/the-tsunami-brought-peace
  2. Medcom.id (2023.01.11) 12-case list · Media · Lean: Indonesian media group — https://www.medcom.id/english/national/eN4qy1yb-govt-acknowledges-12-serious-human-rights-violations-jokowi

Occupation and Conflict in East Timor Civil War · 1975–1999

Following Indonesia's entry into East Timor in 1975, armed conflict continued until 1999. East Timor became an independent state (Timor-Leste) in 2002; the detailed narrative is placed in the Timor-Leste country profile. This country profile connects only Indonesian-side dataset records.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
Combat Fatalities (1976–1979)5,000(COW v5.1 'East Timorese War phase 3')Unconfirmed (see Timor-Leste country profile)

Data record: In the COW v5.1 collection, war_num 806 appears twice (pipeline data/IDN.json). UCDP v26.1: conflict 330 'Government of Indonesia vs CNRT' 1975–1999.

  1. COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/IDN.json) · Public DB — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

May 1998 Riots and Suharto's Resignation (Reform Era) Regime Overthrow · 1998

Large-scale riots broke out in Jakarta and other locations on May 13–15, 1998, burning shops and buildings and leaving many dead. The government Fact-Finding Team (TGPF) recorded conflicting death tolls from different agencies side by side and confirmed 85 cases of sexual violence (including 52 rapes). Suharto resigned on May 21, 1998. On June 16, 2025, Minister of Culture Fadli Zon stated that there was no evidence of mass rape, and the National Commission on Women's Rights (Komnas Perempuan) demanded a retraction, stating that the TGPF documents were official state documents (Hukumonline). The 1998 May riots, the forced disappearances of 1997–1998, and the Trisakti and Semanggi incidents were included in the 12 major human rights violations recognized by the government in 2023.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
Jakarta Fatalities451 (Jakarta Metropolitan Police Polda Metro Jaya); 463 (Military Regional Command Kodam, including security personnel); 288 (Jakarta Provincial Government Pemda DKI)1,217 (Humanitarian volunteer team Tim Relawan: fires 1,190, weapons and other items 27)
InjuriesNot tallied (Polda); 69 (Kodam); 101 (Pemda DKI)91 (Tim Relawan)
Sexual Violence Victims85 confirmed by TGPF (including 52 rapes)Minister of Culture 'No Evidence of Large-Scale Rape' (2025) — Rejected by Komnas Perempuan

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'anti-Suharto campaign' 1997–1998 nonviolent and successful. Archigos 4.1: Suharto's resignation on 1998-05-21 recorded as regular (Regular) — although he resigned amid mass protests, the form of his departure was classified as regular. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 23 events in 1998 (11 violent protests; the highest for 1990–2020).

  2. Hukumonline (2025.06.17) Rudapaksa Mei 1998 fakta sejarah · Media · Lean: Legal specialist outlet — https://www.hukumonline.com/berita/a/rudapaksa-mei-1998-fakta-sejarah-lt6850ff6161123?page=all
  3. Medcom.id (2023.01.11) 12-case list · Media · Lean: Indonesian media group — https://www.medcom.id/english/national/eN4qy1yb-govt-acknowledges-12-serious-human-rights-violations-jokowi

August 2025 Protests Against MPs' Housing Allowance Protest · 2025

In August 2025, protests began in Jakarta following reports that all 580 members of the House of Representatives received a housing allowance of 50 million rupiah per month in addition to their salaries. After delivery driver Apan Kurniawan (21) was killed by a police mobile unit armored vehicle on August 28, the protests spread nationwide. The National Commission on Human Rights (Komnas HAM) reported preliminary findings at a House committee hearing on September 29 that he had died after being struck by a police tactical vehicle. The scale of fatalities, injuries, and arrests was recorded differently across agencies.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
Fatalities9 confirmed (Komnas HAM preliminary results, 2025.09.29)Unconfirmed (reports of human rights organizations tallying 10 are not reviewed in the original text)
Injuries655 (Komnas HAM); 1,363 across 12 provinces (ombudsman)Unconfirmed
Arrests and Detentions1,689 (police); 2,332 (Komnas HAM 9.29); approx. 951 in Jakarta through 8.28 (Komnas HAM, NPR report); 4,667 detained (ombudsman)Unconfirmed

Data record: ACLED (pipeline version): 4,334 protests in Indonesia in 2025 (the highest since 2015), political violence 631 events, 258 deaths. The Mass Mobilization collection is incomplete up to 2020 and is not included.

5. Modern Events in the Literature

The 1820 Anti-Vietnamese Uprising Led by 'the Saint (nak sel)' Uprising · 1820

In 1820, an anti-Vietnamese uprising led by 'the Saint (nak sel)' broke out in border areas of Cambodia and Vietnam; Buddhist monks participated, and complicity among Cambodian officials was alleged. Chandler (1975) describes the uprising in connection with the mobilization of Cambodian labor for the Vinh Te canal project in Vietnam, identifying as grievances the non-payment of forced-labor wages, changes to tax and household registration records, land loss, and the replacement of local patrons by Vietnamese officials. The British envoy Crawford reported that approximately 10,000 workers on the canal had died. Some participants were executed in Phnom Penh and Saigon. Chandler characterizes this as a 'response to pre-colonial imperialism and pre-nationalist conditions.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths of Vinh Te Canal WorkersUnconfirmed (original texts of Vietnamese Nguyen court records unverified)Approximately 10,000 (Crawford report, cited in Chandler 1975 n.15)

Data record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or UCDP (outside state structures after 1816; data/KHM.json checked).

  1. Chandler, D. P. (1975). An Anti-Vietnamese Rebellion in Early Nineteenth Century Cambodia: Pre-colonial Imperialism and a Pre-Nationalist Response. Journal of Southeast Asian Studies 6(1): 16–24 · Academic journal · Orientation: Western academia (citing British sources such as Crawford) — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-southeast-asian-studies/article/an-antivietnamese-rebellion-in-early-nineteenth-century-cambodia-precolonial-imperialism-and-a-prenationalist-response/13EF3837803AAB0565B338D8A06E46DF

The Samlot Uprising Uprising · 1967

Cambodia's economy depended heavily on taxes on rice exports, and as sales to the Viet Cong in the black market increased, Prime Minister Lon Nol mobilized the military to implement a new rice procurement system (ramassage du paddy). The military purchased rice at prices lower than those of the black market or forced farmers to surrender their harvest. On April 2, 1967, a peasant uprising broke out in Battambang Province; King Sihanouk, who was residing in France, approved its suppression for Lon Nol. The military executed rebels, burned villages, and bombed them; it is recorded that severed heads were sent to Phnom Penh on trucks. By the end of 1967 unrest had been reported in 11 provinces. Many historians regard this event as the beginning of a civil war that continued until 1975 (Keene State College Holocaust and Genocide Studies Center data).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths and ArrestsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: UCDP v26.1 conflict 300 (government vs. rebels, 1967–1998) begins in 1967 and corresponds to the time of the Samlot uprising. COW records it from 1971 as the 'First Khmer Rouge War' — start dates differ across datasets.

  1. Keene State College Cohen Center — Cambodian Genocide: A Short History (Tom White, 2024 PDF) · Public DB · Orientation: U.S. university educational resource — https://www.keene.edu/academics/cchgs/resources/documents/cambodian-genocide/download

The March 18, 1970 Coup and the Khmer Republic Coup · 1970

On March 12, 1970, a large anti-Vietnamese riot broke out in Phnom Penh, damaging the Viet Cong and North Vietnamese embassies. On March 18, the National Assembly voted to strip Sihanouk of his powers, and General Lon Nol became acting head of state. Pro-Sihanouk mass protests were suppressed, and the Khmer Republic was proclaimed. Sihanouk established a government-in-exile (GRUNK) (Keene State College data). Although the form was a parliamentary vote, datasets classify it as an irregular ouster by the military.

Data record: Powell & Thyne: coup successful on 1970-03-18. Archigos 4.1: Sihanouk's irregular departure from power by the military on 1970-03-18 (without foreign support), into exile; Lon Nol took office irregularly on the same day.

  1. Keene State College Cohen Center — Cambodian Genocide: A Short History · Public DB · Orientation: US university educational resource — https://www.keene.edu/academics/cchgs/resources/documents/cambodian-genocide/download
  2. Powell & Thyne coup data (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://www.uky.edu/~clthyn2/coup_data/home.htm

The Civil War within Khmer Rouge and the Democratic Kampuchea Regime (Genocide) Civil War · 1970–1979

During the civil war between the Khmer Republic and the Khmer Rouge after 1970, the United States dropped more than 2.7 million tons of bombs on over 113,000 sites in Cambodia, and more than 2 million people left their homes. Lon Nol resigned and went into exile on April 1, 1975; on April 17, the Khmer Rouge entered Phnom Penh and proclaimed the 'Year Zero' (Keene State College data). Under the Democratic Kampuchea regime of 1975–1979, an estimated 500,000 to 1.5 million people died of starvation alone, while Yale University's Cambodian Genocide Program estimates total deaths at approximately 1.7 million (about one-quarter of the population), noting that the exact number is unknown. The Vietnamese military overthrew the regime in January 1979. In November 2018, Trial Chamber I of the Extraordinary Chambers in the Courts of Cambodia (ECCC) convicted Nuon Chea and Khieu Samphan of crimes against humanity, grave breaches of the Geneva Conventions, and genocide against the Vietnamese; it also convicted Nuon Chea of genocide against the Cham, sentencing both men to life imprisonment (the full judgment was delivered on March 28, 2019).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths, 1975–79No official figure confirmed (ECCC verdicts address individual acts of violence)Approximately 1,700,000 (Yale University Cambodian Genocide Program estimate, NED 2017 recataloging); starvation deaths 500,000–1,500,000 (Keene State College data)
Mass GravesUnconfirmedMapping of 19,521 mass graves and 194 detention centers (Cambodian Documentation Center DC-Cam, NED 2017 recataloging)
Combat Deaths in the 1971–75 Civil War100,398 (COW v5.1 estimate)Unconfirmed

Data record: COW v5.1: First Khmer Rouge War 1971–1975 (combat deaths 100,398, internationalized). UCDP v26.1: conflict 300 (internationalized civil war, 1967–1998, over 1,000 for 8 years), conflict 328 (Cambodia–Vietnam interstate 1975–1977). Archigos: Lon Nol ousted by rebels on 1975-04-10; Pol Pot ousted by foreign military threat on 1979-01-07. NAVCO 1.3: Khmer Rouge success 1970–1975; KUFNS success 1978–1979. The internal massacres of the 1975–79 regime were unilateral violence and fall outside the definitions of armed conflict used by COW and UCDP.

March 30, 1997 Grenade Attack Protest · 1997

On March 30, 1997, four grenades were thrown at a rally of about 200 supporters of the Cambodian People's Party (led by Sam Rainsy) protesting judicial corruption in a park across from the National Assembly in Phnom Penh, killing at least 16 people and injuring more than 150. A US citizen was seriously injured, and the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) investigated; according to a June 1997 Washington Post report, an FBI confidential preliminary report had 'tentatively' identified Hun Sen's bodyguard unit as responsible. Human Rights Watch reported in 2007 that the Cambodian authorities had never conducted a serious investigation and that the FBI investigation had effectively been discontinued. Hun Sen was reported to have demanded the arrest of the rally organizers and their expulsion from the country.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths·InjuriesUnconfirmed (original government figures not verified)At least 16 dead, over 150 injured (Human Rights Watch 2007)

Data record: outside UCDP and COW definitions (one-off attack). Mass Mobilization v5.1: 2 events in 1997.

  1. Human Rights Watch (2007.03.29) Cambodia: After 10 Years, No Justice for Grenade Attack on Opposition · Human rights organization · Orientation: international human rights organization (critical of government) — https://www.hrw.org/news/2007/03/29/cambodia-after-10-years-no-justice-grenade-attack-opposition

Armed clashes in Phnom Penh, July 5–6, 1997 Coup · 1997

From July 5 to 6, 1997, street fighting broke out in Phnom Penh between forces loyal to Deputy Prime Minister Hun Sen and those loyal to Prime Minister Norodom Ranariddh (FUNCINPEC). The government (the Hun Sen faction) asserted in its White Paper "Ranariddh's Provocation Strategy" that Ranariddh had been building up military capacity for 18 months, seeking military support from remaining Khmer Rouge forces under Pol Pot, and that the weapons seized by port authorities in May were intended to arm his forces against the CPP; Hun Sen accused Ranariddh of endangering national security by bringing Khmer Rouge troops into the capital. Joint Defense Minister Tea Ban explained this as an operation to eliminate 'illegal elements.' Ranariddh called it a 'cruel and barbaric coup' from Paris. The Cambodia Daily reported that analysts had found no convincing evidence that Ranariddh had brought Khmer Rouge forces into Phnom Penh. Human Rights Watch (August 1997) characterized the event as a coup led by Hun Sen and recorded that between 30 and 40 FUNCINPEC members were killed while in custody by CPP forces on the first days. Hun Sen apologized and demanded staff changes, claiming that the UN Human Rights Centre's Phnom Penh office had falsely reported executions.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Killed in Custody (Initial)The government disputed the UN Human Rights Centre's report of executions as false (described by HRW 1997)30–40 FUNCINPEC members (Human Rights Watch 1997.08)

Data Records: Powell & Thyne: 1997-07-05 coup successful. Archigos 4.1: Ranariddh 1997-07-06 irregularly ousted by other government actors (went into exile), Hun Sen took office on the same day — although Hun Sen had been Prime Minister since 1985, Archigos records 1997-07-06 as his date of accession. UCDP conflict 300 lists 'Military faction (Forces of Hun Sen)' under the event.

  1. Human Rights Watch (1997.08) Cambodia: Aftermath of the Coup, Vol. 9 No. 8(C) · Human rights organization · Orientation: International human rights organization (critical of government) — https://www.hrw.org/legacy/reports/1997/cambodia/Cambodia.htm
  2. The Cambodia Daily — A Look Back at the Buildup and Explanations Behind the Fighting July 5-6 · Media · Orientation: Independent English-language daily (closed 2017), citing content from the government white paper — https://english.cambodiadaily.com/?p=9202

2013–14 Election Protest and Veng Sreng Shooting Protest · 2013–2014

On July 28, 2013, following the results of the general election, the opposition Cambodia National Rescue Party (CNRP) alleged electoral fraud, demanded the resignation of Prime Minister Hun Sen and new elections, and boycotted parliament. At the same time, garment workers went on strike demanding an increase in the minimum wage (to $160 per month). On January 3, 2014, near the Kantha Bopha hospital and the Kandal Province Industrial Park in Veng Sreng Road, Phnom Penh, a mixed security force opened fire with live ammunition, killing four workers (Kim Phalyup, Sam Rabi, Yean Liti, Peng Kosal) and injuring at least 38; Kem Sokha, who was shot and taken away, is missing. Ricardo (LICADHO) called this the worst state violence in 15 years. A gendarmerie spokesperson stated that nine police officers were injured by stones and slingshots. Twenty-three union leaders and activists were arrested at the time and held for five months; as of January 2019, none of those among the security forces have been dismissed or prosecuted.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (no official death toll reported in RFA article)4 dead, 1 missing (LICADHO and other civil society organizations 2019)
Injuries9 police officers injured (gendarmerie spokesperson Kheng Tito)At least 38 (LICADHO); approximately 40 (RFA)

Data Records: NAVCO 1.3: '2013 Cambodian election protests' 2013–2014 nonviolent and unsuccessful. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 7 events in 2013, 8 in 2014 (the highest period of 1990–2019). ACLED: 195 protests in 2013, 219 in 2014 (most collected).

  2. Radio Free Asia (2014.01.03) Four Shot Dead as Cambodian Police Open Fire on Workers' Protests · Media · Orientation: U.S.-government-funded broadcaster — https://www.rfa.org/english/news/cambodia/shooting-01032014110118.html

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War

The 1688 Ayutthaya Coup (Petraja's Rise to Power and the Expulsion of French Forces) Modern Era (Ayutthaya Dynasty) · 1688

In the later years of King Narai, diplomacy aimed at converting the king to a monotheistic religion continued in Ayutthaya, with the Greek advisor Constantine Phaulkon leading this religious diplomacy. French troops were stationed in Bangkok. In 1688, Petraja ascended the throne and expelled the French forces; Strathern (2024) describes how he successively neutralized Phaulkon, Narai, the heir favored by Narai, and the French troops in Bangkok, mobilizing the sangha (the Buddhist monastic order) to raise opposition against the 'Christian king' and leading crowds to carry the abbot of Ratchaburi (Sangharaja) all the way to the palace gates. Strathern (2021) examines how much the coup depended on popular participation, what among ethnic and foreigner hatred, anti-Christian logic of Buddhist intellectuals, or monastic organization motivated it, and concludes that because its aims were conservative and restorative, it was not a modern 'revolution' but may have broadened popular involvement in the succession of a monarch who defended Buddhism. Most accounts rely on records from French and British sources of the time.

  1. Strathern, Alan (2021). Thailand's first revolution? The role of religious mobilisation and 'the people' in the Ayutthaya rebellion of 1688. Modern Asian Studies 56(4): 1295–1328 (Oxford ORA record) · Journal article · Orientation: Academic journal article (University of Oxford); a substantial portion of primary sources are European records — https://ora.ox.ac.uk/ark:/29072/ora_5d1d3346b1c242dfad82b8468997e762
  2. Strathern, Alan (2024). Repulsion from Siam: The Revolt of 1688. In Converting Rulers, pp. 217–244. Cambridge University Press · Monograph · Orientation: Summary of a chapter in an academic monograph (publisher's page) — https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/converting-rulers/repulsion-from-siam-the-revolt-of-1688/28A6BBCCE95FB96EF6289102304BDC31

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Praya San's Rebellion and the Deposition of King Taksin 1781–1782

After the Burmese army captured and burned Ayutthaya after a 15-month siege in 1767, Taksin rebuilt the kingdom with Thon Buri as its center. According to the EBSCO overview (Haas 2024), around 1781 Taksin regarded himself as a deity and demanded that monks worship him; about 500 monks who refused were flogged, and their leaders were imprisoned. Rebel forces staged a coup and arrested Taksin; the supreme military commander Chakri, who was on a Cambodian campaign, returned to Thon Buri, investigated the charges, and left punishment to the bureaucracy; Chakri subsequently ascended the throne (Rama I, 1782). A Mahasarakham University thesis (2015) identifies Praya San as the leader of the rebellion that caused the turmoil in Thon Buri and analyzes him as having betrayed both Taksin and Chakri. The veracity of the claim itself that Taksin was mentally ill is not adjudicated on the basis of the consulted materials.

  1. Haas, Michael (2024). Taksin. EBSCO Research Starters · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Academic database overview (secondary narrative) — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/taksin/
  2. Wrañgkhana Nipattasukhik (2015). The Treason of Phraya San at the Transition of Thonburi and Rattanakosin History. Silapa Khon National University Journal of Humanities 37(2): 60–77 · Article · Orientation: Thai domestic academic journal (interpretations close to the narrative of legitimacy surrounding dynastic succession possible) — https://so04.tci-thaijo.org/index.php/jasu/article/view/238632

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Pu Yibun (the Saint) Rebellion — Isan and southern Laos Uprising · 1901–1902

Millennarian popular uprisings centered on leaders who styled themselves 'Phu Bim (men of merit)' took place across northeastern Thailand (Isan), southern Laos, and the adjacent central highlands of Vietnam. Murdoch (1974) views these uprisings as having extended beyond current borders, sharing a common leadership and common local causes. Baird (2022) cites the transition of Siam from the mandala system to the Tesaphiban (central administrative circle) system, which reduced the authority and revenue of the old local elites (including the Chao Phuak), as the background, and notes that the followers of the rebel forces were estimated at approximately 6,000 in February 1902. Weerawat Somnoek (2021) describes how up to 60 'saints' rose across 13 provinces, that the state referred to them by the pejorative term 'Phii Bun (possessed persons),' and that government forces armed with trains and artillery broke the rebels in early clashes. The number of casualties at the Battle of Ubon Ratchathani Rasfia is cited as 'up to 300' according to one lecturer (Tiraphon Anmai), but this is not a verified tally.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of Followers (1902.2)Unconfirmed (original Siamese government figures unverified)Estimated at approximately 6,000 (Baird 2022, The Isaan Record)
Casualties in the Rasfia BattleUnconfirmedUp to 300 (explained by Tiraphon Anmai; cited by The Isaan Record 2021 — not a verified tally)

Data Record: Not included in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, Powell & Thyne, or Archigos (COW records only one Thai civil war, in 1972–73).

  1. Baird, Ian G. (2022.4.19). The Holy Men Revolt: A Tale of Two Countries (Part I). The Isaan Record · Media · Orientation: Independent Isan outlet; author is a geography professor at the University of Wisconsin-Madison (scholarly contribution) — https://theisaanrecord.co/2022/04/19/the-holy-men-revolt-a-tale-of-two-countries/
  2. Weerawat Somnoek (2021.7.3). Phi Bun Rebellion. The Isaan Record (Opinion) · Media · Orientation: Opinion outlet of an independent regional media (rebel perspective and local viewpoint) — https://theisaanrecord.co/2021/07/03/phi-bun-rebellion/
  3. Murdoch, John B. (1974). The 1901–1902 'Holy Man's' Rebellion. Journal of the Siam Society 62(1) · Academic Paper · Orientation: Academic journal (Siam Society journal) — https://thesiamsociety.org/wp-content/uploads/1974/03/JSS_062_1e_Murdoch_1901to1902HolyMansRebellion.pdf

1932 Constitutional Revolution (People's Party Coup) Overthrow of Regime · 1932

On June 24, 1932, members of the People's Party (Khana Ratsadon) seized the commander of the Royal Guards and arrested more than 40 royal family members before declaring that the absolute monarchy had been 'overthrown.' The coup leadership demanded that King Prajadhipok (Rama VII) introduce a constitutional monarchy. Prajadhipok was the last absolute monarch of Siam; he abdicated, died in Britain in 1941, and his remains were returned to Thailand in 1949.

Data Record: Archigos 4.1: Records Rama VII's departure from office on 1932-12-10 (date of promulgation of the constitution) as 'ousted by the military (irregular)' — differs from the coup date (6.24). Powell & Thyne coup data begin in 1950 and are therefore not included. Not recorded in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO.

  1. Bangkok Post Thailand Journey, '24 May 1949: Nation pays homage to King Rama VII' · Media · Orientation: Thai major English-language daily chronology (royal-related accounts subject to domestic legal constraints) — https://thailandjourney.bangkokpost.com/timeline/1949/nation-pays-homage-to-king-rama-vii

October 14 Popular Uprising (Toppling of the Tanom Regime) Protest · 1973

On October 14, 1973, troops attempted to disperse tens of thousands of protesters gathered in front of Chitralada Palace in Bangkok, and gunfire erupted. Reports that a ceasefire had been reached between the regime and student leaders the previous night make it unclear how the shooting began; accounts coexist that tear gas was fired first and that troops under Colonel Narong Nantichai opened fire around the palace and Democracy Monument while Narong shot from a helicopter. The clashes continued throughout the day, and student 'commando units' threw Molotov cocktails and set fire to government buildings including the Ministry of Interior headquarters. King Bhumibol opened the palace grounds as a refuge and addressed the nation that night. This uprising forced the Tanom Kittikachorn regime to step down.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths77 (official tally, cited in Bangkok Post chronology)First Bangkok Post report: 'at least 400' → subsequently revised downward; exact number unknown
Injuries857 (official tally)Over 800 (Bangkok Post chronology)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 'student protests' (1973, nonviolent, successful). Archigos 4.1: Tanom 1973-10-13 'ousted by popular uprising' (peak of uprising on 10.14, one day off), successor Sanan 1973-10-14 irregular accession. Not a Powell & Thyne coup (not included).

  1. Bangkok Post Thailand Journey, '14 October 1973: More than 70 protesters killed by soldiers' · Media · Orientation: Thai major English-language daily chronology (explicitly notes history of correcting earlier reports) — https://thailandjourney.bangkokpost.com/timeline/1973/more-than-70-protesters-killed-by-soldiers

October 6 Thammasat University Massacre and Military Coup Coup · 1976

Before dawn on October 6, 1976, police and military personnel, along with right-wing groups (Red Gaur, Nawaphon, Boy Scouts), attacked Thammasat University, where students had gathered to protest the return of Thanom Kittikachorn from exile. The attackers were provoked by photographs they believed showed students hanging a model of the Crown Prince in effigy and by radio broadcasts inciting murder. Students were beaten, killed, and sexually assaulted; some were hanged from trees, and reports also emerged of retaliatory gunfire by students. More than 1,000 people were arrested. That same evening at 6:00 p.m., state media announced a coup, martial law, and military rule. Thongchai (2020) addresses the 'silence' that followed this event and the problem of memory.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths46 (official tally, Bangkok Post chronology)Student groups claim that the actual death toll was far higher (Bangkok Post chronology account; independent figures unverified)
Injuries and ArrestsOver 1,000 arrestedHundreds injured (Bangkok Post chronology)

Data record: Powell & Thyne: 1976-10-06 coup successful. Archigos 4.1: Seni Pramoj ousted military 1976-10-06 (irregular), Same day irregularly took power. The massacre itself (military and militia killing protesters) is not recorded in NAVCO or UCDP.

  1. Bangkok Post Thailand Journey, '6 October 1976: Dozens slaughtered at Thammasat, government toppled' · Media · Orientation: Chronology of a major Thai English-language daily — https://thailandjourney.bangkokpost.com/timeline/1976/dozens-slaughtered-at-thammasat-government-toppled
  2. Thongchai Winichakul (2020). Moments of Silence: The Unforgetting of the October 6, 1976, Massacre in Bangkok. University of Hawai'i Press · Monograph · Orientation: The author is a historian who was a student leader on the scene that day (first-person perspective) — https://uhpress.hawaii.edu/title/moments-of-silence-the-unforgetting-of-the-october-6-1976-massacre-in-bangkok/

Suppression of the 1992 Democratization Protests ('Black May') Protest · 1992

A military coup in February 1991 overthrew the elected government of Chatichai Choonhavan and established the National Peace-Keeping Council (NPKC). When General Suchinda Kraprayoon, the coup leader, became prime minister without election following the March 1992 general election, hundreds of thousands protested his appointment in May 1992. According to Amnesty International, security forces fired at protesters' head height and beat civilians who posed no threat. On May 23, Prime Minister Suchinda announced a sweeping amnesty applicable to both protesters and the military and resigned the following day; a transitional government ran the country until September 1992, when Chuan Leekpai was elected prime minister. The amnesty decree has since been upheld by the Thai judiciary. Although several investigative commissions were established by the government, no official responsible for the order to fire was publicly identified (as of 2002).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text of the Thai government's official tally unverified)At least 52 (Amnesty International 2002)
MissingUnconfirmed34 'missing' (Amnesty International 2002); the UN Working Group on Enforced or Involuntary Disappearances received 31 of 34 cases

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 'pro-dem movement' (1992, nonviolent, successful). Mass Mobilization v5.1: 4 events in 1992. Powell & Thyne: 1991-02-23 coup successful (background). Archigos 4.1: Suchinda's resignation is not listed in the irregular exit list.

  1. Amnesty International (2002.5.16). Thailand: 10 years later — still no justice for the May 1992 victims. AI Index ASA 39/004/2002 · Human rights organization · Orientation: International human rights organization (inclined to hold government and military accountable) — https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2021/06/asa390042002en.pdf

Southern Border Provinces Conflict — Krue Se Temple and Pattani Events Civil War · 2004–

On the morning of April 28, 2004, armed groups simultaneously attacked 11 government facilities in Pattani, Yala, and Songkhla provinces. The siege of the Krue Se temple in Pattani ended approximately nine hours later with a final military assault, leaving 32 armed militants and 3 security personnel dead inside the temple. According to Thai authorities' tally, 107 militants and 5 security personnel died across the 11 clashes that day. In July 2004, an independent commission of inquiry concluded that although the armed groups had initiated violence first, security authorities had used excessive force and heavy weaponry in relation to the threat, and General Parnlop Pinmanee had failed to follow orders to surround and negotiate. On October 25, 2004, security forces fired live ammunition into a crowd of more than 1,500 protesters demanding the release of six village defense volunteers outside the Pattani police station in Narathiwat province, killing 7 people; during the subsequent transport of over 1,300 people with their hands tied behind their backs in multiple layers on military trucks for approximately 150 km, 78 died of suffocation. On August 23, 2024, a court in Narathiwat province recognized the grounds for referring seven former high-ranking military, police, and administrative officials to trial, and the Attorney General indicted an additional eight, resulting in arrest warrants issued for 14 individuals; however, the defendants did not appear in court, and the 20-year statute of limitations expired on October 25, 2024. Casualty tallies for the conflict as a whole vary depending on the tallying body.

Death of Diabai (2004.10.25)Unconfirmed (original government announcement unverified); Songkhla Provincial Court autopsy (2009): 78 died of suffocation85 — 7 shot at the scene, 78 while being transported in a truck (ICJ 2024)

2010 'Red Shirt' protests and suppression Protests · 2010

From March to May 2010, the United Front for Democracy Against Dictatorship (UDD, 'red shirts') held large-scale anti-government protests in Bangkok and elsewhere. Following the April 7 incident at the parliament building, a state of emergency was declared; on April 10, during clashes at Phan Fa Bridge, armed elements associated with the UDD ('black shirts') used rifles and grenades against the military, and Colonel Romklao Tuwattham appears to have been killed by a targeted grenade attack. On May 13, red shirt supporter Colonel Katya Sawatdipon was shot in the head and died; on May 19, troops backed by armored vehicles broke through the Ratchaprasong barricades, firing live ammunition at protesters, medical personnel, and journalists. Inside and outside Wat Phutthamonthon, at least four people, including medical volunteers, were killed by military gunfire. Subsequently, arson continued in Bangkok and elsewhere. The Truth and Reconciliation Commission of Thailand (TRCT), established by the Abhisit government in July 2010, issued its final report in September 2012; the UN Human Rights Office commented that the report did not identify those responsible for the deaths.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
Deaths92 (based on the TRCT final report, UN News 2012.9.18)At least 90 (HRW 2011, based on 94 interviews)
InjuriesThousands (UN News)More than 2,000 (HRW 2011)

Data records: NAVCO 1.3 'Red Shirt Campaign' (2008–2010, nonviolent, failed). Mass Mobilization v5.1: 12 events in 2010. ACLED: 217 protests and 785 political violence events in Thailand in 2010, with 448 deaths (including southern conflict fatalities; not a figure specific to this event alone). Not included in UCDP.

  3. UN News (2012.9.18). UN rights chief welcomes Thai truth commission report · International organization · Orientation: Official UN news — https://news.un.org/en/story/2012/09/419902

2020–2021 youth democratization protests Protests · 2020–2021

On July 18, 2020, the 'Free Youth' movement held a rally in Bangkok demanding the dissolution of parliament, the drafting of a new constitution, and an end to the suppression of freedom of expression; the demands subsequently broadened to include reforms of the monarchy. On October 15, Prime Minister Prayuth declared a 'serious' state of emergency in Bangkok, and on October 16, police used water cannons mixed with blue dye and tear-inducing agents to disperse protests around Victory Monument. On November 17, at least 55 people were injured when police used water cannons and tear gas to block a march toward parliament; six of them were shot during clashes with pro-government royalist groups. On November 19, Prime Minister Prayuth instructed that 'all laws and articles' be applied to protesters, and prosecutions under Section 112 of the Criminal Code (lese majeste) resumed after an interruption of approximately three years. The Thai Lawyers for Human Rights (TLHR) and the International Federation for Human Rights (FIDH) recorded at least 461 people prosecuted under Section 112 between May 2014 and July 2026, of whom at least 199 received prison sentences, and at least 21 were children.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
November 17 Injuries (2020)Unconfirmed (original government announcement unverified)At least 55 injured, 6 shot (HRW World Report 2021)
Prosecutions under Section 112 (2014.5–2026.7)Unconfirmed (original court and prosecution statistics unverified)At least 461 prosecuted, at least 199 sentenced to imprisonment, at least 21 children (FIDH·TLHR 2026.9.2)

Data Records: ACLED: Thailand protests 828 events in 2020 and 939 in 2021 (maximum during the collection period). Mass Mobilization v5.1: 5 events in 2020 (coverage extends only to early 2020). NAVCO 1.3 covers only up to 2019, so omitted ('New Democracy Movement' 2014–2019 is the preceding movement).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Daguoyoi Rebellion (Bohol) Spanish colonial rule · 1744–1829

The rebellion began on January 24, 1744, on the island of Bohol. When a constable who had been killed at the Jesuit priest Gaspar Morales's orders was denied a Christian burial, Francisco Dagohoy, the constable's brother, incited the uprising. On the same day, Father Giuseppe Lamberti was killed, followed shortly thereafter by Father Morales. The rebel forces grew from approximately 3,000 to approximately 20,000 and are recorded as reaching 30,000 followers in 1770; they resisted Spanish rule from mountain strongholds in Inabanga and Kayelangan. Efforts by the bishop of Cebu to persuade them and some twenty military expeditions between 1744 and 1825 failed. Under Governor Mariano Ricafort, who arrived in 1825, Captain Manuel Sans landed in April 1828 and fought for about a year; Spanish rule was restored on August 31, 1829. The remaining approximately 19,000 rebels were pardoned and resettled in the villages of Batuanan, Cabulao, Catigbian, and Villar. The rebellion lasted for about 85 years and is described as the longest uprising of the Philippine colonial era.

  1. Knotwell, James (2023). Dagohoy Rebellion. EBSCO Research Starters · Encyclopedia · Tone: Academic database overview (secondary account); primary sources are records of the Spanish colonial authorities and religious orders — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/dagohoy-rebellion

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Diego and Gabriela Silang's Ilocos Uprising 1762–1763

In 1762, a British fleet from India occupied Manila, and Diego Silang of Ilocos declared himself supreme commander and local ruler under British protection in Vigan, proclaiming on December 14, 1762, the independence of the Ilocos region and the establishment of 'a government of the people.' On May 28, 1763, he was shot and killed at his home by Miguel Vicos; Vicos and Pedro Bakio are described as having been hired by church officials and local notables who harbored resentment. Silang's wife Gabriela Silang rallied the remaining troops, attacked the Bakio followers in Santa, organized a Tingian force, and advanced on Vigan but fell into an ambush; the Spanish returned to Vigan victorious on July 11, 1763. Spanish forces under Manuel Arzadon y Urrutia pursued into the interior of Abra, capturing him and 90 followers, and he was hanged in Vigan on September 20, 1763. He was forced to watch hundreds of alleged collaborators flogged before his execution, and an additional 90 were hanged.

  1. Veneracion, Jaime B. 'Silang, Gabriela (1731–1763)'. Women in World History: A Biographical Encyclopedia(Gale), Encyclopedia.com · Encyclopedia · Tone: Entry on a historical figure written by the chair of history at the University of the Philippines Diliman; perspective on Philippine national history — http://www.encyclopedia.com/women/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/silang-gabriela-1731-1763

Basi Revolt (Ilocos Norte) 1807

In 1786, the Spanish colonial government introduced a monopoly on basi, a sugarcane wine, regulating drinking and forcing producers to sell at low prices. In 1807, dissatisfied elements in several towns of Ilocos Norte rebelled against this monopoly. The local governor mobilized town mayors who controlled troops and arms in Vigan, and bloody fighting took place in Abacay; the government side won on September 7, 1807. The rebels were defeated, and their leaders were arrested and publicly executed. The painter Esteban Villanueva of Vigan painted the event in a series of fourteen oil paintings in 1821, commissioned as a warning to those plotting rebellion; the series covers troop assembly, battle, and burial through trial, hanging, and decapitation and is held by the National Museum of the Philippines.

  1. Guillermo, Alice G. & Pilar, Santiago A. '[Basi Revolt]'. CCP Encyclopedia of Philippine Art(2020.11.18) · Government report · Tone: Philippine National Commission for Culture and the Arts encyclopedia (government agency); the series itself is a record commissioned by the colonial authorities — https://epa.culturalcenter.gov.ph/3/82/2142
