5. Modern Events in the Literature

The Beginning of the 1896 Philippine Revolution (Katipunan Uprising) Uprising · 1896

On August 19, 1896, the existence of the secret society Katipunan was revealed to Spanish authorities. Teodoro Patiño told his sister about the society, and this information was passed through Father Agustín Fernández to the authorities. The authorities searched the printing press of Diario de Manila and found documents; Governor-General Ramón Blanco ordered the arrest of members. Andrés Bonifacio began moving around Caloocan and Balintawak from August 21–22, holding meetings, and 'the first cry' followed as members tore their cédulas (residence certificates) to signify their rejection of Spanish rule. The date and location of this 'first cry' are still disputed. The state-recognized commemoration date is August 23, Pugad Lawin, based on the interpretation of Teodoro Agoncillo and the recollections of Pío Valenzuela; the shrine in Quezon City also uses this date. Solita Monsod Borromeo-Bühlmann (1998), however, argues for August 26 in Balintawak based on Spanish accounts, maps, and contemporary newspapers from 1897, while the preamble of the 1897 Biac-na-Bato Constitution and draft minutes reviewed by Jim Richardson (2013) point to August 24. For August 25–27, there are records of clashes between Spanish security forces led by Lieutenant Manuel Los and Katipunan.

Data Record: The 1896–1898 anti-Spanish revolution is not recorded in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or Powell & Thyne. NAVCO 1.3 includes the 'Filipino nationalists' (targeting U.S. occupation, violence, failure) from 1899–1902.

  1. Montejo, Jeremiah Julian (2024). Ang Unang Sigaw ng Himagsikan: Isang Pagsusuring aklat sa The Cry of Balintawak ni Soledad Borromeo-Buehler. Mabini Review XIII: 29–50 · Journal article · Orientation: Book review paper in the Polytechnic University of the Philippines journal (review of Borromeo-Bühlmann's book on Balintawak) — https://publishing.pup.edu.ph/ojs/index.php/MabRev/article/download/11/15/194
  2. Quezon City Government, 'Ang Sigaw ng Pugad Lawin' · Government report · Orientation: Official municipal government guide (state-recognized August 23 account) — https://quezoncity.gov.ph/place/ang-sigaw-ng-pugad-lawin/

Martial Law (1972) and the 1986 EDSA People Power Revolution Regime overthrow · 1972–1986

On September 21, 1972, President Ferdinand Marcos signed Proclamation No. 1081, declaring martial law nationwide. The University of the Philippines Institute of Human Rights (UP IHR) describes widespread torture, forced disappearances, and extra-judicial killings during the martial rule period affecting tens of thousands of people. On February 22, 1986, Defense Secretary Juan Ponce Enrile and former Armed Forces of the Philippines Chief of Staff Fidel V. Ramos broke with Marcos, and on February 22–25, millions gathered on EDSA (Epifanio de los Santos Avenue) demanding Marcos's departure. On February 25, Corazon Aquino, the wife of assassinated Senator Benigno Aquino Jr., was inaugurated as president, and the Marcos family left Malacañang Palace. Subsequently, the Human Rights Victims' Claims Board (HRVCB), established under Republic Act No. 10368, recognized 11,103 martial rule victims as eligible for monetary compensation.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Victims of Martial Rule Human Rights Violations11,103 compensated (HRVCB, Republic Act No. 10368; only recognized victims counted)'Tens of thousands' (UP IHR 2020); Amnesty International figures (approx. 70,000 detained, approx. 34,000 tortured, approx. 3,240 killed) confirmed only through secondary citations — original text not verified

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 'People Power' (1983–1986, nonviolent, successful). Archigos 4.1: Marcos 1986-02-25 'Departed by popular protest' (exile); Aquino took irregular power on the same day. Armed conflicts of the same period are recorded separately as COW 'First Philippine-Moro War' (1972–81, combat deaths 32,000) and 'First Philippines-NPA War' (1981–86, 30,000), and UCDP CPP government conflict (1946–2025).

  1. UP Institute of Human Rights (2020.9.22). Never again. Never forget. Martial Law Revisionism and the Fight for History · Paper · Orientation: Institute of Human Rights, College of Law, University of the Philippines (perspective of the movement to remember martial rule victims) — https://law.upd.edu.ph/martial-law-revisionism-and-the-fight-for-history/
  2. GMA News (2017.2.24). A timeline of the 1986 EDSA People Power Revolution · Media · Orientation: Major private broadcaster in the Philippines — http://www.gmanetwork.com/news/topstories/specialreports/600884/a-timeline-of-the-1986-edsa-people-power-revolution/story

December 1989 Coup Attempt Coup · 1989

Several coup attempts occurred during the Corazon Aquino administration between 1986 and 1990, and the December 1989 attempt was the largest. The government established the Fact-Finding Committee (Chairman Hilario Davide Jr., the so-called Davide Commission) under Republic Act No. 6832 to investigate the failed December 1989 coup. On September 30, 1990, Resolution No. 114 recommended that the Department of Justice file charges and investigate civilians who might be legally liable in connection with the coup; on September 24, 1991, Administrative Order No. 236 dismissed one Baguio City assistant prosecutor named in the resolution from office for one year.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths and InjuriesUnconfirmed (original Davide Commission report not consulted)Unconfirmed (99 deaths and 570 injuries confirmed only in Wikipedia-derived and secondary sources)

Data record: Powell & Thyne: 5 coups on 1986-07-06, 1987-01-27, 1987-08-27, 1989-12-01, and 1990-10-04 all failed. UCDP: Government conflict (conflict_id 209) includes 'CPP, Military faction (forces of Honasan, Abenina & Zumel)' as opponent.

  1. Administrative Order No. 236 (1991.9.24), Malacañang — Lawphil Project · Government report · Orientation: Original text of a Philippine presidential administrative order (government official) — https://www.lawphil.net/executive/ao/ao1991/ao_236_1991.html

EDSA II (Estrada's Departure) 2001 Regime Overthrow · 2001

On January 16, 2001, while President Joseph Estrada was under impeachment trial, the Senate impeachment judges voted 11 to 10 against opening a second envelope purportedly containing a P3.3 billion account in the name of 'Jose Velarde,' and that night thousands gathered at the EDSA Shrine. On January 19, Armed Forces of the Philippines Chief of Staff Angelo Reyes announced at the EDSA Shrine that the military was withdrawing its support for the government, followed by Police Chief Director General Panfilo Lacson and each service commander. Around noon on January 20, Supreme Court Chief Justice Hilario G. Davide Jr. administered Vice President Gloria Macapagal-Arroyo's presidential oath of office at the EDSA Shrine, and around 2:30 p.m., the Estrada family left Malacañang Palace. In its March 2, 2001 decision in Estrada v. Desierto, the Supreme Court ruled that Estrada had resigned on the basis of the 'totality' of pre-, contemporaneous, and post-factual circumstances.

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 'Second People Power Movement' (2001, nonviolent, successful) and the pro-Estrada 'People Power III' (2001, nonviolent, failed) recorded separately for the same year. Estrada is not listed in the Archigos 4.1 (records up to 2015) list of irregular departures — in line with the Supreme Court's ruling that he had resigned. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 3 events in 2001.

  1. Supreme Court of the Philippines, Estrada v. Desierto, G.R. Nos. 146710-15 & 146738 (2001.3.2) — Lawphil Project · Government report · Tone: Supreme Court judgment (facts are judicially established facts) — https://lawphil.net/judjuris/juri2001/mar2001/gr_146710_2001.html

2017 Marawi Siege Civil War · 2017

On May 23, 2017, several hundred armed militants waving black flags in the style of the Islamic State (IS) seized the central commercial district and outlying areas of Marawi City on Mindanao Island. After approximately five months of fighting, on October 23, 2017, Defense Secretary Delfin Lorenzana declared an end to hostilities at a Southeast Asian defense ministers' meeting near Manila, stating that 'there are no more militants in Marawi City.' At least 1,131 people died in the fighting, of whom 919 were militants and 165 military and police personnel, and at least 1,780 hostages were rescued. Tens of thousands of residents fled.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (as of 2017.10.23)At least 1,131 — militants 919, military/police 165 (Philippine government announcement; AP report); the number of civilians is not separately stated in this reportUnconfirmed (independent investigation figures unverified)
Hostages RescuedAt least 1,780 (AP report)Unconfirmed

Data Records: UCDP: 'Government of Philippines vs IS' territorial conflict 2016–2024 (9 active years; 1 year with over 1,000 war deaths). ACLED: 2,610 political violence events and 4,358 fatalities in the Philippines in 2017 (including drug-enforcement-related deaths; not specific to this event alone). Not recorded in COW.

  1. Dawn/AP (2017.10.23). 'No more militants in Marawi': Philippines declares end to 5-month siege · Media · Tone: Reprinting by the Pakistani daily Dawn of an AP report; figures are from the Philippine government — https://www.dawn.com/news/1365703

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Uprising of Wonjong and Aenoe Ancient · 889

In 889 (Queen Jeongseong 3), local prefectures and counties failed to pay tribute, leading to fiscal adjustments by the court that sent envoys to demand taxes. This prompted uprisings across the country. In Sangju (present-day Sangju), Wonjong and Aenoe raised a force and rebelled; this uprising is noted as a typical peasant rebellion. The court official Nam Yeong, sent to suppress them, was unable to attack under the momentum of the peasant army, and the village headman Uyeon died in battle. Nam Yeong was executed for this, and Uyeon's son, who was about 10 years old, succeeded as village headman (accounted in the Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture).

  1. Uprising of Wonjong and Aenoe - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0040826

Uprising of Mangi and Mangsoi Medieval · 1176–1177

In January 1176 (Mingjong 6), Maengi and Maengsoyi of Myeonghaesowon, a special administrative district under Gochang jurisdiction, gathered a band and proclaimed themselves Sanghae Bicheomsa, capturing Gochang. The court responded with a carrot-and-stick approach, upgrading Myeonghaesowon to Chongsun County. After requesting an armistice in January 1177, the court neither punished them nor sent grain to induce their return; however, in May of the same year it demoted Chongsun County back to Myeonghaesowon and dispatched troops to suppress them. In July, Maengi, Maengsoyi, and others were captured by Jeong Seuye and imprisoned in Cheongju Prison, bringing the approximately one-and-a-half-year resistance to an end. It is described as a resistance against the discriminatory status of being residents of a (所) under military rule.

  1. Myeonghaesowon - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0017893

Kim Sambi and Hyo-sim's Rebellion (Um-mun and Cho-cheon's Rebellion) Middle Ages · 1193–1194

In 1193 (Mingjong 23), Kim Sambi rose from Umyeon Temple in Cheongdo as a base, while Hyo-sim rebelled in Cho-cheon (the Ulsan area); the two coordinated and expanded their power across several regions of Gyeongsang Province. The suppression forces suffered repeated defeats; records indicate that Eo Je-sun (son of the ruling military minister Eo Ui-min), who served in the punitive expedition, divulged secrets. Kim Sambi surrendered in February of the following year but was executed, and Hyo-sim was also suppressed later. As background, the expansion of Eo Ui-min's faction based in Gyeongju and resentment among the landless class are cited.

  1. Kim Sambi's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Authorial stance: personal theory (not institutional official position) — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0009297
  2. Hyo-sim Related Entry - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0009296

Mandok's Rebellion Middle Ages · 1198

In 1198 (Sinjong 1), in Gaegyeong, Manjok and others plotted an uprising aimed at freeing slaves from servitude and seizing power. The background is described as the erosion of the authority of the social order following the military coup, with cases of people from the slave class entering officialdom emerging thereafter. When a secret report by Han Chongyu, a Yulhak (Jurisprudence) Doctor, was leaked to his master, Manjok and about 100 others were executed (the method of execution is not recorded in the consulted sources).

  1. Mandok's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Authorial stance: personal theory (not institutional official position) — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0017709

Sambyeolcho Uprising Middle Ages · 1270–1273

In June 1270, soldiers opposed to the Goryeo court's return to Gaegyeong and the dissolution order for Sambyeolcho rose in rebellion under Baek Jeongsun's command, moved their base to Jindo, established a separate government, and began the uprising. In 1271, when the Mongo-Yuan coalition forces captured Jindo, the remaining forces moved to Jeju under Kim Tongjeong's command, made Hwangpado-ri Fortress their base, and resisted. The uprising was pacified in 1273 with the Mongo-Yuan coalition's assault on Jeju (the month of the fall of Jeju is given as February or April in different sources). In nature, it is more a military uprising by a regular military organization established and operated by a powerful minister against the government and the Mongols than a popular uprising; accordingly, it is classified as an 'armed uprising (partial defection of government troops)' in this series.

  1. Baek Jeongsun - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0021928
  2. Sambyeolcho - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0026604
  3. Hwangpado-ri Fortress Related Entry - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0010884

Im Gyeokjeong's Rebellion Early Joseon · 1559–1562

Im Gyeokjeong, a native of Yangju who had been a Baekjeong (a low-status group), gathered a band and made the area around Gugwalsan in Hwanghae Province his base under conditions of deteriorating livelihood caused by successive poor harvests, the arbitrary rule of the favorites Yun Won-gyeong and Lee Rang, and official extortion. He attacked local government offices and granaries and confronted government troops across several provinces. Large-scale punitive expeditions continued for about three years, and he was captured and executed in 1562 (Mingjong 17).

  1. Im Gyeokjeong - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0047411

Lee Mong-hak's Rebellion Later Joseon · 1596

In July 1596 (Seonjo 29), during the Imjin War, Lee Mong-hak plotted with Han Hyun and others at Hong Mountain Muryangsa and raised a force through a secret society called Donggabhoe, rising in Hongsan County, Chungcheong Province. After raiding Hongsan County on July 6, the rebels successively occupied Gimcheon, Jeongsan, Cheongyang, and Daehung and attacked Hongju Fortress without capturing it; Lee Mong-hak withdrew toward Deoksan early on the morning of the 11th. When news spread that a reward would be given to anyone who beheaded Lee Mong-hak regardless of whether they had participated in the rebellion, he was decapitated by rebel leaders such as Kim Gyeongchang. Han Hyun fled with his troops but was captured and sent to Seoul under Hong Ga-sin's advance. Hong Ga-sin and others who contributed to suppressing the rebellion were enfeoffed as Cheongnan Gongshin in 1604.

  1. Lee Mong-hak's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0044263
  2. Cheongnan Gongshin - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0056296

Lee Gwal's Rebellion Later Joseon · 1624

In January 22, 1624 (Jeongjo 2) (lunar calendar), Lee Gwal, a meritorious official of the Injo Restoration, led about 10,000 troops with 100 or so anti-Japanese soldiers as vanguard and departed from Yeongbien. Avoiding the main government forces, the rebels moved south and entered Hansung (Seoul) on February 11; King Injo fled to Gochang. The rebels were defeated at Anheon (Gilmaeje), and Lee Gwal was killed by his subordinates in Mokbang-ri, Icheon, on the night of February 15. King Injo returned to the capital on February 22.

  1. Lee Gwal's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0043666

Lee Injo's Rebellion (Military Coup) Later Joseon · 1728

After King Yeongjo's accession, radical factions of the Saong and Nam-in who had been marginalized from power joined forces; on March 15, 1728 (Yeongjo 4), Lee Injo captured Cheongju Fortress and rose in rebellion, with Jeong Hee-yoeng in Gyeongsang and Pak Pil-sun in Honam responding. On March 14, the court had already prepared for defense on account of an informer's tip-off; government troops led by O Myeong-hang defeated the rebels at Anseong and Chuk-san on March 24, capturing Lee Injo alive. The Gyeongsang rebels were also suppressed, and the government declared martial law on April 19. Thereafter, the Saong faction's position weakened and the grounds for the Tangpyeong policy (a policy of reconciliation) strengthened.

  1. Lee Injo's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0045630
  2. Record of the Suppression of Hong Gyeong-rae's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture (official record, perspective of the suppression side) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: the primary source is compiled from the perspective of the suppression side — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0000723

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Hong Gyeong-rae's Rebellion (Pyongan Province Peasant War) 1811–1812

On December 18, 1811 (lunar calendar), Hong Gyeong-rae rebelled from Gasa Daebok-dong and successively occupied counties and prefectures north of the Cheongcheon River: Gasa, Gwaksan, Jeongju, Seoncheon, Yongcheon, and others. Discrimination against residents of the northwest and exploitation by the factional government were cited as the justification. After counterattacks by government forces, the rebels were isolated in Jeongju Fortress; government troops buried explosives in tunnels to blow up the walls, and in April 1812 (late May solar calendar) they captured the fortress. Hong Gyeong-rae was killed in battle. Characterizations of the event coexist as the 'Peasant War' thesis and the thesis that it was a rebellion led by a marginalized local elite (Sun Joo Kim, 2007).

  1. Se Jeong Diary - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture (interpretation of a participant's diary) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: the primary source is a record by a participant on the suppression side — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0028177
  2. Sinmirok - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0032960
  3. Kim, Sun Joo (2007). Marginality and Subversion in Korea. University of Washington Press · Monograph — https://korea.fas.harvard.edu/publications/marginality-and-subversion-korea-hong-ky%C5%8Fngnae-rebellion-1812

5. Modern Events through Literature

Imsool Minlan (Jeongju Peasant Uprising) Uprising · 1862

In protest against the extortion by Gongsang Uibyeongsa Baek Nak-sin (estimated at 40,000 to 50,000 yuan), Yu Gyeojun, Kim Su-man, and others rose up on February 18, 1862 (lunar calendar) in Jinju, attacking the yŏkhae and the pohoe. The court dispatched Pak Kyusoo as Anheubsa. The uprising spread to more than 70 counties across Gyeongsang, Jeolla, and Chungcheong, and the government established the Samjeong Cheongcheong, but the reforms were soon reversed. The date of the Jinju uprising is recorded in some sources as February 14, and the uprising preceding it in Dangseong is also recorded as February 4.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of Counties with UprisingsMore than 70 counties (Cheongsammin Noran, Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture)71 locations (Mangwon Korean History Research Institute, 1988, Table on pp. 59–61; cited in Cheongju City educational materials)

Data Record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or UCDP (post-1816 but not meeting the war threshold).

  1. Jinju Uprising - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0054895
  2. Imsool Min Noran - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0047507

Donghak Peasant Revolution Uprising · 1894

Following the uprising in Gobu in January 1894, peasant troops led by Jeon Bongjun defeated government forces (yeongun) at Haengchotong in early April and occupied Jeongju Castle on April 27. As Chinese and Japanese troops entered Korea, the Cheongju Peace Treaty was concluded in early May, and gijangso were established throughout Jeolla Province. After the outbreak of the First Sino-Japanese War, the peasant troops raised the banner of anti-Japanese resistance and rose up for a second time around September; after suffering a major defeat at Uigun-chi near Gongju in early November, they were suppressed. Jeon Bongjun was arrested on December 2 in Chuncheon. In 2004, the Special Act on the Rehabilitation of Participants in the Donghak Peasant Revolution and Others was enacted, and May 11 (the Gregorian conversion of the Haengchotong victory) was designated a national memorial day in 2019.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Combat Fatalities1,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, 'Tonghak Rebellion phase 1 of 1894', war_num 623; COW minimum threshold value)Unconfirmed

Data Record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1: 'Tonghak Rebellion phase 1 of 1894', 1894, Korea vs Tonghak Society, total combat fatalities 1,000, internationalized (internationalized) = true (checked in data/KOR.json for this pipeline).

March 1st Movement Protest · 1919

Following the declaration of independence by the representatives of the nation on March 1, 1919, Manse protests spread nationwide; casualties occurred as Japanese military and police fired and suppressed them. The scale of casualties differs by more than tenfold between Japanese estimates and South Korean estimates.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Fatalities553 (Japanese estimate, reported by Seoul Shinmun 2019.02.27)7,509 (Park Eun-sik, A History of Korean Independence Movement Bloodshed, 1920); up to 934 (National Institute of Korean History March 1st Movement database, reported by Seoul Shinmun 2019.02.27)
Injuries and ArrestsUnconfirmed15,961 injured, 46,948 arrested (estimated by Park Eun-sik, cited in the report from the Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture)

  2. Seoul Shinmun (2000.03.02) Citing Figures from the Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Media — https://www.seoul.co.kr/news/2000/03/02/20000302007001

Jeju April 3 Incident Peasant Uprising · 1948–1954

Article 2 of the Special Act on the Truth Commission and Restoration of Honor for Victims of the Jeju April 3 Incident defines the incident as a disturbance that broke out on April 3, 1948, following an armed uprising by the South Korean Workers' Party Jeju Branch, and as events in which residents were killed during armed clashes and suppression from March 1, 1947 to September 21, 1954 on Jeju Island. The government's truth commission report (2003) described it as an event in which residents were killed during armed clashes and suppression from the police shooting incident of March 1, 1947, following the uprising of the South Korean Workers' Party Jeju Branch on April 3, 1948, until the full opening of Mount Halla's restricted area on September 21, 1954. The report was adopted in final form on October 15, 2003, and the president apologized officially for the wrongful acts of state power in the same month. There are differing views regarding the legal characterization of 'disturbance' and the nature of the event as defined in the report.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Victims14,028 reported victims (as of 2003, based on reports to the committee)Estimated 25,000–30,000 (same truth commission report estimate)

Data Record: The pipeline collection UCDP v26.1 contains only the inter-Korean interstate conflict (1949–53) under Korea-related conflicts and has no separate entry for April 3.

April 19 Revolution Overthrow of Regime · 1960

Protests against the fraudulent election of March 15 spread nationwide, and on April 19 multiple casualties resulted from police gunfire. President Syngman Rhee issued a statement of resignation on April 26 and stepped down on April 27.

May 16 Military Coup Coup · 1961

On the morning of May 16, 1961, Army officers led by Major General Park Chung-hee mobilized troops to occupy key facilities in Seoul and elsewhere and declared that the Military Revolutionary Council would exercise governing authority. The Chaesin cabinet collapsed after only nine months in office and was reorganized as the National Council for Unification on May 18. Although it was formerly called a 'revolution,' the current official curriculum defines it as a 'military coup.'

Data Record: Powell & Thyne (V2026.08.29): South Korean coup recorded only once, 1961-05-16 (success). Archigos 4.1: Chaesin's irregular ouster by the military on 1961-05-16.

  1. Powell & Thyne coup data (pipeline collection) · Public DB — https://www.uky.edu/~clthyn2/coup_data/home.htm
  2. The Hankyoreh articles on 5·16 · Media · Orientation: Progressive — https://pressian.com/pages/articles/196080

Busan Mass Democratic Struggle Popular Uprising · 1979

Beginning with a student protest at Pusan National University on October 16, 1979, protests against the Yushin system took place in Busan, Masan, and Changwon until around October 20. The government declared martial law over the entire area of Busan from midnight on October 18 and activated a state of siege over Masan and Changwon at noon on October 20; many were arrested and detained. The Act on the Rehabilitation and Compensation of Persons Concerning the Busan Mass Democratic Struggle serves as the legal basis for the investigation of the truth and the rehabilitation of honor.

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3: Overlaps in time with the 'South Korean anti-junta' entry for 1979–1980 (failure).

  1. Busan Mass Democratic Struggle - Korea National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0024157
  2. Act on the Rehabilitation and Compensation of Persons Concerning the Busan Mass Democratic Struggle (Act No. 18845) · Government Report · Orientation: Legislation — https://www.law.go.kr/법령/부마민주항쟁관련자의명예회복및보상등에관한법률

December 12 Military Rebellion Coup · 1979

On the evening of December 12, 1979, following the October 26 Incident, members of the new military leadership led by Jeon Doo-hwan, who headed the Joint Investigation Headquarters, seized without President Choi Kyu-hah's authorization Jeong Seung-hwa, the martial law commander-in-chief and Army Chief of Staff, from his residence; force was used in the process. President Choi gave his approval to the seizure early on the morning of the 13th, and the new military leadership took control of key military posts. On April 17, 1997, the Supreme Court ruled by a full bench (Case No. 96Do3376) that it dismissed most appeals by both defendants and prosecutors, upholding the lower court's finding that December 12 was a military rebellion and that the nationwide extension of martial law under May 17 amounted to an insurrection; Jeon Doo-hwan's life sentence and No Tae-woo's 17-year imprisonment (original Seoul High Court Case No. 96No1892) were upheld. The two men were pardoned in December 1997.

Data Record: Powell & Thyne data record only the 1961 coup for South Korea and omit December 12; Archigos records only Park Chung-hee's departure (1979-10-26).

  1. Supreme Court 1997. 4. 17. Verdict of the Full Bench in Case No. 96Do3376 (National Legislation Information Center case law) · Judgment — https://www.law.go.kr/LSW/precInfoP.do?mode=0&precSeq=209155
  2. High Court and Supreme Court Rulings on the May 18 Democratization Movement - Korean History through Historical Documents (Korean Academy of National History, Our History Net) · Public DB — https://contents.history.go.kr/front/hm/view.do?levelId=hm_151_0020

May 18 Gwangju Democratization Movement Popular Uprising · 1980

On May 18, 1980, protests in Gwangju against the nationwide extension of martial law were suppressed by the martial law forces, with continued gunfire; citizens armed themselves and resisted until they were suppressed on May 27. The Truth Commission on the May 18 Democratization Movement published a comprehensive report on June 24, 2024, sending it to the Presidential Office and the National Assembly, and stated that it had reaffirmed the martial law forces' helicopter fire and the mass killing of civilians (reported by the Seoul Shinmun), but decided that six of the seventeen special tasks were 'unverifiable.' In the same report, it confirmed the number of civilian deaths at 166 (reported by Newsis). The death toll varies depending on the tallying agency and time point.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (during period)170 (144 civilians, 22 military, 4 police; martial law command announcement 1980.05.31); 165 (Gwangju District Public Prosecutors' Office autopsy); 191 (Ministry of National Defense 1985); 193 (Gwangju City 1988)167 civilian provisional (Truth Commission 2021); 166 confirmed (Truth Commission comprehensive report 2024, reported by Newsis and Seoul Shinmun 2024.06.24)
Missing84 recognized (compensation up to 2015)73 (state recognition; The Fact 2026.07.21 report; whether the Truth Commission's re-investigation results and the reference point are confirmed unknown)
Damage among beneficiariesDeaths 155, missing 81, later deaths 110 (Gwangju Metropolitan City and Jeollanam-do, Democratic Purification Movement 100 Years; Newswire report, 2017)

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'South Korean anti-junta' 1979–1980, failed.

June Democratic Uprising People's uprising · 1987

On April 13, 1987, President Chun Doo-hwan refused to amend the constitution during his term and announced the April 13 martial law measure, under which he would run for the next presidential election under the existing constitution. Following the January death of Park Jong-chul from torture, the May 18 Justice Realization Priests' Association's exposure of cover-up and manipulation, and the shooting of Yonsei University student Hyeon Jeong-eun with tear gas on June 9, nationwide protests followed the National Assembly meeting on June 10. On June 29, ruling party floor leader Roh Tae-woo announced the June 29 Declaration, which accepted demands for a direct presidential election amendment.

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'South Korean anti-military' 1986–1987, success; 'Student's Anti-Chun Protest' 1982–1987.

  1. June Uprising - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0068343
  2. Sa Il-sam's Martial Law Measure - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0073741
  3. Seoul Shinmun (2017.06.29) Column on the 30th Anniversary of the June 29 Declaration · Media · Orientation: Column (opinion) — https://www.seoul.co.kr/news/editOpinion/weft-column/2017/06/29/20170629039008
  4. Korean Academy of Korean Studies Digital Humanities Education Wiki 'June 1987' · Public DB · Orientation: Educational wiki (supplementary) — https://dh.aks.ac.kr/Edu/wiki/index.php/1987년_6월과_영화

2016–17 Candlelight Protests and the President's Impeachment Protest · 2016–2017

In October 29, 2016, the first weekend candlelight rally was held in Gwanghwamun Plaza following the scandal of state intervention in public affairs; subsequent weekend rallies followed. The National Assembly passed an impeachment motion on December 9, 2016, with the approval of 234 out of 300 voting members, and the Constitutional Court decided on March 10, 2017, by unanimous opinion of all judges to remove President Park Geun-hye from office (2016ConNa1).

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
Number of participantsPolice estimate unconfirmed8.92 million over 9 weeks (organizer's count; reported by Money Today); 23 rallies, 17 million total (recorded in the Democracy Memorial Foundation's Open Archive)

Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Anti-Park protests' 2015–2017, success. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 3 events in 2016, 3 events in 2017.

December 3 Emergency Martial Law Declaration and Revocation, President's Impeachment Overthrow of the government · 2024–2025

On the night of December 3, 2024, President Yoon Suk-yeol declared martial law and issued Proclamation No. 1. The National Assembly voted on December 4 at 01:02 to adopt a resolution demanding the lifting of martial law by unanimous vote (190 present and voting), and at 04:20 the same day, following the President's address announcing the lifting, the Cabinet meeting at 04:29 adopted the proposal to lift martial law. On December 14, the National Assembly voted to pass an impeachment motion by 204 votes out of 300 voting members and submitted the motion of impeachment to the Constitutional Court on the same day; the Court ruled unanimously on April 4, 2025, to remove him from office (2024ConNa8). The Court found that the declaration of martial law, the deployment of military and police to the National Assembly, and the issuance of the proclamation violated the Constitution and laws.

Data record: ACLED (pipeline collection): South Korean protests, 5,810 events in 2024, 6,031 events in 2025.

  1. Constitutional Court 2025. 4. 4. 2024ConNa8 Decision (National Law Information Center Constitutional Court Precedent) · Judgment — https://www.law.go.kr/LSW/detcInfoP.do?detcSeq=197519
  2. Constitutional Court Recent Major Decisions - 2024ConNa8 Impeachment of President (Yoon Suk-yeol) · Judgment — https://www.ccourt.go.kr/site/kor/ex/bbs/View.do?cbIdx=1195&bcIdx=4243345
  3. Legal Times 'Second Presidential Removal in Constitutional History' · Media · Orientation: Legal specialist outlet — https://www.lawtimes.co.kr/news/206921

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Rebellions

Unrest and the City Wall Dispute during the Tenure of the First Captain-General Mascarenhas Recent Times (Ming-era Portuguese Settlement) · 1623–1625

The documents released by the Public Library and Archive of Évora state that the society in Macau was not peaceful from 1623, when the first Captain General Francisco Mascarenhas took office, to 1625. According to the same release, most of the documents from this period concern the fort and battery disputes of 1624–1625, in which the Chinese (Ming) were forced to demolish the city walls built by the Portuguese; this issue escalated into a severe conflict between the Senate (Senado) and the Captain General. The Chinese side viewed the walls as aimed at them and demolished most of them toward China, leaving only the sea-facing walls intact. Mascarenhas had ordered the construction of the walls, and they were almost completed during his tenure. Although the 1624 entry in the release notes 'the slave problem and rebellion' together, the actors and course of the rebellion are confirmed only at the level of the document list.

  1. Isabel Cid, 'Documentation on Macau and China in Évora's Public Library and Archives', Review of Culture (Macau Department of Culture, June–August 1990 issue) · Institutional academic journal (archive release) · Orientation: release of documents held by the Portuguese archive — perspective of Portuguese administration and church documents — https://www.icm.gov.mo/rc/viewer/20010/902

5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

The Assassination of Governor Amaral and the Pak-Shan-Lan Conflict Uprising · 1846–1849

João Maria Ferreira do Amaral took office as Governor of Macau on April 21, 1846. Enforcing the 1845 decree on free navigation, he armed-occupied Taipa and Coloane islands, ordered a road to be built from the city walls to the Barrier Gates (Portas do Cerco), and ordered the relocation of Chinese cemeteries outside the city walls; he also suspended payments of the ground rent (foro do chão) to China and expelled several officials. On March 5, 1849, he abolished the Chinese customs post 'Ho-Pu'. CGD historical records state that the measure was opposed by Chinese residents and the Leal Senado, that the governor dissolved the Leal Senado, and that general discontent arose among Macau residents as tax increases coincided with forced conscription. In the afternoon of August 22, 1849, Amaral was ambushed and killed by a group armed with knives across the Barrier Gates on the Chinese side; his body was found with its head and one remaining hand severed (a report by Lieutenant-Adjutant Jerónimo Pereira Laires). On August 25, Sub-Lieutenant Vicente Nicolau de Mesquita, a Macanese, led 32 soldiers to occupy the Pak-Shan-Lan (Pak-Shan-Lan, Passarão) fort of the Qing. The remains were returned in January 1850. Accounts from the Estado Novo period (1943) treated Amaral as a national hero, and in 2015 the Cantonese-language writer Chao Tang's novel Assassin retold the same event from three perspectives—the governor of Guangzhou, the governor of Macau, and the assassin Shen Zhiliang (沈志亮)—(Lugarinho 2018). Amaral's statue was returned to Portugal on October 28, 1992.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Troops Occupying Pak-Shan-Lan (Portuguese Side)32 soldiers (CGD historical records, Portuguese state bank)Unconfirmed
Casualties on Both SidesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: no entry for Macau in COW internal wars, UCDP, Powell & Thyne, Archigos, NAVCO, Mass Mobilization, or ACLED (all entries in data/MAC.json are blank). The conflict also falls outside the threshold of interstate war (1,000 deaths) in the COW list of interstate wars since 1816.

The 12·3 Incident (Motim 1-2-3) Uprising · 1966–1967

When permission to expand a left-wing private school on Taipa Island was withheld for nearly three months, in the morning of November 15, 1966, approximately 100 young people began demolishing three dilapidated houses without permission; after a standoff with the public security police lasting seven hours, the police intervened for 4–5 minutes (Fernandes reported two injured, while the protest side claimed multiple injuries). From November 30 to December 2, several protests took place inside the Governor's Office; on December 3 (Saturday), under the instigation of pro-Chinese business elites, Chinese students destroyed the city hall, a notary office, and a statue of Colonel Mesquita. Governor Nobre de Carvalho declared a state of siege and deployed troops; the next day, Nationalist Party-affiliated organizations became targets of attack. The Chinese People's Liberation Army deployed 3,000 troops and patrol boats to prevent Red Guards from entering Macau. Pro-Chinese groups formed the 'Anti-Portuguese Oppression Struggle Committee (13-person committee)' and, on January 29, 1967, the governor signed a written apology and accepted the closure of Nationalist Party institutions and the extradition of seven alleged 'spies' from the party (Fernandes 2010; Lam & Clayton 2016). Lam & Clayton view this event not as a simple spillover of the Cultural Revolution but as an incident in which anti-colonial, anti-Nationalist, and Cultural-Revolution-style investigations intersected. Carmen Amado Mendes evaluates that Portugal lost de facto sovereignty over Macau at this time (Macau Daily Times 2016).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

InjuriesUnconfirmed212 people (Fernandes 2010) / hundreds of people (Lam & Clayton 2016) / approximately 200 people (Macau Daily Times 2016)

2007 Labor Day Protests and Police Discharge of Firearm Protest · 2007

On May 1, 2007, a march organized by six small labor unions drew thousands of participants as passersby joined in. Banners demanded an end to corruption, improvements in housing rights and livelihoods, and opposition to the illegal influx of labor; protesters called for the resignation of Chief Executive Edmund Ho (何厚鏵). A scuffle broke out with police over the march route at a crossroads in the historic center, and a civilian was seriously injured when a police officer discharged five shots (Liu 2008).

Data record: No separate Macau entries in Mass Mobilization (by country) or ACLED (data/MAC.json blank) — whether included under the China (CHN) entry was not verified in this round.

2014 Protests Against the Bill on Retiree Benefits for Senior Officials Protest · 2014

On May 25, 2014 (Sunday), protests were held against a bill that would provide benefits to retiring officials and grant the Chief Executive immunity from criminal prosecution during his term. The bill provided a lump sum of 30% of salary to non-civil servant former senior officials and 14% to former civil servants, along with 70% of salary as compensation for suspension of duties. On May 28, more than 7,000 protesters surrounded the Legislative Assembly, lawmakers canceled the final vote, and on May 29 morning, Chief Executive Chui Sai On (崔世安) announced a suspension of the bill at a special press conference (Macau Business 2019).

Data record: No separate Macau entries in Mass Mobilization or ACLED (data/MAC.json blank). Outside the NAVCO campaign scope (continuous mass campaign).

    2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Dzungar (Oirat) uprising — Amursana Qing Dynasty (Dzungar Campaign) · 1755–1757

    A U.S. Congressional Research Service study notes that serious rebellions against Qing rule broke out in Xinjiang in 1755–1757, which the Qing army quickly suppressed, strengthening its control over western Mongolia and Oirat areas. In 1771, the Qing government persuaded some Kalmyks who had gone to Russia to resettle in this devastated area. The Great Soviet Encyclopedia entry on Chinggis Khan notes that this uprising was led by the Oirat prince Amursana and describes how Qing repression intensified thereafter.

    1. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Chinese Control' section (edited by Robert L. Worden & Andrea Matles Savada, 1989) · Government report · Orientation: Library of Congress Federal Research Division country study (Cold War-era U.S. government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/25.htm
    2. Great Soviet Encyclopedia (3rd ed., 1979), 'Chingunjav' entry · Encyclopedia (academic) · Orientation: Soviet official encyclopedia — anti-Qing national liberation narrative, possible ideological bias (publication source also specified) — https://encyclopedia2.thefreedictionary.com/Chingunjav

    Chingunjav's Khalkha Anti-Qing Uprising Qing Dynasty · 1756–1757

    According to the Great Soviet Encyclopedia, Chingunjav was a noyon (feudal lord) of Khalkha who commanded a Mongol force of 2,000 men in the Qing army based in the Dzungar region. As the Qing fought a long war with the Dzungars and intensified political and economic pressure, and as repression increased following Amursana's uprising, the immediate trigger was the execution in Beijing of Erinchindorji, a Mongol general and brother of the Bogd Gegeen (the head of Mongolian Lamaism). In July 1756, Chingunjav returned to his native pastoral lands near Lake Hobsugul, urged Mongol lords to rise up, and contacted Amursana. The Qing redirected Dzungar forces to Khalkha to suppress the rebels, and Chingunjav, captured in January 1757, was transported to Beijing with his sons and executed (the same entry gives his date of death as June 12, 1757).

    1. Great Soviet Encyclopedia (3rd ed., 1979), 'Chingunjav' entry (Reference: Zlatkin 1964; Ishzhamts 1962) · Encyclopedia (academic) · Orientation: Soviet official encyclopedia — anti-Qing national liberation narrative, possible ideological bias (publication source also specified) — https://encyclopedia2.thefreedictionary.com/Chingunjav

    5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

    The 1911 Declaration of Independence and the Establishment of the Bogd Khaanate Regime Overthrow · 1911–1915

    In late Qing-era Hulunbuir, anti-Qing incidents such as uprisings and mutinies by Mongolian soldiers became frequent. On December 1, 1911, Outer Mongolia declared its practical independence, stating that its object of loyalty was not China but the Manchu imperial court; on December 28, Jebtsundamba Khutuktu ascended as Bogd Khaan, and a military force of 20,000 was established. The Mongol–Russian Agreement was signed on November 3, 1912, and the Mongol–Tibetan Agreement on December 19, 1912; autonomy under Chinese suzerainty was formalized by the Khyaty (Khalkha) Treaty of May 25, 1915 (U.S. Congressional Research Service).

    ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigation · Other
    CasualtiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (the country study does not provide figures)

    Data record: The 1911 Mongolian independence event is not recorded in COW civil war, UCDP (after 1946), Powell & Thyne (after 1950), or NAVCO (campaigns after 1900) datasets (data/MNG.json). Archigos also has records only from 1921 onward for Mongolian leaders.

    1. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Period of Autonomy, 1911-21' · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service country study (foreign government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/26.htm
    2. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Chinese Control' · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service country study (foreign government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/25.htm

    The 1921 People's Revolution and Early Purges (Execution of Böröd and Dandzan) Regime Overthrow · 1920–1924

    The Mongolian People's Party was founded at the Irkutsk Conference in March 1920, and Sukhbaatar's Mongol partisan force was established in February 1921. In the same month, Ungern-Sternberg's White Army drove Chinese forces from Niislel Horoo (Ulaan Üüd), and in early July the Mongol–Soviet allied forces expelled the Whites. On July 11, 1921, the Bogd Khaanate government was replaced by a People's Government with Jebtsundamba Khutuktu as nominal monarch. In August 1922, Premier Böröd and approximately 40 others were arrested; Böröd and 14 others were executed. In August 4–24, 1924, during the Third Party Congress, party leader Dandzan was arrested and executed. Jebtsundamba Khutuktu died on May 20, 1924, and on November 25 the First State Great Khural adopted a Soviet-style constitution, establishing the Mongolian People's Republic (U.S. Congressional Research Service).

    ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigation · Other
    Execution of Böröd in 1922UnconfirmedBöröd and 14 others executed among approximately 40 arrested (U.S. Congressional Research Service)

    Data record: Archigos 4.1: Dandzan (Danzin) took office normally on 1923-02-23 → ousted 'without foreign support by other government actors' on 1924-08-26, recorded as an abnormal departure with the end 'death'. The country study only notes that the execution occurred during the congress period (August 4–24), differing by two days from Archigos's end date (August 26). The 1921 revolution itself is not recorded in COW civil war or coup data sets.

    1. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Period of Autonomy, 1911-21' · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service country study (foreign government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/26.htm
    2. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Revolutionary Transformation, 1921-24' · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (foreign government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/27.htm

    1932 armed uprising (Khövsgöl uprising) Uprising · 1929–1932

    After the Seventh Party Congress at the end of 1928, the left-wing line took control; in 1929 more than 600 feudal estates were confiscated, and in 1931–1932 the property of more than 800 religious and secular leaders was seized, and more than 700 household heads were killed or imprisoned. By 1931 more than one-third of herding households had been forcibly collectivized, and herdsmen slaughtered approximately 7 million livestock over three years, while several thousand people suffered severe food shortages in 1931–1932. In western Mongolia an anti-communist uprising broke out in 1932 and was suppressed by government forces with Soviet troops and tanks. In May 1932, following instructions from the Comintern and the Communist Party of the Soviet Union, the June Central Committee meeting characterized the previous line as a 'leftist deviation' and rescinded collective farms (U.S. Library of Congress Country Study). Kuzmin and Oyuunchimeg (2015) regard this uprising as the largest rebellion in 20th-century Mongolia; they attribute its cause to the 'leftist policies' of the Mongolian People's Revolutionary Party rather than to the Panchen Lama or Japan, and suggest that it could be regarded as a civil war.

    ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
    Uprising fatalitiesUnconfirmed (original texts of official Mongolian and Soviet government tallies not consulted)Unconfirmed (neither the Kuzmin & Oyuunchimeg 2015 abstract nor the Country Study contains figures)
    Preceding repression (1931–32)UnconfirmedProperty seized from more than 800 people, more than 700 household heads killed or imprisoned (U.S. Library of Congress Country Study)
    Livestock slaughtered (over three years)UnconfirmedApproximately 7 million (U.S. Library of Congress Country Study)

    Data record: COW Civil War v5.1: the 'Mongolia' entry in data/MNG.json has cow_state 'Matanza War of 1932' (El Salvador government vs. left, combat fatalities 2,600, war_num 712), so the El Salvador event is linked to Mongolia rather than the 1932 Mongolian armed uprising. The UCDP, Powell & Thyne, and NAVCO databases contain no entry for the 1932 Mongolian uprising.

    1. Library of Congress Country Studies, 'Mongolia — Purges, Collectivization, and the 1932 Reversal' · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (foreign government perspective) — https://countrystudies.us/mongolia/29.htm
    2. S. Kuzmin & J. Oyuunchimeg, 'The Khövsgöl Rebellion of 1932 in Mongolia', Vostok (Oriens) 2015 No.3, pp.47–62 · Journal article · Orientation: Russian Academy of Sciences journal for East Asian studies, Russian and Mongolian scholars — based on Comintern and Soviet documents — https://vostokoriens.ru/en/articles?artid=2733

    1989–1990 democratization movement Protest · 1989–1990

    On December 10, 1989, approximately 200 people held the first public democratization protest in Sükhbaatar Square, and the Mongolian Democratic Union (MDU) was subsequently officially established. Protests continued on December 17 with 2,000 participants and on January 14, 1990, with 1,000 people gathered outside the Lenin Museum. On March 7, 10 MDU members wearing traditional Mongolian dress, who had been banned from doing so, began a hunger strike in Sükhbaatar Square, and on March 9 the Politburo announced its resignation. On May 10 parliament declared the holding of free multiparty elections, and in July the People's Revolutionary Party won nearly three-quarters of the seats. During the hunger strike period, one person died at the hands of the crowd, and property damage and vehicle thefts are recorded (Swarthmore Nonviolent Action Database).

    ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
    Scale of participationUnconfirmedApproximately 200 on 1989.12.10 → 2,000 on 12.17 → more than 40,000 by late April 1990 (Swarthmore GNAD)
    DeathsUnconfirmed1 (death at the hands of the crowd during the hunger strike period, Swarthmore GNAD)

    Data record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Mongolian anti-communist' 1989–1990, target communist regime, goal regime change, nonviolent, successful — consistent with the narrative. Mass Mobilization v5.1: 12 protests in 1990. This movement is classified as nonviolent, but GNAD records 1 death.

    1. Global Nonviolent Action Database (Swarthmore College), 'Mongolians win multi-party democracy, 1989-1990' (Goldberg 2009; Rennebohm revised 2011) · Academic database · Orientation: nonviolent resistance research database — greater weight to the perspective of civil resistance — https://nvdatabase.swarthmore.edu/content/mongolians-win-multi-party-democracy-1989-1990

    July 1 election fraud protest crisis of 2008 Protest · 2008

    Following the June 29, 2008 general election, widespread allegations of fraud emerged, and on July 1, protests in Ulaanbaatar escalated into violent unrest. Protesters set fire to the headquarters of the Mongolian People's Revolutionary Party and looted commercial offices. Police used tear gas, rubber bullets, and live ammunition (citing local reports). On July 2, a four-day state of emergency—the first in Mongolia—was declared in Ulaanbaatar. Five people died, hundreds were injured including police officers, and ten police officers arrested on suspicion of planned murder were released on bail. The government paid 1 million tugriks to each victim's family and 100,000 tugriks from other state funds (Amnesty International 2009). Amnesty noted in 2010 that no police officer responsible for excessive use of firearms had been held accountable.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    DeathsUnconfirmed (original Mongolian government announcement not consulted)5 — 4 shot, 1 presumed to have died from smoke inhalation (Amnesty International 2009·2010)
    InjuriesUnconfirmedHundreds including police officers (Amnesty International 2009)
    Police ProsecutionsTen police officers arrested and charged with planned murder (measures reported by Amnesty)No convictions by 2010 (Amnesty International 2010)

    Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1: of 4 protests in 2008, 3 involved protester violence. Outside the UCDP and COW criteria (organized armed actors). ACLED records for Mongolia begin in 2018 and are therefore excluded.

    1.     2. Amnesty International, 'Mongolia: Submission to the UN Human Rights Committee' (ASA 30/005/2010, 2010.4.30) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: international human rights organization — https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/ASA30/005/2010/en/

    2022 Anti-Corruption Protest over Coal Exports Protest · 2022

    In November 2022, the Anti-Corruption Agency announced that it was investigating more than 30 officials, including the CEO of state-owned coal company Erdenet Tavan Tolgoi, on embezzlement charges. Following reports of $12.8 billion in embezzled state coal revenue, protests began in Ulaanbaatar on December 4 (according to the US Embassy, several hundred participants), and on December 5 thousands gathered at Sükhbaatar Square in temperatures of −21°C. Some protesters attempted to enter government buildings, toppling barriers and breaking windows; police dispersed them at 9 p.m. (Al Jazeera 2022). The government declassified operational documents of state-owned coal enterprises but refused to disclose a list of implicated officials (International IDEA).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

    Scale of Embezzlement AllegationsMore than 30 under investigation (Anti-Corruption Agency, Mongolia)$12.8 billion (figure reported by International IDEA)

    Data Record: ACLED: 129 protests in 2022, political violence and deaths = 0 (data/MNG.json). Mass Mobilization records only up to 2020.

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Rebellions

    Dairō no Masakado's Rebellion (平将門の乱) Ancient (Heian) · 935–940

    Masakado, a local lord of the Kantō (坂東), fought repeatedly with his clan from around 935 (Shōhei 5) (『改訂新版 世界大百科事典』; 『山川 日本史小辞典』 dates it from 931), and in 939 (Tenchō 2) he attacked the provincial office of Hitachi Province, capturing its governor, Fujiwara no Korechika (accounts differ on the month of the attack, giving either November or December). He then subjugated the provincial offices of Shimotsuke, Gozuke, Sagami, and others; styling himself 'New Emperor' (新皇), he appointed his brothers governors of various Kantō provinces. In February 940 he was attacked by an army led by Fujiwara no Hidesato of Shimotsuke and Dairō no Sadamori and died in battle in the Sashima District of Shimosa. It is described together with the rebellion of Fujiwara no Sumitomo in the west as 'the Uprising of Chōkai and Denjō'.

    1. The Rebellion of Taira Masakado - Kotonban (Revised Edition, World Encyclopedia; Yamakawa Japanese History Dictionary) · Encyclopedia · Tone: Differences in chronology between dictionary entries — https://kotobank.jp/word/平将門の乱

    Shōchō's Tsuchi-ikki (正長の土一揆) Middle Ages (Muromachi) · 1428

    In August 1428 (Shōchō 1), transporters (bashaku) in Ōmi (present Shiga Prefecture) rebelled, demanding the cancellation of debts (tokusei) and attacking sakaya and dozo (sake brewers and warehouse keepers); the uprising spread to Kyoto, Nara, and the Kinai region. (The Nihon Daihyakka Jiten describes it as extending to Iga, Ise, Yamato, Kii, Izumi, Kawachi, Tango, Settsu, and Harima.) The bakufu rejected the demand for tokusei, but the rebels recovered loan contracts and collateral by force, a practice known as sētokusei (private tokusei). In Ōmi, the shugo Rokkaku clan issued a tokusei order in September, and in November, Kōfukuji issued one in Nara. A monument inscribed with debt cancellations for four villages of Gane is still standing on a roadside rock in Yagyū, Nara Prefecture.

    1. 正長の土一揆 - Kotonban (Nihon Daihyakka Jiten, Revised Edition; World Encyclopedia, Revised Edition; Digital Daijisen, etc.) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/正長の土一揆

    Yamashiro Kuni-ikki (山城の国一揆) Middle Ages (Warring States period) · 1485–1493

    From October 1485 (Kansei 17), when the two armies of Hatakeyama Yoshinari and Masanaga faced each other across the Uji River in southern Yamashiro, a kuni-ikki centered on kokujin (local samurai) demanded that both armies leave the country, and it was settled in December. The kuni-ikki established rules by agreement, exercised its own judgment and policing, and levied hanzai (half of the annual tribute), exercising autonomous rule for approximately eight years (『精選版 日本国語大辞典』). In 1493 (Meiō 2), when the kuni-ikki permitted the new shugo Ise Sadamichi to enter, autonomy came to an end (『山川 日本史小辞典』).

    1. Yamashiro Kuni-Ikki - Kotonban (精選版 日本国語大辞典・山川 日本史小辞典) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/山城の国一揆

    Kaga Ikkō-Ikki (加賀の一向一揆) Middle Ages (Warring States period) · 1488–1580

    In 1488 (Chōkyō 2), followers of the Jōdo Shinshū (Ikkō-shū) and kokujin forces destroyed Kaga no kuni's shugo, Togashi Masachika, and for the next 90 years they governed and operated Kaga no koku (『デジタル大辞泉』). In 1580 (Tenshō 8), the Oda army captured the stronghold of Ōyama-Gobo, and after Hongan-ji submitted to and reconciled with Oda Nobunaga, the ikki was annihilated (『山川 日本史小辞典』).

    1. 加賀の一向一揆 - コトバンク(デジタル大辞泉・山川 日本史小辞典) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/加賀の一向一揆

    Shimabara Rebellion (島原の乱) Early Modern Period (Edo period) · 1637–1638

    In October 1637 (Kan'ei 14), Shimabara and Amakusa peasants rose against the harsh annual tribute levies of Shimabara daimyō Matsukura Katsumasa and the shogunate's persecution of Christians (some dictionaries date the outbreak to October 25, others to a November clash between samurai and peasants in Arima village). The uprising forces, led by Amakusa Shiro Tokisada (numbering approximately 30,000, or 38,000 according to some dictionaries), besieged Hara Castle, which had been abandoned. The shogunate mobilized approximately 120,000 troops (some dictionaries state 'several tens of thousands', 124,000) and, in late February 1638, captured the castle with a general assault on February 27–28 or 28–29, killing most of the besieged except for the traitors. The shogunate's forces are recorded to have suffered approximately 2,000 dead and more than 10,000 wounded (『改訂新版 世界大百科事典』).

    1. 島原の乱 - コトバンク(共同通信ニュース用語解説・改訂新版 世界大百科事典・山川 日本史小辞典・旺文社日本史事典など) · Encyclopedia · Tone: most primary sources are records of the suppression side (shogunate and daimyo) — https://kotobank.jp/word/島原の乱

    Battle of Shakushain (シャクシャインの戦い) Early Modern Period (early Edo period) · 1669

    In 1669 (Kanbun 9), Ainu leader Shakushain rallied Ainu from eastern and western Ezochi to rise in rebellion. The background included the Matsushima Domain's monopoly of trade, which reduced the rice exchanged for five kinds of cod (100 fish) from 2 koku to 7–8 koku, environmental degradation of rivers caused by the domain's gold mining, and rumors that another leader, Onibishi, had been poisoned by the domain (『日本大百科全書』). According to the 『津軽一統志』, approximately 390 Japanese, including hunters and ship crewmen, were killed. Shakushain was killed at a peace conference in the same year. Most of the sources are records from the wajin (Japanese) side.

    1. シャクシャインの戦い - コトバンク(日本大百科全書・デジタル大辞泉) · Encyclopedia · Tone: most primary sources are records of the wajin (Matsushima Domain and Tsugaru Domain) side — https://kotobank.jp/word/シャクシャインの戦い

    3. 1760–1815 — Literature

    Denmei's Uchikowashi (天明の打ちこわし) 1787

    The Denmei famine (1782–87, centered on Ōu and Kantō; over 900,000 dead of starvation or disease) drove up rice prices, leading to a series of uchikowashi in which crowds attacked rice hoarders and wealthy merchants (bukō) in Osaka in 1787 (Tenmei 7) and subsequently in Kyoto, Ōtsu, Nara, Nagasaki, Edo, and elsewhere, destroying their homes and property (『旺文社日本史事典』). In Edo, violent uchikowashi broke out across the machi (towns) in May 1787. The event is described as having had political repercussions for the bakufu's political situation immediately prior to the establishment of the Matsudaira Sadanobu administration following the fall of the Danuma administration, and as a direct trigger for the implementation of the Kansei reforms, including the establishment of machikaisho in Edo (『世界大百科事典』).

    1. Uchikowashi - Kotonaru (World Encyclopedia · Ōbunsha Nihon Shigaku Jiten · Hyakka Nii Pedia) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/%E6%89%93%E3%81%A1%E3%81%93%E3%82%8F%E3%81%97-618920
    2. Tenmei Famine - Kotonaru · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/天明の飢饉

    Kunashiri and Menashi Ainu Uprising 1789

    In 1789 (Kansei 1), the Ainu of Kunashiri Island and the opposite Menashi region rose up. The uprising is described as having been caused by harsh labor conditions under the kajika (fishery) system operated by Hidaya Kibe, who managed the kajika on a commission basis, and by the 'tyranny' of dispatched inspectors who disregarded custom. 71 people were killed, including managers, inspectors, workers, crew members of Hidaya vessels, and officials of the Matsumae clan; subsequently, 37 Ainu were executed for the killings at Wajin (『山川 日本史小辞典』).

    1. Kunashiri・Menashi Uprising - Kotonaru (Yamakawa Nihon Shigaku Jiten Revised Edition) · Encyclopedia · Tone: source record is from the Japanese side — https://kotobank.jp/word/クナシリ・メナシの蜂起

    5. Modern Events in the Literature

    Oshio Heihachirō's Rebellion (大塩平八郎の乱) Uprising · 1837

    Amid rising rice prices and widespread starvation following the Denpo famine (the great crop failure of 1836), Oshio Heihachirō rebelled in Osaka on February 19, 1837 (Kōka 8), motivated by dissatisfaction with the inadequate response of the Osaka Machibugyō, the rice shipments to Edo, and the actions of the wealthy merchants. Fighting took place in areas such as Senba and Tenma within the city, and a fire destroyed one-fifth of the Osaka market (『日本大百科全書』; 『ブリタニカ』 records the destruction of 10,000 dwellings). Oshio committed suicide in March after his hideout was discovered approximately 40 days later.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    Scale of DestructionUnconfirmedOne-fifth of the Osaka market (日本大百科全書); 10,000 dwellings (ブリタニカ国際大百科事典)

    Data Record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or Archigos (recorded after 1816 but not under civil war criteria).

    1. Oshio Heihachirō's Rebellion - Kotonban (日本大百科全書・ブリタニカ国際大百科事典) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/大塩平八郎の乱

    Boshin War (戊辰戦争) Civil War · 1868–1869

    The war between the new government forces and the former shogunate began on January 3, 1868 (Kōō 4), with the Battle of Toba–Fushimi. Edo Castle was bloodlessly occupied by the new government forces on April 11, and the Aizu garrison, which had held out for about one month, fell on September 15. The war ended on May 18, 1869 (Meiji 2), when Hakodate Goryokaku surrendered. None of the consulted dictionary entries provides a total casualty figure.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    Battle DeathsUnconfirmed9,000 (COW Intra-State Wars v5.1, 'Meiji Restoration of 1868-1869', war_num 588)

    Data Record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1: 'Meiji Restoration of 1868-1869', Japan vs Tokugawa Bafuku, total battle deaths 9,000, outcome code 1 (this pipeline data/JPN.json).

    1. 戊辰戦争 - コトバンク(精選版 日本国語大辞典・改訂新版 世界大百科事典・ブリタニカ) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/戊辰戦争
    2. COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/JPN.json) · Public DB — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

    Seinan War (西南戦争) Civil War · 1877

    On February 15, 1877 (Meiji 10), approximately 13,000 men loyal to Saigō Takamori departed from Kagoshima, and on February 22 they attacked Kumamoto Castle, beginning a siege (the castle held out under siege under Commander-in-Chief Tani Tateki). In the Battle of Daburazaka from March 4, the Saigō forces were defeated, and the war ended on September 24 when Saigō committed suicide during a government general offensive at Shiroyama. Government forces numbered approximately 58,000, while the total strength of the Saigō forces is recorded differently in each dictionary as between 30,000 and 40,000.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    Government forces killed6,527 dead and wounded (total casualties 16,195; Ministry of the Army Third Annual Report; cited in revised edition of Nihon Kokugo Daijiten)Approximately 6,800 dead (Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan); approximately 15,000 casualties (Shinjiryo Nihon Kokushi Jiten)
    Saigō forces killedUnconfirmedApproximately 6,000 dead (Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan); approximately 15,000 casualties (Shinjiryo Nihon Kokushi Jiten)

    1. Southwestern War - Kotonban (Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan, revised edition Shinjiryo Nihon Kokushi Jiten, Yamakawa Nihonshi Jiten, Biko Jiten MyePedia) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Government figures based on the Army Ministry annual report — https://kotobank.jp/word/西南戦争
    2. COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 (pipeline collection ui_book/data/JPN.json) · Public DB — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

    Chichibu Incident (秩父事件) Uprising · 1884

    Farmers in Chichibu, Saitama Prefecture, burdened by the kōri tax, formed the Chichibu Kōmin Party (led by Prime Minister Saigō Tsugumichi) in August 1884 and demanded a 10-year grace period and 40-year installment repayment on debts, as well as reductions in the kōri tax, village expenses, and school fees. Some rose first on October 31, and several thousand armed farmers who had gathered at Muku Shrine on November 1 occupied Omiya Station on November 2, briefly controlling all of Chichibu. The gendarmerie and Tokyo Imperial Guard troops intervened and suppressed the uprising around November 4–5. Accounts of the scale of punishment differ across sources, and seven people, including Saigō, are recorded as having been sentenced to death.

    ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigations and other sources
    Scale of participationUnconfirmedReported to have exceeded 10,000 (Kokugo Daijiten Seisei-hen); 3,000 who entered Omiya Station (Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan)
    Punishment296 serious offenses (including 7 death sentences), 448 minor offenses, and 2,642 fines (Kokugo Daijiten Seisei-hen); 3,386 convictions (Britannica)More than 4,500 punished (revised edition Shinjiryo Nihon Kokushi Jiten); more than 6,400 defendants (Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan)

    Data record: Not included in systematic datasets such as COW or Archigos (undercounted by civil war criteria).

    1. Chichibu Incident - Kotonban (Kokugo Daijiten Seisei-hen, revised edition Shinjiryo Nihon Kokushi Jiten, Nippon Kokuritsu Kōkogaku Hakubutsukan, Britannica, etc.) · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Punishment figures based on judicial records; differ across sources — https://kotobank.jp/word/秩父事件

    Rice Riots (米騒動) Uprising · 1918

    On July 23, 1918, in Ōzu, Toyama Prefecture, wives of fishermen gathered on the coast to prevent the outflow of rice; on August 3 in Nishimizuhashi, women rushed to rice brokers after which disturbances spread nationwide. They occurred on August 10 in Kyoto and Nagoya and on August 13 in Tokyo, among 40 districts and 30 villages across 18 prefectures. The extent of the spread is recorded as '38 cities, 153 districts, and 177 villages, excluding Aomori, Iwate, Akita, and Okinawa' (Shihō Daihyakka Jiten) or '1 do, 3 fu, 38 ken, 368 locations' (Nihon Daihyakka Jiten). Troops totaling more than 90,000 to 100,000 were dispatched to 120 locations. The Derauchi cabinet resigned en masse on September 21, and the Hara Takashi cabinet took office on September 29.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
    ParticipantsUnconfirmedEstimated at approximately 700,000 (Britannica · Ōbunsha); millions (Nihon Kokugo Daijiten)
    Arrests and IndictmentsAt least 25,000 arrests, 8,253 prosecution decisions, 7,786 indictments (Revised Edition World Encyclopedia, judicial disposition tally)7,776 indictments (Encyclopedia Mypedia)
    Casualties on the popular sideUnconfirmed30 or more (revised and enlarged edition of the World Encyclopedia; 山川 is approximately 30); 13 killed in Ube (Japan Encyclopedic Dictionary)

    Data record: Not included in COW or Archigos. Archigos does not record Derauchi's departure as an irregular transition (no Derauchi entry in the pipeline collection).

    1. Kita-ōdo (米騒動) - Kotonban (revised and enlarged edition of the World Encyclopedia, Japan Encyclopedic Dictionary, MyPedia, Britannica, Ōbunsha Nihon Shigaku Jiten) · encyclopedia · orientation: scale figures differ substantially across dictionaries — https://kotobank.jp/word/米騒動

    May 15 Incident (五・一五事件) Coup · 1932

    On May 15, 1932, a group of radical young naval officers was joined by military cadets and the Iikubo faction of Kozaburo Ichikubo to attack the Prime Minister's residence, the residence of the Grand Chamberlain (Makino Nobuaki), the Mitsubishi Bank, the Yokohama Specie Bank, the Kōdōkai headquarters, and the Metropolitan Police Department; an Iikubo detachment attacked a substation near Tokyo. Prime Minister Tsuyoshi Inukai was killed. A national unity cabinet headed by Makoto Saito subsequently formed, bringing the era of party cabinets to an end. At the military tribunal, naval defendants were sentenced to 10–15 years' imprisonment, all army defendants to 4 years, and civilian defendants to life imprisonment for Ichikubo and 3 years and 6 months to 15 years for others (ブリタニカ).

    Data record: Archigos 4.1: Inukai 1932-05-15 'Ousted by rebel forces without foreign support', deceased. Powell & Thyne coup data cover only events after 1950 and fall outside the scope of inclusion.

    1. May 15 Incident - Kotonban (Nihon Daihyakka Jiten, Britannica, Shōgakukan Kokushi Daihyakka Jiten, Digital Daijisen, Ōbunsha) · Encyclopedia — https://kotobank.jp/word/五・一五事件

    February 26 Incident (二・二六事件) Coup · 1936

    On February 26, 1936, young officers of the Army faction loyal to the Emperor rose up with approximately 1,400–1,500 troops (the Shōgakkō Kokushi states 1,473) and killed Grand Minister Takahashi Korekiyo, Imperial Household Minister Saitō Makoto, and Education General Inspector Watanabe Jōtarō, wounding Chief Chamberlain Suzuki Kantarō seriously. Prime Minister Okada Kiyosuke survived, while his uncle-in-law, Dendojō Matsuo, was killed after being mistaken for the prime minister. A state of martial law was proclaimed in Tokyo on February 27 and suppressed by February 29. The special Tokyo Army Military Tribunal sentenced 17 defendants to death on July 5 and executed 15 of them on July 12 (Shōgakkō Kokushi; other dictionaries describe the execution of '19 ringleaders').

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Death Sentences17 sentenced to death, 15 executed on July 12 (改訂新版 世界大百科事典 · 日本大百科全書)19 ringleaders executed (共同通信ニュース用語解説 · Britannica; whether the ruling in a separate case is included is unconfirmed)

    Data Record: Archigos 4.1 contains no entry for an irregular departure of Prime Minister Okada (the cabinet resigned en masse after the incident). Powell & Thyne record only events from 1950 onward, falling outside the scope. No COW civil war entry.

    1. The February 26 Incident - Kotonban (Nihon Daihyakka Jiten · Shōgakkō Kokushi · Kyodo News · Britannica · Digital Daijisen) · Encyclopedia · Tone: Discrepancies between sources on the number of executions — https://kotobank.jp/word/二・二六事件

    1960 Security Treaty Struggle (安保闘争) Protests · 1960

    Opposition to the new Japan–US Security Treaty expanded when, following the Diet's Security Special Committee on May 19, it was concluded at a late-night plenary session on the same day without the opposition or non-mainstream factions of the Liberal Democratic Party. On June 15, during an attempt by mainstream Zengokuren members to enter the Diet building, clashes with riot police inside the Diet compound resulted in the death of Michiko Kamba, a University of Tokyo student. On June 16, the Cabinet Council decided to postpone US President Eisenhower's visit to Japan. On June 18, approximately 330,000 people participated in a protest surrounding the Diet building (日本大百科全書), and on June 19 the treaty was approved by default. Prime Minister Kishi resigned on the day the treaty took effect, June 23, and the cabinet resigned en masse in July.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Participation in the Diet building encirclement protest (June 18)Police estimate unconfirmed330,000 (日本大百科全書); over 300,000 around June 19 (百科事典マイペディア)
    Deaths1 (Michiko Kamba, June 15)1 (same)

    Data Record: NAVCO 1.3: 'Anti-Kishi Campaign' 1960, nonviolent, partial success. Archigos treats Kishi's departure as a regular turnover (not listed in the irregular list).

    2. Packard, G. R. (1966). Protest in Tokyo. Princeton University Press (De Gruyter Brill bibliography) · Monograph · Perspective: US researcher's viewpoint — https://www.degruyterbrill.com/document/doi/10.1515/9781400878673/html
