    2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

    Miao Qing's Rebellion (Western Capital Rebellion, Great Wei State) Middle Ages (Goryeo) · 1135–1136

    Following the political turmoil after Yi Jahyeon's rebellion, the Western Capital (present-day Pyongyang) faction claimed a relocation of the capital from Gaegyeong to the Western Capital, the assumption of imperial titles, and the conquest of Jin. When Injong kept them at a distance through the opposition of Gaebyeok and other Gaegyeong aristocratic factions, Miao Qing and others rebelled in 1135 (Injong 13) from the Western Capital, imprisoned officials dispatched from Gaegyeong, severed the Jeongeun Pass, and declared their state name Great Wei, their era name Cheonggye, and their army the Cheonggyeon Chongui Army. The court appointed Gaebyeok as Commander to Pacify the West, and Miao Qing was killed by his subordinates after being betrayed by Jo Gwang. The Western Capital fell the following year (1136).

    1. Miao Qing's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Perspective: author's theory (not institutional official position); original source 『Goryeosa』 compiled from the perspective of the suppressing side — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0018893
    2. Great Wei State - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0014651

    Jo Wijong's Rebellion Middle Ages (Goryeo, military rule) · 1174–1176

    Following the uprising of Gwon Bok-dang in 1173, as awareness of crisis under military rule grew, Jo Wijong, Governor-General of the Western Capital, rose up in 1174 (Mingjong 4) with the slogan of eliminating the ruling military ministers Jeong Jungbu and Lee Eehyang, dispatched missives to the Eastern and Northern Provinces from the Western Capital, and received support from more than forty fortresses north of Jibiyeong except Yeongju. The government forces led by Yun In-cheon suffered a crushing defeat at Jeongeun-yeok amid storms and heavy snowfall; in retaliation, Lee Eehyang pushed the rebels back to the Daedong River. In June 1175, Yun In-cheon besieged the Western Capital and fought a protracted battle; Jo Wijong's request for Jin reinforcements was rejected. In June 1176, government forces captured the Western Capital and executed Jo Wijong. Uprisings continued in the northwest in 1177 and 1179.

    1. Jo Wijong's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Perspective: discrepancies between editions in period descriptions (about one year / 22 months) — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0052464

    Lee Sa-ae's Rebellion Early Joseon · 1467

    Hamgyeong Province had appointed local clan leaders as officials on account of its geographical peculiarities, but under King Sejo's centralizing policies, the dispatch of officials from the center and the tightening of the Ho Paebok system increased discontent among the northern clans. In May 1467 (Sejo 13), Lee Sa-ae, a native of Gyeongju, conspired with his younger brother Lee Sa-hab and his father-in-law Lee Myeonghyo to kill Hamgyeong Province Governor-General Kang Hyo-mun and rebel from Gyeongju, claiming that it was 'not rebellion but a righteous cause.' After passing through Dancheon, Bukcheong, and Hongwon, they seized Hamhung; in Hamhung, a faction mobilizing yuhaengso killed Governor-General Sin Myeon. The court appointed Jo Jun of Joong-gun as Commander-in-Chief of the Army and Navy, and government forces defeated the rebels in the battles of Bukcheong and Manryeong. Lee Sa-ae was arrested by Ho Seoryeo's trickery and executed on August 12. Thereafter, yuhaengso in the northern provinces were abolished, Hamgyeong Province was divided into left and right provinces, and Gyeongju was demoted to Gyeongsung County.

    1. Lee Sa-ae's Rebellion - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Perspective: original source is a record from the court (the suppressing side) — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0044984
    2. Lee Sa-ae - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0044983

    3. 1760–1815 — Literature

    Hong Gyeong-rae's Rebellion (Pyongan Province) 1811–1812

    Hong Gyeong-nae prepared for over ten years from his base at Gasa Daebo-dong before rising up in 1811 (Seonjo 11) when severe famine made public sentiment turbulent. He also reached out to the class of yangban who had accumulated wealth in rural areas and become part of the lower ruling stratum, as well as to the saenggajin (private merchants) of Hwanghae and P'yongan provinces. In the early stages, with the help of turncoat forces, he occupied seven counties including Gasa and Gwaksan, but after suffering defeats at Bokcheon and Songnim against government troops, he retreated to Jeongju Fortress. The rebels held out for four months in Jeongju Fortress but on April 19 (lunar date) of the following year, the fortress fell, Hong Gyeong-nae was shot and killed, and the uprising ended. This event is also recorded in the Korea (KOR) volume, and its location lies within present-day North Pyongan Province, North Korea.

    1. Hong Gyeong-rae - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0063945
    2. Jeongju Army - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia — http://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Contents/Item/E0050904
    3. Jeongju Fortress - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0050908

    5. Modern Events in the Literature

    Sinuiju Anti-Communist Student Uprising (Sinuiju Student Incident) Protest · 1945

    On November 16, 1945, at a Christian Social Party local conference held in Yongampo, the speaker demanded the reopening of the closed Sansan School and criticized an official from the communist side. Students supporting him were attacked by Soviet troops and communist forces, resulting in the killing of one church elder and serious injuries to 12 students and civilians. On November 23, students from six boys' and girls' middle schools in Sinuiju gathered to resolve on anti-communism and at 2 p.m. attacked three branches of the People's Committee for North Pyongan security department, the North Pyongan Communist Party headquarters, and the Sinuiju Security Bureau. In front of the security department, machine-gun fire killed 13 people and wounded hundreds, and the unit attacking the party headquarters also suffered 10 deaths. The entry in the Korean National Culture Encyclopedia defines this as an anti-Soviet and anti-Kim Il-sung protest, recording 23 student deaths and approximately 700 wounded, with approximately 2,000 arrested and imprisoned. No official North Korean account could be verified.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
    DeathsUnconfirmed (announcements by North Korean and Soviet authorities could not be verified)23 students (Korean National Culture Encyclopedia, written by Yoon Sang-hyun · revised 2023)
    WoundedUnconfirmedApproximately 700 (同)
    Arrested and ImprisonedUnconfirmedApproximately 2,000 (同)

    Data Record: Not included in UCDP, COW, NAVCO, or MM (1990 onwards) (Soviet military administration period prior to state establishment).

    1. Sinuiju Anti-Communist Student Uprising - Korean National Culture Encyclopedia · Encyclopedia · Orientation: Author's theory (not the institutional official position); anti-communist narrative of the South Korean side; North Korean account unverified — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0033275

    Korean War (June 25 War) Civil War · 1950–1953

    A war that began on June 25, 1950, and ended with the armistice agreement of July 27, 1953, in which UN forces and Chinese forces intervened in armed clashes between the governments of North and South Korea. Estimates of human casualties vary widely by reporting body; the Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture synthesizes multiple sources to describe the combined loss of Korean lives at around 5.2 million. Although not an uprising or an internal resistance event, it is recorded because it is the only armed conflict concerning North Korea included in the UCDP.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    South Korean Military Losses (Killed, Wounded, Missing)Approximately 257,000 (UN forces' announcement); approximately 586,000 (Communist forces' announcement)988,403 (estimated by the Japanese newspaper Chōitsu Joseon Sinmun)
    Total North and South Korean Human CasualtiesUnconfirmedAround the 5.2 million line (Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture synthesis)

    Data Record: UCDP/PRIO v26.1: conflict_id 235, North Korean government vs. South Korean government, interstate and territorial conflict, 1949–1953; in the 5 active years, over 1,000 war deaths in 4 years. Not included in the COW civil war list.

    1. Korean War - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia · Tone: South Korean account ('illegal invasion by North Korean forces' defined); sources of figures (UN forces, Communist forces, Japanese newspaper) each have a partisan bias. The North Korean side has formulated an alternative account of the outbreak, but the original text was not verified in this round — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Article/E0042143

    August Plenary Meeting Incident (August Sectarian Incident) Regime Overthrow · 1956

    At the August plenary meeting of the Central Committee of the Workers' Party of Korea held on August 30, 1956, Choi Chang-ik, Pak Chang-ok, and Yun Gong-heum criticized Kim Il-sung's line. The meeting adopted a resolution 'On the Sectarian Plot of Comrades Choi Chang-ik, Yun Gong-heum, Seo Hui, Ri Pil-gyu, Pak Chang-ok, etc.' and expulsion and dismissal measures followed. This event is described as the occasion for the downfall of the Lian Gang faction and the Soviet faction and the consolidation of power by the partisan faction; Kim Doo-bong, who did not directly participate, was also purged. 'Sectarian incident' is the North Korean designation; it is also referred to in academia as the 'August plenary meeting incident.' Lankov (2005), drawing on Soviet records and other sources, analyzes this as a failed 'August plot' aimed at ousting Kim Il-sung, and describes how visits by Feng Dazhui and Mikoyan to North Korea that same year temporarily halted the purges but resumed within a year.

    Data Record: Not included in Powell & Thyne coup data or Archigos (a party challenge without replacement of the leader). Whether it falls under the definition of a coup (an illegal ouster attempt by military or government elites) is disputed among scholarly accounts.

    1. Lian Gang Faction - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — https://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Contents/Item/E0066630
    2. Seo Hui - Korean National Encyclopedia of Culture · Encyclopedia — http://encykorea.aks.ac.kr/Contents/Item/E0028311
    3. Lankov, A. (2005). Crisis in North Korea: The Failure of De-Stalinization, 1956. University of Hawai'i Press (UBC Press introduction) · Monograph · Tone: Based on Soviet records and interviews with defectors — https://ubcpress.ca/crisis-in-north-korea

    2009 Currency Reform and Reports of Backlash Protests · 2009–2010

    North Korea announced a currency reform on November 30, 2009, replacing all circulating currency with new banknotes and coins; it imposed an exchange limit that prevented people from exchanging all their old currency. The exchange limit was repeatedly changed. Haggard & Noland (2010) noted that reports in outlets such as the Chosun Ilbo (2009.12.8) mentioned 'protests' and 'riots' against the reform, but these were either unconfirmed by subsequent reporting or small and localized; they also reported numerous accounts of people being executed for evading the exchange limit or burning their old currency. North Korean official media remained largely silent on the reform.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    Scale of Protests and UnrestNo official announcementUnconfirmed — the 'protests' and 'riots' reported by the press were either unconfirmed by subsequent reporting or small-scale (Haggard & Noland 2010)

    Data Record: Mass Mobilization v5.1: 1 event in 2009 (nonviolent). The ACLED collection begins in 2018, falling outside the scope.

    1. Haggard, S. & Noland, M. (2010). The Winter of Their Discontent: Pyongyang Attacks the Market. PIIE Policy Brief PB10-1 · Research Report · Tone: U.S. think tank; explicitly notes reliance on reports from defectors and North Korea-focused media — https://www.piie.com/sites/default/files/publications/pb/pb10-01.pdf

    2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Rebellions

    Zhen Sheng and Wu Guang's Dazexiang Uprising End of Qin · 209 BCE

    In 209 BCE, Zhen Sheng (陳勝), a Qin military officer, was mobilized to garrison the northern frontier at Yuyang (漁陽) but, together with Wu Guang, raised the banner of rebellion in Dazexiang (大澤鄕) after heavy rain blocked their route and they missed their deadline. The World of Chinese introduces this as the first armed uprising against an imperial dynasty, noting that its strength grew to 10,000 within months, that Zhen Sheng proclaimed himself 'King Zhangchu' (張楚王), and that he was killed by his subordinate within a year.

    1. Sun Jiahui, 'Rebels With a Cause', The World of Chinese (2019.9.18) · Magazine (English-language monthly on Chinese culture) · Tone: Beijing-based general-interest English-language magazine — summarizes the standard view of Chinese academia; original sources (Records of the Grand Historian biography of Zhen Sheng) were not consulted directly — https://theworldofchinese.com/post/rebels-with-a-cause

    Yellow Turban Rebellion Late Eastern Han · 184

    The Daoist healer Zhang Jiao (張角, also Zhang Jiang, died 184 CE), together with his brothers Zhang Bao and Zhang Liang, rebelled in 184; the uprising quickly spread into a nationwide revolt. Joshua J. Mark of World History Encyclopedia describes how the court was corrupt to the point that officials bought offices for money, eunuchs held real power, and the rebels believed that a dynasty had lost its mandate from heaven. The court dispatched Huangfu Song, Lu Su, and Zhu Jun, and the rebellion was suppressed within a year; Zhang Jiao also died in that year. The same article notes that warlords such as Dong Zhuo and Cao Cao subsequently rose to power, leading into the Three Kingdoms period. The World of Chinese introduces this as the first rebellion in Chinese history motivated by religious belief.

    1. Joshua J. Mark, 'The Mandate of Heaven and The Yellow Turban Rebellion', World History Encyclopedia (2019.12.17) · Public history encyclopedia (non-profit) · Tone: introductory for general English-speaking readers — centered on the interpretation of the mandate of heaven — https://www.worldhistory.org/article/1461
    2. Sun Jiahui, 'Rebels With a Cause', The World of Chinese (2019.9.18) · Magazine · Tone: summary of the standard view of Chinese academia — https://theworldofchinese.com/post/rebels-with-a-cause

    The Huang Chao Rebellion Late Tang · 875–884 (Seizure of Chang'an 881, retreat 883)

    Huang Chao (黃巢), a salt smuggler turned rebel leader, seized Chang'an (modern Xi'an) in 881, forcing Emperor Xizong to flee and proclaiming himself 'Emperor of Dàqí'. He retreated in 883 and was later killed by his nephew Lin Yan. The World of Chinese notes that this rebellion severely weakened the Tang dynasty, which fell in 907.

    1. Sun Jiahui, 'Rebels With a Cause', The World of Chinese (2019.9.18) · Magazine · Tone: summary of the standard view of Chinese academia — https://theworldofchinese.com/post/rebels-with-a-cause

    The Li Zicheng Rebellion and the Fall of the Ming Late Ming · 1633–1645

    Born in 1606, Li Zicheng joined the rebel army of Gao Yingxiang in 1633; after Gao's death, he inherited the title 'Trench King' (闖王) and raised an army of more than 30,000 within three years. In 1644, he captured Beijing, where the Chongzhen Emperor committed suicide; Li Zicheng proclaimed the Great Succession (大順), but was defeated by a combined Manchu (Qing) and Wu Sangui force at Shanhai Pass about one month later and retreated to Xi'an, where he is said to have died in 1645 on Mount Jiugong in Hubei.

    1. Sun Jiahui, 'Rebels With a Cause', The World of Chinese (2019.9.18) · Magazine · Tone: summary of the standard view of Chinese academia — circumstances of death marked as 'allegedly' — https://theworldofchinese.com/post/rebels-with-a-cause

    3. 1760–1815 — Literary Sources

    No literary records for this interval or pending investigation.

    5. Modern Events in the Literature

    Taiping Heavenly Kingdom Movement Civil War · 1850–1864

    Hong Xiuquan (洪秀全, b. 1814), a Hakka from Guangxi who failed repeatedly in the imperial examinations, believed himself to be the younger brother of Jesus and launched the Jintian uprising in 1851; he occupied Nanjing in 1853, made it his capital as 'Heavenly Capital' (天京), and established the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom. The Taiping Heavenly Kingdom reformed the imperial examination system and bureaucracy and banned opium (The World of Chinese). With the help of newly organized Xiang Army and foreign-commanded mercenaries, the Qing captured Nanjing in 1864; Hong Xiuquan died under siege. Britain initially declared neutrality but eventually sided with the Qing (Eberspächer 2013, Platt 2012 review).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    DeathsQing court official total unverified in original textOver 20 million (Eberspächer 2013, Platt 2012 review — method of calculation not stated)

    Data record: COW Intra-State Wars v5.1 includes only 'Taiping Rebellion phase 2 of 1860-1866' (battle deaths 250,025) as a Chinese entry — the 1850–1860 Phase 1 is not in the data/CHN.json country entry (observed without interpretation; appears to be an issue of the timing of COW system inclusion). Because it is based on battle deaths, its unit differs from literature estimates that include famine and epidemic deaths.

    1. Cord Eberspächer, review of Stephen R. Platt, Autumn in the Heavenly Kingdom, Michigan War Studies Review 2013-034 (2013.5.6) · Academic book review · Orientation: German university researcher — Critical perspective on Western intervention; review of Platt's work — https://michiganwarstudiesreview.com/2013-034.htm
    2. Sun Jiahui, 'Rebels With a Cause', The World of Chinese (2019.9.18) · Magazine · Orientation: Summary of the standard view in Chinese academia — https://theworldofchinese.com/post/rebels-with-a-cause

    Cultural Revolution rural violence Civil war · 1966–1971

    Walder and Su (2003) analyzed 1,520 local gazetteers published after 1987 and estimated rural deaths during the Cultural Revolution at 750,000 to 1.5 million, together with a similar number of permanently injured persons and 36 million victims of political persecution. The authors state that most casualties occurred between 1968 and 1971, after the temporary local power bodies (revolutionary committees) were established following the end of the mass uprising and factional fighting phase. External observers initially viewed this movement as having barely reached remote rural areas, but post-Mao open sources suggest the opposite.

    ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
    Rural deathsChinese Communist Party and government official total; original text unconfirmed750,000 to 1.5 million (Walder & Su 2003, 1,520 local gazetteers)
    Victims of political persecutionUnconfirmed36 million (Walder & Su 2003)

    Data record: COW includes 'Cultural Revolution phase 2 of 1967-1968' as a civil war; NAVCO 1.3 includes it under the 'Cultural Revolution Red Guards' campaign (data/CHN.json). The state-led suppression in 1968–1971, when most deaths occurred, falls outside the COW period (1967–68).

    1. Andrew G. Walder & Yang Su, 'The Cultural Revolution in the Countryside: Scope, Timing and Human Impact', The China Quarterly 173 (2003) — Stanford APARC abstract · Journal article · Orientation: US university sociology — Estimates based on local gazettes (official publications) — https://aparc.fsi.stanford.edu/node/24671

    1989 Tiananmen Democracy Movement and June 4 Suppression Protest · 1989

    The democratization protests in Beijing in the spring of 1989 ended with military suppression on June 3–4. The government characterized the protests as a 'counterrevolutionary riot.' On June 6, State Council spokesman Yuan Mu stated at a press conference that combining military personnel, 'bandits,' and onlookers yielded approximately 300 deaths; however, the central government has never since released an official death toll or list of names. In 2012, the Tiananmen Mothers group stated that it had confirmed 186 deaths (with a caveat that not all were caused by the military). When RFA contacted Yuan Mu in 2012, he declined to comment.

    ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
    Deaths'Approximately 300' (military personnel, 'bandits,' and onlookers included; State Council spokesman Yuan Mu, 1989.6.6)186 confirmed identities (Tiananmen Mothers group; reported by RFA 2012.5.30)

    Data record: NAVCO 1.3 includes 'Tiananmen Square' under the 'Democracy Movement' campaign. Mass Mobilization includes the China entry (data/CHN.json). Outside the UCDP definition of armed conflict (government vs. organized rebel groups).

    1. Radio Free Asia, 'Tiananmen Spokesman Evades Comment' (2012.5.30) · Media · Orientation: US government-funded international broadcaster — critical of the Chinese government — https://www.rfa.org/english/news/china/tiananmen-05302012120418.html

    2009 Urumqi 7·5 Incident Uprising · 2009

    On July 5–7, 2009, protests and violent unrest occurred in Urumqi, Xinjiang. Human Rights Watch reported that according to the latest official tally, 197 people were killed (the majority Han) and more than 1,600 injured; it noted that the official announcement classified the deceased as 'innocent persons,' including 134 Han, 11 Hui, 10 Uyghurs, and 1 Manchu. Uyghur groups raised objections that the official tally undercounted Uyghur casualties. The same report documented the enforced disappearance of 43 Uyghur men and boys detained by security authorities after the incident.

    ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
    Deaths197 (majority Han; Chinese government official tally, cited by HRW 2009)Uyghur groups claim that the official tally undercounts Uyghur casualties (HRW 2009) — independent figures unverified in original text
    InjuriesMore than 1,600 (official, cited by HRW)Unconfirmed

    Data record: Outside the UCDP and COW definitions of civil war (organized rebel groups and combat deaths). Whether the ACLED China entry includes this event follows the table in the aggregation file (data/CHN.json); it was not cross-checked separately in the narrative.

    5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

    1899 Xiangguan Uprising of the Villagers (Six-Day War) Uprising · 1899

    In 1898, Britain concluded the 'Extension of Hong Kong Territory Convention' with the Qing, leasing Xinjiang for 99 years, and began taking possession from March 1899. Fearing the loss of their livelihoods through the expropriation of land, villagers armed themselves under the leadership of local gentry (xiangshen), and resistance began on April 14. On the afternoon of April 17, villagers attacked British military camps in Tai Po and ambushed British troops in Lam Tsuen Valley; the following day they attacked near Pauting Cheng Chau in Shitouwei (石頭圍). Outnumbered in arms and lacking regular training, the villagers were defeated; at least 500 villagers died in this 'Six-Day War' of 1899, which ended on April 19. The Datede Public Hall in Ping Shan is regarded as one of the meeting places where resistance was organized (Old Things and Ancient Customs Court). Britain subsequently chose not to prosecute the villagers but to cooperate with the gentry in administering Xinjiang, establishing police stations throughout the territory. The iron gates taken by British troops from Jingtian's Ji Qingwei (吉慶圍) were returned at a ceremony presided over by Governor Stubbs on May 26, 1925, following a request for their return by the Deng (鄧) clan in 1924. This resistance is recorded in the Qing diplomatic history Qingji Waijiao Shiliao (清季外交史料).

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
    Villager CasualtiesOriginal text of British colonial authorities' official tally unconfirmedAt least 500 (Academy of Chinese Studies, 劉智鵬·劉蜀永 data)

    Data record: COW, UCDP, Archigos, NAVCO, Mass Mobilization, and ACLED all lack a separate Hong Kong entry (all items in data/HKG.json are blank). In the COW system, small-scale conflicts in the British colony do not appear under any national entry for the United Kingdom or China (data/CHN.json).

    1. Academy of Chinese Studies (中國文化研究院), 'Villagers in the New Territories Resisted the British Takeover', The Splendid Chinese Culture · Educational institution historical materials (Hong Kong nonprofit, educational materials) · Orientation: Chinese cultural education institution — proportion of anti-colonial resistance narratives, data provided by Professors Liu Zhipeng and Liu Shuyong — https://chiculture.org.hk/en/node/2076

    1967 Leftist Riots (六七暴動) Uprising · 1967

    The riots that continued from May to December 1967 were initiated by Hong Kong leftists following the outbreak of the Chinese Cultural Revolution, triggered by a labor dispute at the Xin Pu Gang (新蒲崗) factory (Hong Kong Police 'Police History' account, cited by HKFP 2015). The police history recorded that 8,074 suspected explosive devices were planted that year, of which 1,167 were actual explosives, and that at least 51 people died—including 10 police officers—and 802 were injured. Lam Fung (林彬), a commercial radio host, was burned to death in his car, and a 2-year-old and a 7-year-old brother and sister died in an explosion. In the Sutao Kok border area, five police officers were killed and nine wounded in a gunfight. Ray Yep (2008) analyzes that the event, which began as a labor dispute, soon escalated into a major crisis that led Britain to consider withdrawal and became a turning point in colonial development. In 2015, the Hong Kong Police deleted phrases such as 'shaking Mao's Little Red Book' and 'making bombs in leftist school classrooms' from its online police history, changed 'Communist militia' to 'gunmen,' and altered 'the Chinese border' to 'the Sutao Kok border area'; a police spokesperson stated that these changes were made to simplify the content and correct sentences (HKFP 2015). The riot leader Yang Guang (楊光) received Hong Kong's highest honor, the Grand Bauhinia Medal, in 2001.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    DeathsAt least 51 (including 10 police officers) — Hong Kong Police 'Police History' (cited by HKFP 2015)Unconfirmed (51 deaths and over 50 described in academic books identified at the search stage; original texts not consulted)
    Injuries802 (Hong Kong Police 'Police History')Unconfirmed
    Explosives1,167 actual among 8,074 suspected devices (Hong Kong Police 'Police History')Unconfirmed

    Data record: all 7 datasets lack a Hong Kong entry (all items in data/HKG.json are blank). Whether events in the British colony or the inclusion of the United Kingdom under COW and UCDP were checked in this round is not specified.

    1.     2019 Anti-Extradition Bill Protests Protest · 2019

    In June 2019, large-scale marches were held over consecutive weekends in opposition to a bill amending the Fugitive Offenders Ordinance to allow the extradition of criminal suspects to mainland China. Estimates of the number of participants in the June 16 march varied widely among different counting bodies. The organizer, the Civil Human Rights Front (民間人權陣線), stated that 2 million people took to the streets (counting all participants), while the Hong Kong Police estimated the peak crowd at 380,000 within the designated protest zone from Victoria Park to Tai Lam Park (Reuters). Prof. Paul Yip (葉兆輝) of the Department of Sociology at the Chinese University of Hong Kong observed approximately 500,000 to 800,000 participants based on observation. Chief Executive Carrie Lam exceeded the deadline for withdrawal demanded by the protesters.

    ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
    June 16 march participants380,000 (Hong Kong Police, peak density within designated zone — Reuters 2019.6.20)2 million (organizer's Civil Human Rights Front) / 500,000–800,000 (Prof. Paul Yip of Chinese University of Hong Kong observation estimate)

    Data record: all 7 datasets lack a separate Hong Kong entry (all items in data/HKG.json are blank). NAVCO 1.3 includes 'Hong Kong pro-democracy (Umbrella Movement)' under the China (CHN) entry — Hong Kong protests are attributed to CHN in national-level data (data/CHN.json). Whether Mass Mobilization and ACLED include 2019 Hong Kong events under CHN was not checked in this round.

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      The Nauru tribal civil war (Ten Years War) and German annexation Civil War · ca. 1878–1888

      Foreigners began settling on the island from the 1830s, including escaped convicts and shipwrecked sailors; in 1843, British naval commander Simpson recorded testimony that John Jones, an escaped convict from Norfolk Island, had exercised a reign of terror over both indigenous people and foreigners until 1842. When missionary Doane (E. T. Doane) wrote in 1879 that the population numbered approximately 1,200, it was immediately after the outbreak of prolonged internal warfare on the island (Underwood 1989). In 1888, the Eber arrived with German forces to formally annex the island and 'pacify warring factions'; the German administrative authorities recorded 1,294 indigenous people in the first census of 1889 (435 adult men, 573 adult women, 139 boys, 147 girls). Kretzschmer (1913) and Viviani (1970) attributed the population decline primarily to disease and epidemics, while noting that male deaths in the 'Ten Years War' contributed to the sex ratio imbalance; Hambruch (1915) and Kayser (1918) raised doubts about the mortality effects of indigenous warfare (Underwood 1989 synthesis). The number of fatalities in the war itself is not presented in any academic source consulted.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
      Indigenous Population1,294 (German administrative authorities, first census 1889)Approx. 1,400 (Simpson's estimate 1843), approx. 1,200 (missionary Doane's estimate 1879), 1,500–1,800 (Geiseler's estimate 1884; Underwood evaluates this as an overestimate) — Underwood (1989) synthesis
      War FatalitiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (Underwood 1989 does not provide figures)

      Data record: no Nauru entries in COW Civil War, UCDP, Powell & Thyne, Archigos, or NAVCO (this pipeline's data/NRU.json). Because Nauru was outside the state system before 1888, it falls outside the definitions of interstate and intrastate war used by COW.

      1. Underwood, Jane H. (1989). Population History of Nauru: A Cautionary Tale. Micronesica 22(1): 3–22 · Journal article · Orientation: anthropological journal (secondary synthesis of colonial administration and missionary records) — https://www.micronesica.org/sites/default/files/2-population20history20of20nauru20a20cautionary20tale20micronesica20vol.202220no.20120aug.2c201989.pdf

      Unrest at the Nauru Detention Facility (Australian offshore refugee detention center) Uprising · 2013

      On July 19, 2013 (Friday), unrest broke out at an offshore detention facility for asylum seekers on Nauru run by Australia. Protesters threw rocks at guards and police; a fire destroyed the entire accommodation block and medical facilities and offices, leaving only the dining hall and recreation building standing (AP report). The background was reported to be Australian Prime Minister Kevin Rudd's announcement, immediately prior, that he would not allow boat arrivals to settle in Australia (UPI, citing Sydney Morning Herald). Reports stated that one police officer was stabbed and taken hostage (UPI). The participants in the unrest were not Nauruan citizens but foreign asylum seekers accommodated under Australian policy.

      ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
      Property damageApproximately 60 million Australian dollars (Australian Department of Home Affairs spokesperson, reported by AP 2013.07.21)24,000 USD (UPI 2013.07.22 report, source unindicated — differs substantially from other reports)
      Detention and arrests125 detained by police (AP 2013.07.21, as of Sunday)At least 59 escapees arrested (UPI 2013.07.22, citing Sydney Morning Herald)
      InjuriesUnconfirmedEight asylum seekers treated in hospital (AP 2013.07.21)

      Data record: because ACLED's coverage of Nauru begins only in 2021 (0 protests), the 2013 incident falls outside the scope. Not recorded in Mass Mobilization or NAVCO.

      2. UPI (2013.07.22), 'Australian refugee policy sparks Nauru riots' · Media · Orientation: wire service, reprinted by Sydney Morning Herald — https://www.upi.com/Top_News/Special/2013/07/22/Australian-refugee-policy-sparks-Nauru-riots/92901374487680/

      Protest outside Parliament and the 'Nauru 19' trial Protest · 2015–2019

      In June 2015, hundreds protested outside Parliament after the government suspended the duties of three lawmakers who had given critical interviews to foreign media. According to a report by Hawaii Public Radio, the crowd spilled onto an adjacent airport runway, flights were canceled, and some windows were broken by rocks. Nineteen people, including former presidents and former legislators, were charged with rioting, obstructing Parliament, and entering restricted areas. In September 2018, Supreme Court Justice Geoffrey Muecke ruled that a permanent stay of prosecution was warranted because a fair trial was impossible, describing the government's conduct as 'an outrageous affront to the rule of law.' The government appealed, and in 2018 the Australian High Court declined jurisdiction; the newly established Nauru Court of Appeal overturned the permanent stay in June 2019. In December 2019, Justice Daniel Fatiaki found 11 people guilty of rioting (not guilty of obstructing Parliament), four guilty of assaulting a police officer, and two guilty of entering restricted areas; sentences ranged from three to eleven months (CIVICUS summary). Former President Sprent Dabwido, one of the main defendants, died in 2019. The government (Attorney General David Adey) maintained its prosecution position, while defense counsel argued that a retrial would be a 'scripted trial' (RNZ report) — both accounts are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
      Number of indictments19 (Nauru Public Prosecutor's indictment)19 (CIVICUS 2020, Hawaii Public Radio 2018)
      Convictions11 for rioting, 4 for assaulting a police officer, 2 for entering restricted areas; sentences of 3 to 11 months (Nauru Supreme Court, Justice Fatiaki, December 2019)Same figures (CIVICUS 2020 summary); three of the originally indicted individuals had pleaded guilty earlier and had their sentences increased on appeal

      Data record: neither ACLED's Nauru coverage (2021–) nor Mass Mobilization records the 2015 protests — a coverage gap for small states.

      2. Hawaiʻi Public Radio, Pacific News Minute (2018.09.18), 'Judge throws out case against the Nauru 19' · Media · Orientation: US public broadcaster — https://www.hawaiipublicradio.org/pacific-news-minute/2018-09-18/pacific-news-minute-judge-throws-out-case-against-the-nauru-19

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      New Zealand Wars (Ngā Pakanga o Aotearoa) Civil War · 1840s–1872

      Following the 1840 Treaty of Waitangi, armed conflict continued between British and colonial government forces and several Māori tribes over land and sovereignty, and actual fighting ended around 1872 (O'Malley 2019, Spinoff review). Under the 1863 New Zealand Settlements Act, more than 3 million acres were confiscated in Waikato, Taranaki, Tauranga, East Bay of Plenty, and Mohaka-Waikare, and land was also acquired and transferred along the east coast at Tūranganui-a-Kiwa, Whakatōhea, and Waikaremoana. Casualty figures are not precisely tallied, and historians typically cite James Cowan's estimates. Because some Māori fought alongside government forces (kūpapa), the conflict is not described as a single binary of 'British versus Māori'.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
      Fatalities (Government Side)Unconfirmed (original colonial government official tally not consulted)560 British and colonial troops killed, 1,050 wounded (Cowan's estimate, cited in O'Malley 2019 review)
      Fatalities (Māori)UnconfirmedAnti-government Māori: 2,000 killed, 2,000 wounded; pro-government Māori: 250 killed (Cowan's estimate — including a caveat that Māori figures are only estimates)
      Total CasualtiesUnconfirmed5,860 (Cowan's estimated total)
      Confiscated LandConfiscation under the 1863 New Zealand Settlements ActMore than 3 million acres (cited in O'Malley 2019 review)

      Data record: all entries for New Zealand in this pipeline's data/NZL.json — COW intra-state war, UCDP (1946–), P&T, Archigos, and NAVCO — are 0. The New Zealand Wars occurred during the British colonial period and may fall under COW extra-state wars; however, because the collection contains no table of extra-state wars, they could not be checked.

      1.       Parisian Hakka Nonviolent Resistance and Suppression Protests · 1879–1881

      The Parisian Hakka settlement in Taranaki, established in 1866 by Te Whiti o Rongomai and Tohu Kākahi, became a center of nonviolent resistance against land confiscation. In 1879, people plowing confiscated land to resist surveying and road construction were arrested, reaching 182 by the end of July. Parliament enacted the Maori Prisoners' Trials Act (1879), the Maori Prisoners Act (1880), the West Coast Settlements Act (1880, authorizing warrantless arrests), and the West Coast Districts Maintenance of Law and Order Act (1882) in rapid succession. On November 5, 1881, 655 armed police and approximately 1,000 settler volunteers entered the settlement; Te Whiti and Tohu were arrested on charges of incitement and held without trial for 16 months (Gillespie 2019). In 2017, the government apologized to Parisian Hakka residents on behalf of the monarch, a $9 million reconciliation package was finalized in 2018, and the apology was enacted into law in 2019 as the Parihaka Reconciliation Act (Te Pire Haeata ki Parihaka) (official commentary of the New Zealand Courts).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
      Forces EnteringUnconfirmed (original government announcement text not consulted)655 armed police + approximately 1,000 settler volunteers (Gillespie 2019); over 1,500 and approximately 1,600 (sources at different search stages, unconsulted)
      Arrests of Plow ResistanceUnconfirmed182 by the end of July 1879 (Gillespie 2019)
      Detention of Leaders16 months without trial (New Zealand Courts commentary)16 months without trial (Gillespie 2019)

      Data Record: The event occurred after 1816 but is not recorded in any collection, including COW and NAVCO (1900–) — nonviolent resistance preceded the start of systematic datasets.

      Protests against the 1981 Springbok Tour Protests · 1981

      Protests against matches involving the South African rugby team (the Springboks) under apartheid continued. On July 25, 1981, hundreds of protesters opposing the tour entered Hamilton Rugby Park and the Springboks-Waikato match was canceled; clashes then broke out between pro- and anti-tour factions, resulting in over 50 arrests (DigitalNZ compilation of records). The event is recorded as a case in which society divided along lines for and against the tour.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
      Arrests at Hamilton MatchUnconfirmed (police announcement text not consulted)Over 50 (DigitalNZ 2020, based on records from museums and libraries)

      2022 Occupation of Parliament Grounds Protests Protests · 2022

      Protests against mandatory COVID-19 vaccination began on February 8, 2022, when a convoy opposing the mandate arrived at Wellington's parliament; protesters occupied the parliamentary grounds and surrounding streets. Police attempted to disperse the crowd on February 10, arresting 108 people before suspending operations; an operation on March 2 ended occupations of the parliamentary grounds, Victoria University campus, St Paul's Cathedral, and surrounding streets. The Independent Police Conduct Authority (IPCA) concluded in a review report dated April 20, 2023, that police had 'generally served the public well,' but noted that there were flaws in planning before the occupation, in preparations for the February 10 dispersal attempt, and in communication, and that arrest procedures and evidence recording were poor, resulting in many prosecutions being withdrawn (reported by 1News and The Spinoff). There were 1,905 complaints related to the occupation.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
      Police-Related Complaints1,905 (IPCA 2023, of which 19 were investigated separately and 14 were referred for further investigation)6 findings of excessive force — 2 on February 10, 2 on February 21–23, 2 on March 2 (reported by 1News 2023.12.14)
      Number of Arrests108 on February 10 (Spinoff 2023.04.20, citing IPCA report); total for the entire occupation unconfirmedUnconfirmed

      Data record: ACLED (pipeline collection): New Zealand protests, 235 events in 2021, 305 in 2022 (the highest in a year with occupations), 214 in 2023. Political violence: 3 incidents and 1 death in 2021.

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      Petition to Halt Nuclear Testing (submitted to the Trusteeship Council) Protest · 1954

      The fact that the fallout from the March 1, 1954 U.S. hydrogen bomb test (Bravo) damaged the health and property of residents outside the closed-off zone was also stated in a July 23, 1954 letter to the U.S. representative Sears (Mason Sears). Marshall Islanders submitted a petition to the UN Trusteeship Council protesting the use of the UN Trust Territory under U.S. administration as a 'nuclear weapons test site.' On May 3, 1954, a U.S. deputy permanent representative's dispatch reported that Ralph Bunche, an official at the UN Secretariat, had shown this petition concerning 'explosions of lethal weapons within the homeland islands' to him. In the 14th session, a Soviet resolution calling for a halt to testing and a draft resolution seeking an advisory opinion from the International Court of Justice were defeated, while a U.S. resolution was adopted by 9 to 3 (with the Soviet Union, Syria, and India voting against). Sears assessed this resolution as implicitly endorsing continued U.S. testing, and India stated that it would raise the issue on the agenda of the Ninth UN General Assembly (U.S. Department of State FRUS 1952–1954 Vol. 3). The account is based on internal U.S. government documents; the original petition text could not be checked.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Trusteeship Council Vote on U.S. Resolution9 in favor, 3 against (Soviet Union, Syria, India) — U.S. Department of State FRUS document 956 (1954.07.23)Unconfirmed (original UN records not checked)

      Data Record: COW, UCDP, NAVCO, and Mass Mobilization all lack a Marshall Islands entry (data/MHL.json). The Marshall Islands at the time of 1954 were a UN Trust Territory administered by the United States and lie outside the definition of national unit datasets.

      1. U.S. Department of State, FRUS 1952–1954 Vol. III, Doc. 956: Sears to the Secretary of State (1954.07.23) · Government Document · Orientation: U.S. government internal document (perspective of the implementing state) — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1952-54v03/d956
      2. U.S. Department of State, FRUS 1952–1954 Vol. III, Doc. 932: Wadsworth to the Department of State (1954.05.03) · Government Document · Orientation: U.S. government internal document (perspective of the implementing state) — https://history.state.gov/historicaldocuments/frus1952-54v03/d932

      Kwajalein 'Operation Homecoming' Occupation Protest · 1982–1983

      Following the start of missile tests on Kwajalein Atoll in 1964, most landowners were relocated to Ebeye Island (CSM 1982). In June 1982, landowners opposing the extension of the lease for 50 years reoccupied Kwajalein, Roi-Namur, and the six central islands. Their demands were the cessation of base operations, compensation for affected landowners, and a shortened lease term; their leader was Ataji Balos of the Kwajalein Atoll Corporation (KAC) (Swarthmore Nonviolent Action DB). Initial police arrests numbered approximately 15. The U.S. side responded with intensified searches of Ebeye–Kwajalein commuters, dismissal of non-essential workers, closure of banks, water shutoffs at occupied sites, and blockades of food shipments to Ebeye; a federal district court in Washington ordered the military to restore water supplies (CSM 1982). On August 3, 1982, protesters refused to evacuate, leading to the cancellation of one missile test. A Pentagon spokesperson stated that there had been no impact on the test schedule through mid-July, and a State Department official viewed the discontent as stemming from fiscal conditions (CSM 1982) — both accounts are reported side by side. After approximately four months of occupation, the U.S. and Marshall Islands governments and KAC entered negotiations; an agreement was reached on a 30-year lease instead of 50 years, $10 million for Ebeye improvements, and the return of six islands. However, the landowners opposed the 30-year term and government management of rent and took up occupation again (Swarthmore DB).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Occupation ParticipantsUnconfirmedApproximately 1,000 (CSM 1982.07.19), 800–1,000 (Swarthmore Nonviolent Action DB)
      ArrestsUnconfirmedApproximately 15 (Swarthmore Nonviolent Action DB)
      RentFrom $750,000/year after the 1979 protests → $1.9 million/year (CSM 1982 report)Unconfirmed
      Agreed Conditions30-year lease, $10 million for Ebeye improvements, return of six islands (Swarthmore DB compilation)Unconfirmed (agreement text not checked)

      Data Record: At the time of 1982, the Marshall Islands were a Trust Territory (the Compact of Free Association took effect in 1986), so they are not included in COW, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization national unit records. They are recorded in the Swarthmore Global Nonviolent Action Database.

      1.       2. Christian Science Monitor (1982.07.19), Karla Vallance, 'Protesting US missile tests on atolls' · Media · Orientation: U.S. major media (citing Pentagon and State Department statements) — https://csmonitor.com/1982/0719/071945.html

      Viktor Trust Fund Use Protest Rally (Maajro) Protest · 2023

      In May 2023, residents of Bikini protested outside the Kiribati, Bikini, and Ejit (KBE) local government building in Majuro, demanding accountability from elected local government leadership for the use of millions of dollars from the Bikini Trust Fund. Kiribati had been experiencing power rationing since January of that year owing to the depletion of funds for the Bikinian resettlement trust fund, and the current leadership obtained control of the fund in late 2017. Simon Zamo'o, the grandson of King Juda who led the first relocation of Bikini residents in 1946, addressed the crowd. Mayor Anderson Jibas and some councilors left their posts after pre-protest media interviews. The Marshall Islands Journal characterized the protest as the first in Majuro in years and the first by citizens protesting government management of funds; it cited previous protests, including a 1999 hospital staff protest and strike, a 2003 protest against the second Compact of Free Association by landowners (Nitielar), and a large-scale protest against domestic violence in 2010.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Number of ParticipantsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (Marshall Islands Journal did not provide figures)
      ACLED Protest EventsNone3 events in 2023, 1 event in 2024; 0 deaths (data/MHL.json, ACLED aggregate)

      Data record: whether this event is included among the 3 protests in the Marshall Islands recorded by ACLED in 2023 was not verified against individual events. The 1999, 2003, and 2010 protests fall outside the ACLED collection period (2021–) and are not recorded in Mass Mobilization.

      1. Marshall Islands Journal (2023.05.11), Giff Johnson, 'People protest money use' · Media · Orientation: local weekly — https://marshallislandsjournal.com/kbe-people-protest-money-use/

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      Pohnpei Uprising of 1887 (Spanish colonial outpost Mesenieng) Uprising · 1887

      In 1887 Spain sent its first governor, Isidro Posadillo, to Pohnpei and established a colonial outpost at Mesenieng (present-day Kolonia). According to Micronesian Seminar publications, workers in Pohnpei resisted forced labor by refusing to work; when Spanish troops forced them to return, men from Sokehs and Net fired on them, killing 20 soldiers. In the withdrawal process, Governor Posadillo and several others were killed; the Spanish side remained at anchor for several months before regaining control after three warships arrived in late October. Educational materials from the College of Micronesia–FSM (COM-FSM) describe the incident differently: traditional leaders in Net and Sokehs refused to appear at the governor's residence, and when officials went to arrest them, they were ambushed. Both accounts are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Spanish CasualtiesUnconfirmed (not cross-checked against official Spanish government tallies)20 soldiers + several others including the governor (MicSem publication)

      Data record: Pohnpei in 1887 was outside state systems (a target of Spanish colonial expansion) and thus outside the COW definition of intrastate war. Whether this event is recorded as a colonial or imperial war (extra-state) in COW is not present in the pipeline data (data/FSM.json) and could not be checked.

      1. Micronesian Seminar, 'The Catholic Church in Pohnpei' (Catholic Church in Micronesia series) · Research institute publication · Orientation: Catholic Jesuit research institute (including missionary perspective) — https://micsem.org/pubs/books/catholic/pohnpei/index.htm
      2. College of Micronesia–FSM, 'Spanish Legacy in Micronesia: Influences – Pohnpei' · Educational institution materials · Orientation: Local university educational materials (citing Hezel 1995) — https://www.comfsm.fm/~pken/Spanish%20Legacy%20in%20Micronesia/influences-pohnpei.html

      Owa (Madolenihmw) Uprising of 1890 Uprising · 1890

      In late June 1890 residents of Owa in the Madolenihmw region attacked Spanish troops, killing more than 30. Spain repeatedly retaliated and attempted to retake Owa, with casualties mounting; in late November it raided the fortifications of the defenders but soon withdrew to its colonial outpost. The MicSem publication states that in this second uprising Spain lost 118 men and approximately 200,000 pesos. COM-FSM materials describe how a Catholic missionary built a church next to a Protestant mission in Owa, provoking opposition among Madolenihmw Protestants.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Spanish LossesUnconfirmed (not cross-checked against official Spanish government tallies)118 troops and approximately 200,000 pesos (MicSem publication); more than 30 killed in the first attack in late June

      Data record: Colonial-era events outside the definitions of national unit datasets (COW, UCDP, NAVCO) — no entry in data/FSM.json.

      2. College of Micronesia–FSM, 'Spanish Legacy in Micronesia: Influences – Pohnpei' · Educational institution materials · Orientation: Local university educational materials — https://www.comfsm.fm/~pken/Spanish%20Legacy%20in%20Micronesia/influences-pohnpei.html

      Sokehs Rebellion (German Rule) Uprising · 1910–1911

      On October 18, 1910, an uprising against German rule began in Sokehs, Pohnpei (Office of National Archives, Culture & Historic Preservation, FSM). The labor and tax ordinances of the former district governor Georg Fritz were the background to discontent; after a workers' strike, District Governor Gustav Bödder and the secretary were killed, and Germans who had taken refuge at the Sokehs mission were also killed as they went to the wharf (MicSem publication). The MicSem video introduction describes the incident as retaliation for injuries sustained by Pohnpeian men when they clashed with authorities. The siege lasted 40 days; German reinforcements arrived in January 1911, and the Sokehs fighters surrendered at the end of February. On February 24, 1911, 15 men who had fought alongside General Soumadou of Sokehs were shot and buried together in Komwonlaid Cemetery, Kolonia (National Archives of FSM). The German authorities regarded them as rebel leaders; the remaining residents of Sokehs were deported to Palau. Accounts differ on the number of executions and deportations (table below).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Executions15 shot and buried together (1911.02.24) — FSM Office of National Archives, Culture & Historic Preservation (NACH) historical site guide17 leaders executed (MicSem video introduction); leaders executed, number unrecorded (MicSem publication)
      Deportation to PalauUnconfirmedMore than 400 (MicSem publication), 460 (MicSem video introduction)

      Data record: Because this is a colonial-era event, it falls outside the COW definition of intrastate war and UCDP (1946–) and NAVCO definitions — no entry in data/FSM.json. Even within the same research institute (MicSem), figures for executions (15/17) and deportations (more than 400/460) diverge.

      2. Micronesian Seminar, 'Sokehs Rebellion' (2019 video introduction) · Research institute materials · Orientation: Catholic Jesuit research institute — https://micsem.org/video-listing/sokehs-rebellion/
      3. Micronesian Seminar, 'The Catholic Church in Pohnpei' · Research institute publication · Orientation: Catholic Jesuit research institute (including missionary perspective) — https://micsem.org/pubs/books/catholic/pohnpei/index.htm

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      The Santo rebellion (declaration of separation from Vemana) and Papua New Guinea military intervention Uprising · 1980

      On May 28, 1980, just before the independence of the British–French condominium of New Hebrides (July 30, 1980), a group led by Jimmy Stevens, leader of the Nagriamel movement, seized Espiritu Santo and proclaimed the 'Berevka Republic' (UPI 1980.11.21). Weapons consisted mainly of bows and arrows, with a few rifles (UPI). French-speaking plantation owners who supported the rebels reportedly feared land reform under an English-speaking government (UPI; Christian Science Monitor 1980.07.25). On July 24, 1980 (Thursday), a joint British Marine and French paratrooper force of 200 landed unopposed at an airport near Luganville (CSM 1980.07.25). After independence, Papua New Guinean troops were deployed; reports emerged in late August that the operation had been delayed after Stevens's son was killed (Washington Post headline, 1980.08.31). Stevens was arrested on August 30, and on November 21, Chief Justice Frederick Cook sentenced him to 14 years' imprisonment and a $30,000 fine for admitting guilt on 11 charges, including sedition and possession of weapons and explosives (UPI 1980.11.21). Other defendants received sentences of 18 months to 5 years (UPI 1980.11.21). The judge noted links with French commissaires and some French residents in New Caledonia, acknowledging complicity by some French officials but not by the French government, and reported that a US organization, Phoenix Society, had supported Stevens and assisted in drafting a constitutional draft (UPI). Regarding the extent of French involvement, there are discrepancies between the court ruling reports and subsequent accounts, so no definitive judgment is made.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Troops Deployed (UK–France Joint)200 (context of UK Commissary-General announcement; CSM 1980.07.25 report)Unconfirmed
      Rebel Leader's Sentence14 years' imprisonment and $30,000 fine (Vanuatu Supreme Court, November 21, 1980; UPI report)Same figure (UPI)
      FatalitiesUnconfirmedOne son of Stevens killed (Washington Post headline, 1980.08.31) — total fatalities not stated in sources consulted

The conflict began in colonial territory immediately prior to independence and lies outside the scope of datasets based on state systems; moreover, the scale of fatalities appears not to reach the UCDP threshold (25 per year).

      Port Vila Riots and the President's Attempt to Dissolve Parliament Regime Overthrow · 1988

      In December 1988, shortly after Barak Sope was dismissed from his post as head of the Land Development Corporation on charges of financial mismanagement, protests he had sparked escalated into riots that left one person dead and caused damage of approximately $1 million in Port Vila (Christian Science Monitor 1988.12.20). Later that same month, President Ati George Sokomanu, Sope's uncle, dissolved parliament and sought to dismiss Prime Minister Walter Lini while appointing Sope and four supporters as a 'temporary government.' Lini maintained that the constitution did not grant such exclusive authority to the president and kept parliament in session; troops loyal to Lini arrested Sope and the 'cabinet' on Sunday, and the Supreme Court ruled Sokomanu's actions unconstitutional on Monday (CSM 1988.12.20). UPI reported the arrest of the president on the same day. The presidential side regarded the dissolution as an exercise of constitutional authority, while the government regarded it as a violation of the constitution — both positions are stated in parallel.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Fatalities in 1988 RiotsUnconfirmed1 (CSM 1988.12.20)
      Property DamageUnconfirmedApproximately $1 million (CSM 1988.12.20)

      Data Record: No entry for Vanuatu in 1988 in the Powell & Thyne coup list or Archigos abnormal transfers (data/VUT.json). It appears that the president's attempt to dissolve parliament was invalidated by a Supreme Court ruling, and no leadership change occurred. Vanuatu is not included in Mass Mobilization.

      1. Christian Science Monitor (1988.12.20), 'Coup attempt in S. Pacific nation' · Media · Lean: US daily newspaper, headline characterizing it as a 'coup attempt' — https://www.csmonitor.com/1988/1220/ovanu.html
      2. UPI Archives (1988.12.20), 'Vanuatu president reported arrested' · Media · Lean: US news agency (only headline and search summary verified) — https://www.upi.com/Archives/1988/12/20/Vanuatu-president-reported-arrested/6073598597200/

      Vanuatu Mobile Force (VMF) Mutiny and Kidnapping of the President Coup · 1996

      The semi-military Vanuatu Mobile Force (VMF), dissatisfied with service conditions, kidnapped the president on October 12, 1996; it conscripted a civilian aircraft and its pilot to travel to the acting prime minister in the north at dawn, obtaining an agreement for an inquiry and immunity (Ambrose 1997). Subsequently, the 'VMF Standby Group' forced a change of command and kidnapped and assaulted foreign staff at the Ministry of Finance. In early November, a police operation led by Attorney General Linie arrested more than 100 people (Ambrose 1997). The pilot kidnapping case was dismissed, and the president kidnapping case resulted in convictions with sentences of 18 to 24 months suspended (Ambrose 1997). The author describes this year as one in which 'two events that could be regarded as coup attempts' occurred. The mutineers cited the resolution of grievances over back pay, while the government emphasized law enforcement — both positions are stated side by side.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Number of ArrestsUnconfirmedMore than 100 (Ambrose 1997)
      Sentence for Kidnapping the President18–24 months, suspended (Vanuatu courts)Same content (Ambrose 1997 summary)
      FatalitiesUnconfirmedNo mention of fatalities in reviewed sources

      Data record: The Powell & Thyne coup list contains no entry for Vanuatu in 1996 (data/VUT.json). It appears to have been classified as an event in which the intent to seize power did not materialize, because the president was released on the same day.

      1. Ambrose, David (1997). 'Vanuatu politics—two into one won't go'. Pacific Economic Bulletin 12(2): 115–122 · Journal article · Tone: Situation notes by the head of the Australian National University Melanesia Governance Project (critical of the government) — https://openresearch-repository.anu.edu.au/bitstream/1885/157495/1/122_vanuatu.pdf

      Port Vila Black Sands Clash (Tanna and Ambrym Residents) Uprising · 2007

      In early March 2007, a clash broke out between residents from Tanna Island and those from Ambrym Island in an unauthorized settlement called Black Sands on the outskirts of Port Vila, leaving three dead. The incident was triggered by claims that a woman from the Tanna community had been killed by black magic (RNZ 2007.03.19). In the same month, the National Council of Chiefs held a meeting in Port Vila to discuss the dispute (RNZ). Reports indicated that the government declared a state of emergency for two weeks, but the date of declaration and the number of arrests could not be verified from the original text in this round (see Unclear Items).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      FatalitiesUnconfirmed (original government gazette text not consulted)3 (RNZ 2007.03.19)

      Data record: ACLED Vanuatu counts begin in 2021 (data/VUT.json), so the 2007 event is outside the scope. Whether UCDP records non-state conflicts or Mass Mobilization is not specified in data/VUT.json.

      5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

      The Mau Movement and the 1929 'Black Saturday' Shooting Protest · 1926–1936 (shooting 1929.12.28)

      In Western Samoa, which was governed by New Zealand from 1914, the 1918 Spanish flu, in which approximately one-fifth of the population died, is remembered as a defining event of New Zealand rule, and the Mau movement grew into an independence movement against the New Zealand administration (RNZ 'Untold Pacific History' 2021.06.01). In Apia in 1929, armed New Zealand police shot and killed Mau leader Tupua Tamasese Lealofi III during a peaceful protest (RNZ 2002.06.05). On June 4, 2002, New Zealand Prime Minister Helen Clark formally apologized in Apia for New Zealand's early rule of Samoa, calling it 'clumsy and incompetent,' and laid a wreath at the leader's grave the following day. Tupua Tamasese Efi, former prime minister and nephew of the leader, said this was the first acknowledgment by the New Zealand government, while former Minister of Health Siaosi Mililani 2nd said that New Zealand had also done good things, such as scholarships and trade (RNZ 2002.06.05) — both accounts are presented side by side. The total number of fatalities on the day of the shooting could not be verified from the original text in this round (see Uncorroborated Items).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
      Fatalities in the 1929 ShootingUncorroborated (records of New Zealand administration investigations not reviewed)Uncorroborated — figures from the New Zealand Ministry for Culture and Heritage NZHistory and Te Ara could not be consulted due to access restrictions
      1918 Influenza Mortality RateUnconfirmedApproximately one-fifth of the population (RNZ 2021.06.01)

      Data Record: NAVCO records nonviolent and violent campaigns from 1900 onward, but there is no entry for the Mau movement in data/WSM.json. Because it falls within the colonial period, it lies outside the scope of COW and UCDP state-unit coverage. No entry in Mass Mobilization.

      2.       2021 Constitutional Crisis (Parliamentary Shutdown and Tent Inauguration) Regime Overthrow · 2021

      The results of the April 9, 2021 general election (51 seats) left the ruling HRPP and the newly formed FAST in a deadlocked position, and after independent MP Tuilaepa joined FAST, the party secured a majority. The head of state and the electoral commissioner appointed an additional HRPP member on the basis of the women's seat quota (10%) provision and announced a by-election, but the Supreme Court ruled both invalid on May 17. On May 24, the head of state announced the convening of parliament and then canceled it on the 22nd; the Supreme Court overturned the cancellation on the 23rd, but under the constitution the 24th was the 45th day after the general election, and the doors of parliament were locked by acting Prime Minister Tuilaepa Sailele Malielegaoi's order. FAST held the inauguration oath-taking of Prime Minister Piamē Nōmīmataivai Mata'afa and her cabinet under a tent in the parliamentary grounds (RNZ 2021.05.27). Tuilaepa called this 'treason,' while a FAST spokesperson said that the parliamentary shutdown was 'an accurate description of a coup' (RNZ) — both accounts are presented side by side. On July 23, 2021, a three-judge Court of Appeal, including Chief Justice Perepe, ruled that the May 24 oath-taking was constitutional and that subsequent actions of the transitional government were unconstitutional and unlawful (ABC 2021.07.23). Local media reported that Tuilaepa had challenged the ruling (ABC).

      HRPP Filed Election LitigationUnconfirmed47 cases (RNZ 2021.05.27)
      Fatalities and InjuriesUnconfirmedNo mention of casualties in reviewed reports

      Data record: ACLED Samoa records 12 protests, 3 political violence events, and 0 deaths in 2021 (data/WSM.json), overlapping with this crisis period. No Powell & Thyne or Archigos entries — a case in which the government changed through a court ruling without military mobilization.

      2.       5. Modern Events through the Literature

      The Kwaio Uprising on Malaita — The killing of George Brown and punitive expeditions Uprising · 1927

      On October 4, 1927, during the period of the British Solomon Islands Protectorate, William Bell, a district officer, Kenneth Lilies, an officer cadet, and 15 native police were killed in Sinalagu on Malaita Island while collecting taxes (Australian Naval Historical Society Occasional Paper 154). The TRC records that men from Kwaini led by Basiana killed Bell's party out of resentment over the harsh enforcement of taxation, and the naval historical society source cites both 'unpaid' taxes and demands to surrender firearms. About two weeks later, the Australian cruiser HMAS Adelaide arrived in Sinalagu (October 16), and on October 21 a civilian force of 55 police officers, 29 European volunteers, and 62 porters joined up (Naval Historical Society). The TRC records that the expedition 'killed approximately 60 people and committed a series of atrocities despite encountering no resistance,' and the naval historical society source also notes that 'at least 60 innocent people' were killed. Six men were hanged after the trial (Naval Historical Society). According to the TRC summary, Kwaini people still seek compensation for this event, and the TRC included the 'Kwaini massacre' as an unresolved historical issue in its recommendations. The colonial authorities describe it as a police arrest operation for the murder of public officials, while the Kwaini side and the TRC describe it as a massacre of non-resisting residents — both accounts are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Government-Side FatalitiesBell, Lilies, and 15 native police (Australian Naval Historical Society compilation)Bell, officer cadet, and 13 Melanesian police and servants (Keesing & Corris 1980 review summary; original not consulted) — discrepancy between sources
      Fatalities in Kwaini during the Punitive ExpeditionUnconfirmed (original official protectorate statistics unavailable)Approximately 60 (TRC Summary 14), at least 60 (Australian Naval Historical Society)
      Expedition Troops55 police officers, 29 European volunteers, 62 porters + HMAS Adelaide sailors (Naval Historical Society)50 Australian soldiers, 28 white civilians, Malaita police, and approximately 50 volunteers (TRC Summary 14) — discrepancy between sources
      Death SentenceSix hanged (Naval Historical Society)Unconfirmed

      Data Record: Because this is an event of the protectorate period, it falls outside the scope of datasets based on national system criteria such as COW, UCDP, and Archigos (no data/SLB.json entry).

      The Maasina Rule Movement and the Imprisonment of its Leaders Protests · 1944–1952

      The Maasina Rule began in early 1944 in 'Are'are on Malaita Island. Many Malaitians were working in wartime labor camps at the time, and the movement initially aimed to improve agriculture, integrate villages, and record customary law (Solomon Islands Historical Encyclopedia 'Maasina Rule'). By late 1945 it had become an effective alternative government across the island, refusing external labor, and by 1946 it is estimated that approximately 95% of Malaitians participated (same source). In early 1947 leaders planned a formal strike demanding a minimum monthly wage of £12 for workers, and in June about 6,000 to 7,000 people met with the district officer in Auki (same source). The British authorities arrested the leaders in Operation De-Louse and sentenced the principal chiefs to six years' hard labor (same source). Ten paramount chiefs, including Jonathan Fifi'i of Kwaini, were convicted of sedition in 1947 and released on parole in 1950 (historical encyclopedia entry on Fifi'i). In operations from 1948 to 1952, 'thousands' of Malaitians and Makira Islanders were arrested (same source). Following conciliatory measures by the district officer in 1952, the first Malaita Parliament convened in late 1953. The TRC characterizes this movement as a struggle for independence from Britain and a movement oriented toward 'custom' (kastom). The authorities regarded it as sedition and lawbreaking, while the movement regarded it as a demand for autonomy — both perspectives are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial StatementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Participation Rate (1946)UnconfirmedApproximately 95% of Malaitians (Solomon Islands Historical Encyclopedia estimate)
      Arrests in 1948–52Unconfirmed (original official protectorate statistics unavailable)'Thousands' (historical encyclopedia; exact total unknown)
      Penalties for Paramount ChiefsSix years for sedition (1947)Same content, released on parole in 1950 (historical encyclopedia)

      Data Record: Because this is a nonviolent movement of the protectorate period, there are no entries in datasets such as NAVCO or Mass Mobilization (no data/SLB.json entry).

      2. Solomon Islands Historical Encyclopaedia, Fifi'i, Jonathan (c.1921–1989) · Public history DB · Orientation: Academic biographical entry — https://solomonencyclopaedia.net/biogs/E000452b.htm

      'The Tensions' — Armed conflict on Guadalcanal and Malaita and the ouster of the Prime Minister in 2000 Civil War · 1998–2003

      The Solomon Islands Truth and Reconciliation Commission (TRC, established by a 2008 Act of Parliament) set its period of investigation from January 1, 1998 to July 23, 2003. The armed group on the Guadalcanal side (GRA/IFM, the Isatabu Freedom Movement) claimed greater autonomy for Guadalcanal and an influx of settlers, and in opposition to this the Malaita Eagle Force (MEF) was formed (TRC Summary 19). On June 5, 2000, MEF militants and police stormed the Rove police and prison armories in Honiara, seizing approximately 1,000 firearms; the TRC found that the police had assisted them. On June 6, the MEF placed Prime Minister Bartholomew Ulufala Kaukola under house arrest and demanded his resignation within 48 hours, declaring 'total war' against the IFM (TRC Summary 25, 47). The TRC concluded that opposition leader Manasseh Sogavare was not involved in the armory raids. The Townsville Peace Agreement was signed in Townsville, Australia, from October 9 to 15, 2000, but Harold Keke refused to sign and led the Guadalcanal Liberation Front (GLF) from Weathercoast. The GLF killed seven members of the Melanesian Brotherhood in 2003 and ten Kwaii militants in June 2002 (TRC Summary 29–30). On July 24, 2003, the Australia-led Regional Assistance Mission to Solomon Islands (RAMSI) arrived in Honiara. The TRC summary states that more than 200 people died, most of them civilians unconnected with the conflict, and that thousands were displaced. Records of the Return Commission listed approximately 18,000 registered refugees (TRC Summary 23). The Guadalcanal side claimed land and autonomy issues, while the Malaita side demanded compensation for settler damage — both positions are stated in parallel without adjudication.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Fatalities'Over 200' (TRC Summary, Solomon Islands Government posting)'At least 200', based on victim list (TRC Final Report Vol. 1 summary — confirmed only from search summaries)
      Displaced PersonsApproximately 18,000 registered (Return Commission remarks, TRC Summary 23)'Thousands' (TRC Summary 6)
      Weapons Seized on June 5, 2000Approximately 1,000 (TRC Summary 25)'Hundreds of high-caliber weapons' (TRC Summary different account on p. 23)

      Data Records: data/SLB.json contains no entries for COW civil war, UCDP armed conflict (state-based), or Powell & Thyne coups. Archigos 4.1 records Ulufala's departure on 2000-06-05 as 'ousted by rebels', but the TRC chronology dates the armory raid to June 5 and the house arrest to June 6, a one-day discrepancy. Because most of the fatalities were violence between non-state armed groups or against civilians, they appear not to be captured in state-based datasets.

      Honiara Riots — Election of Prime Minister Lini and the Burning of Chinatown Overthrow of the Regime · 2006

      When Snyder Rini was announced as the elected prime minister on April 18, 2006, many citizens protested, and two days of rioting and looting severely damaged Chinatown (Goldsmith & Dinnen 2007, summarized by the Nautilus Institute). About 50 police officers were injured in the riots, and New Zealand defense forces reported that most of Chinatown was destroyed, with 1,600 jobs lost (Nautilus Institute summary). Following the riots, approximately 500 Australian and New Zealand troops were deployed, and New Zealand increased its contingent from 46 to 125 (same source). According to Archigos 4.1, Rini's tenure ended on May 4, 2006 (the original text of his resignation is not reviewed). The second interim report of the government-established Commission of Inquiry (September 7, 2007) concluded that the unrest was 'not spontaneous as initially claimed.' Protesters emphasized vote-buying and foreign capital influence, while the government stressed planned violence — both accounts are stated in parallel.

      Honiara unrest — Protesters from Malaita and the burning of Chinatown Protest · 2021

      On November 24, 2021 (Wednesday), protests demanding the resignation of Prime Minister Manasseh Sogavare escalated into three days of large-scale unrest (RNZ Pacific, republished by AsiaPacificReport). CNN cited Reuters to report that most protesters were from Malaita, the most populous province, and that their grievances stemmed from resentment over the government's decision in 2019 to sever ties with Taiwan and establish diplomatic relations with China, as well as demands for the recognition of Malaita's right to self-determination and the resumption of development projects. On November 26, police announced the discovery of three bodies in a burned building in Chinatown (Solomon Islands Police), which local reports identified as trapped looters (RNZ). Over 100 people were arrested (CNN). The government declared a nightly curfew from 7:00 p.m. to 6:00 a.m., and at the request of the Solomon Islands government, Australian defense forces and police, and Papua New Guinea dispatched 20 police officers and 15 correctional personnel (CNN). An RNZ correspondent reported that only six buildings remained standing in Chinatown. Protesters emphasized relations with China and Malaita's marginalization, while the government stressed the restoration of law and order — both sides' positions are stated side by side.

      ArrestsOver 100 (police, CNN report)Unconfirmed
      Remaining Buildings in ChinatownUnconfirmed6 (RNZ correspondent)

      2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War
