      3. 1760–1815 — Literature

      Pemulwuy's resistance 1790–1802

      In December 1790, Bidjigal man Pemulwuy stabbed Governor Arthur Phillip's hunter John McIntyre with a spear, and McIntyre died of the wound (Australian Dictionary of Biography ADB). From 1792 onward he raided settlers in various areas; after raiding the government farm at Toongabbie in March 1797, he was pursued to the outskirts of Parramatta and wounded by musket fire. On May 1, 1801, Governor King ordered that settlers shoot any Aboriginal people seen near Parramatta, Georges River, or Prospect immediately; in November he proclaimed Pemulwuy a felon and put a price on his head. Around June 1, 1802, Henry Hacking shot him dead, and his head was severed. King wrote that the head had been 'preserved in spirits' and sent to Joseph Banks, but ADB notes that it has not yet been found in British collections. The colonial authorities described this as a response to settler raids; later Aboriginal accounts and subsequent scholarship describe it as resistance to land occupation — both narratives are presented side by side.

      1. Australian Dictionary of Biography, 'Pemulwuy (c.1750–1802)' · Public history DB · Orientation: Australian National University biographical dictionary (reliant on colonial records) — https://adb.anu.edu.au/biography/pemulwuy-13147

      Castle Hill Convict Uprising (Battle of Vinegar Hill) 1804

      On March 4–5, 1804, Irish convicts at Castle Hill rebelled, and the New South Wales Corps suppressed them (Dictionary of Sydney). The Dictionary of Sydney states that 12 rebels were killed in the suppression and 9 were later executed. The commander of the suppression was Captain George Johnston, and Philip Cunningham is mentioned as a leader (same source, plate description). Other accounts vary on the death toll, including figures of at least 39 convicts killed when combined with martial law punishments (see unverified items).

      1. Dictionary of Sydney, 'Castle Hill uprising / Battle of Vinegar Hill' (node 6519) · Public history DB · Orientation: Sydney local history dictionary (2021 snapshot) — https://dictionaryofsydney.org/node/6519

      Rum Rebellion — Arrest of Governor Bligh 1808–1810

      On January 26, 1808, Captain George Johnston of the New South Wales Corps arrested Governor William Bligh (ADB 'Bligh, William'). The petition asking Johnston to take charge had only nine signatories before the action, and Johnston claimed that 'rebellion and massacre' were imminent; ADB regards this as a grossly exaggerated claim. Bligh had issued orders in 1806–07 to suppress rum trading and illegal distilling, and he clashed with officers over lawsuits against John Macarthur and military interference. Macarthur persuaded the officers that Bligh's arrest was necessary to end 'cruel tyranny.' Bligh was confined in Sydney for more than a year; he left for Van Diemen's Land on March 17, 1809, and returned on January 17, 1810, by which time Governor Lachlan Macquarie had arrived. At the London court-martial, Johnston was found guilty but with a proviso that 'new and unusual circumstances' might partially mitigate the sentence (ADB). The rebels viewed it as redress against tyranny, while Bligh's side regarded it as an illegal rebellion — both perspectives are presented side by side.

      1. Australian Dictionary of Biography, 'Bligh, William (1754–1817)' · Public history DB · Orientation: Australian National University biographical dictionary (close to Bligh's interpretation) — https://adb.anu.edu.au/biography/bligh-william-1797

      5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

      Eureka Stockade Uprising Uprising · 1854

      At the Ballarat goldfields in the colony of Victoria, mining licenses cost '£1 per month or £2 for 3 months', and miners burned their licenses in protest (EurekaPedia, citing 19th-century Ballarat histories). On December 3, 1854 (Sunday), just before dawn, government troops attacked the miners' stockade, and the fighting lasted 25–30 minutes. Fourteen miners were killed at the scene or later from their wounds, twelve were wounded, and on the government side Captain Wise and three soldiers died, with one soldier dying later of his wounds (same source). Thirteen of those arrested were tried in Melbourne for treason and all were acquitted (Victoria Public Record Office VPRS 5527 description · Eureka Centre materials — confirmed via search summary; see unconfirmed items). A general amnesty for participants and 'liberal mining legislation' followed (EurekaPedia). The government viewed it as the suppression of an armed rebellion, while the miners saw it as a matter of taxation and representation — both sides' positions are stated side by side.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Miner DeathsUnconfirmed (original official Victorian government tally not consulted)14 dead, 12 wounded (EurekaPedia, 19th-century account)
      Military DeathsUnconfirmed1 officer and 3 soldiers + 1 who died later from wounds (EurekaPedia)
      Treason Trials13 prosecuted, all acquitted (Victorian courts — PROV VPRS 5527 description)Of 125 arrested, 13 were prosecuted (Eureka Centre materials — search summary)

      Data Record: As a colonial-era event, it falls outside the scope of COW, UCDP, and Archigos (no data/AUS.json entry).

      2. Research Data Australia, 'VPRS 5527 Eureka Historical Collection' (Public Record Office Victoria) · Public history DB · Orientation: record description from the Victorian State archives (government records) — https://researchdata.edu.au/vprs-5527-eureka-historical-collection/162056

      Vietnam War Moratorium Protest Protest · 1970

      On May 8, 1970, nationwide protests against Australia's involvement in the Vietnam War drew approximately 200,000 participants across major cities, and in Melbourne approximately 70,000 peacefully occupied the city center with the Treasury Gardens adjacent to Parliament House as their focal point (Australian War Memorial AWM). The AWM describes this as the largest public protest in Australia up to that time. Participants were predominantly young people, joined by clergy, teachers, academics, unionists, politicians, and students. Follow-up moratoriums were held in September 1970 and June 1971; the AWM notes that attendance declined. Protesters demanded an immediate withdrawal, while the government cited alliance obligations — both sides' positions are stated side by side.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      National Participants (1970.5.8)Unconfirmed (original police official estimate not consulted)Approximately 200,000 (AWM)
      Melbourne ParticipantsUnconfirmedApproximately 70,000 (AWM), approximately 100,000 (GreensLeft — left-leaning, search summary)

      Data Record: The Mass Mobilization dataset records only events from 1990 onward, and data/AUS.json has no MM entry. No NAVCO entry exists either.

      1. Australian War Memorial, 'Hell no, we won't go' (blog) · Public history DB · Orientation: Australian War Memorial (national institution) — https://www.awm.gov.au/articles/blog/blog/hell-no-we-wont-go

      Cronulla Riot and Retaliatory Attacks Protest · 2005

      On December 11, 2005, approximately 5,000 people gathered at Sydney's North Cronulla beach in response to text messages calling it 'the day of assaulting Lebanese and immigrants' (Monash Lens). The trigger was a dispute between lifeguards and young men of Middle Eastern background on the previous weekend. Beer bottles were thrown at police, and ambulances were dispatched for those assaulted on account of their 'Middle Eastern appearance.' In the following nights, groups of men of Middle Eastern background retaliated with vehicle convoys in the southern beach suburbs, causing property damage and violence. More than 50 people were charged (Monash Lens). The state parliament passed the Law Enforcement Legislation Amendment (Public Safety) Act 2005, which included emergency powers such as 'barricade powers.' Protesters claimed repeated humiliations on the beach, but police analyses subsequently did not support this claim (Monash Lens) — both sides' accounts are stated side by side.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      12.11 CrowdApproximately 5,000 (cited in NSW Police Strike Force Neil review — search summary)5,000 (Monash Lens)
      Charges FiledUnconfirmed (Strike Force Neil original not consulted)More than 50 (Monash Lens)

      Data Record: ACLED's Australian aggregation begins in 2021 (data/AUS.json), so the 2005 event falls outside its scope. No Mass Mobilization or NAVCO entries exist.

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      Tabiteuea Religious War Civil war · ca. 1879–1881

      In the 1860s, a syncretic faith combining local beliefs and Christian elements, known as 'Tioba' (meaning 'Yahweh'), emerged in southern Tabiteuea; conflict between converted northern Protestants and Tioba adherents developed into a religious war (the topic of Maude & Maude 1981). Hawaiian missionaries Kapu and Nalimu, who worked under the American Board of Commissioners for Foreign Missions (ABCFM), led most conversions in Tabiteuea; the Worldmark Encyclopedia of Religious Practices entry on Kiribati states that they led a religious war against so-called 'pagans' (Tioba adherents) in southern Tabiteuea in 1881, converting them. According to the same entry, thousands of southern Tabiteueans died during the war, and their land was seized by northern leaders and Hawaiian leaders; this war permanently altered land ownership between Tabiteuea Meang (north) and Maian (south). The war years (1879/1881) and the scale of fatalities differ across sources (unverified reference), and accounts from the Tioba adherent side are not found in reviewed sources.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
      FatalitiesUnconfirmed (no state apparatus at the time)Thousands of southern Tabiteueans (Worldmark Encyclopedia of Religious Practices, 2nd ed., vol. 3, 2014, Kiribati entry)
      Land OutcomesUnconfirmedSouthern land seized by northern and Hawaiian leaders (same entry)

      Data record: no Kiribati entries in COW, UCDP, Powell & Thyne, Archigos, NAVCO, or Mass Mobilization (data/KIR.json). In 1879, the Gilbert Islands were outside state systems and thus fall outside the COW definition of war.

      End of inter-island warfare and declaration of British protectorate Civil war · 1892

      According to the RNZ's Kiribati chronology, inter-island raids, killings, and invasions caused by land competition were frequent in the Gilbert Islands before 1892; after the crew of HMS Royalist ended a battle in 1892, Britain declared the Gilbert Islands a protectorate, and subsequent fighting and hostilities ceased. The dates and casualties of individual wars are not presented in the sources consulted.

      ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
      DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

      Data recorded: inter-island warfare during the period of state transfer falls outside the definitions of COW and UCDP. Not included in any modern dataset.

      1. RNZ, 'Kiribati — a brief history' (Pacific collection) · Media · Orientation: New Zealand public broadcaster (reflecting an 'objective' account from the British colonial perspective) — https://www.rnz.co.nz/collections/nff-kiribati/about-kiribati

      Phosphorus mining protests, litigation, and demands for autonomy on Banaba Protests · 1960s–1979

      On Banaba Island (Ocean Island), the British colonial administration granted Pacific Islands Company a monopoly on mining in 1900; Banaba was incorporated into the protectorate only in 1916 (RNZ). From 1919, the British Phosphate Commission (BPC) of three countries—Britain, Australia, and New Zealand—conducted the mining, and approximately 22 million tons were extracted during the 20th century (Chau Chak Wing Museum, University of Sydney). After Japanese occupation and wartime internment, 1,003 Banabans and Gilbert Islanders were forcibly relocated to Rabi Island in Fiji in 1945. Throughout the 1970s, the Banabans protested the mining and its effects; protest marches led by dancers proceeded alongside legal litigation. The Banabans brought lawsuits against the British government and the BPC (Tito v Waddell, judgment of Justice Megarry), and in 1979 they demanded sovereignty and independence from Britain and the Gilbert Islands colony, protesting the mining. Toward the end of the 1970s, a small number returned to Banaba and remained on ancestral land (University of Sydney Museum). The independence of the Gilbert Islands was delayed by the Banabans' demand for an expanded share of mining revenues and by the issue of parliamentary representation; after these two issues were resolved in favor of the Banabans, Kiribati became independent in 1979. In 2006, the Rabi Island Banabans demanded that Banaba be separated from Kiribati and annexed to Fiji (RNZ). Accounts diverge between the Banaban side (demanding sovereignty and compensation) and the British/BPC side (defendants in litigation), and no determination is made.

      ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations & other
      Mining volumeUnverified (BPC official statistics not cross-checked)Approximately 22 million tons (Chau Chak Wing Museum, University of Sydney)
      First settlers on Rabi IslandUnconfirmed1,003 Banabans and Gilbert Islanders (Chau Chak Wing Museum, University of Sydney)

      Data record: none of NAVCO 1.3, Mass Mobilization, or ACLED (Kiribati 2021–; 0 protests) includes the Banaba protests — a coverage gap for small states and colonial periods.

      2. RNZ, 'Kiribati — a brief history' (Pacific collection) · Media · Orientation: New Zealand public broadcaster (New Zealand was one of the three BPC countries) — https://www.rnz.co.nz/collections/nff-kiribati/about-kiribati

      5. Modern Events through Literature

      Public Service Strike and Democracy Marches Protests · 2005

      In 2005, Tongan public servants went on strike for six weeks against a new pay structure based on performance bonuses. The government agreed to a 60–80% wage increase on September 3, and the memorandum of understanding included a two-year moratorium on disciplinary action against strikers and a review of the remuneration that had triggered the strike. The Public Service Association (PSA) demanded the immediate establishment of a royal commission to review the constitution, but the government refused and agreed only to refer democratization demands to cabinet. On September 6, approximately 10,000 people gathered in front of the Royal Palace; reports described this as the largest protest in Tongan history. The petition demanded the dismissal of Prime Minister Prince Ulukalala Lavaka Ata and 14 ministers, the establishment of a royal commission to review the constitution within 12 months, and the return of government assets taken by the royal family. When Parliament (Fale Alea) voted for a two-week recess on September 9, nine elected representatives opposed it (reported by Green Left Weekly). The government's account was not directly quoted from sources consulted.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
      Wage Increase Agreement60–80% (Government-PSA memorandum of understanding, 2005.09.03 — reported)60~80%(Green Left Weekly 2005.09.21)
      Scale of the Palace ProtestUnconfirmedApprox. 10,000 (Green Left Weekly 2005.09.21)

      Data record: corresponds to NAVCO 1.3 'Pro-Democracy Protests' (2005–2006, nonviolent, regime change objective, partial success). Mass Mobilization is not recorded for Tonga.

      1. Beechey, Bronwen & Martin, Andrew, 'TONGA: Strike ends, but fight for democracy continues', Green Left Weekly 643 (2005.09.21) · Media · Orientation: (Left-wing/socialist-leaning) Australian weekly — perspective of the striking side — https://greenleft.org.au/node/41558

      Nuku'alofa unrest on 11·16 Uprising · 2006

      On November 16, 2006 (Thursday), a protest demanding democratization in the capital Nuku'alofa escalated into a violent outbreak that burned down the central commercial district. The incident occurred two months after King Tupou IV's death and the accession of a prince (ABC). Initial reports of fatalities numbered eight. RNZ (11.17) reported that six bodies (suspected looters) appeared to have been trapped by an LPG tank explosion during arson and were found among the ruins of Shoreline power company, while two were found in a supermarket owned by Prime Minister Felelei Sevele (ABC 2006.11.19). Matangi Tonga (11.22) reported that police had corrected the number of bodies discovered five days after the incident from eight to six. Approximately 150 personnel from Australia (about 50 defense force and 34 police) and New Zealand (about 60 defense and police) were deployed to support the enforcement of martial law in the capital (ABC 11.19). MP Akilisi Pohiva of the democratization movement stated that the intervention demonstrated the failure of an unelected government, characterized the deaths and destruction as 'a regrettable part' of democratic change, and demanded the dissolution of parliament and a transitional administration (ABC). King George Tupou V stated that the looters would be punished (RNZ). In 2018, a motion to establish a committee to investigate the unrest was submitted to parliament, but the Speaker did not respond, and the first repayment of the Chinese loan for reconstruction (119 million US dollars) took place in September 2018 (Matangi Tonga). The accounts from the government side (restoration of order and punishment) and the democratization movement side (government responsibility) are presented side by side without adjudication.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      Fatalities6 (Tonga Police, reported 2006.11.22 in Matangi Tonga)8 (RNZ 2006.11.17 — Shoreline 6, supermarket owned by the Prime Minister 2; ABC 2006.11.19 '8')
      Foreign Deployed TroopsAustralia: approx. 50 defense force and 34 police; New Zealand: approx. 60 (cited in ABC 2006.11.19)Approx. 150 (ABC 2006.11.19)

      Data Record: The event corresponds to the concluding phase of the NAVCO 1.3 campaign (2005–2006), but because NAVCO is organized by campaign, fatalities are not recorded separately. Not included in UCDP, COW, or Powell & Thyne (fall outside their definitions of state vs. non-state armed conflict and coups). The ACLED Tonga collection begins in 2021 and falls outside the scope.

      5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

      The Movement for Separation of the Ellice Islands and the 1974 Referendum Protests · 1960s–1978

      After the United Kingdom incorporated the Ellice Islands into the Gilbert and Ellice Islands Protectorate (subsequently a colony) in 1892, many Tuvaluans migrated to the Gilbert Islands after World War II in search of education and employment. As their share of public office grew, tensions and competition between Micronesian Gilbertese (I-Kiribati) and Polynesian Ellice Islanders (Tuvaluans) intensified, and most Ellice Islanders supported separation and independence (RNZ summary). In the 1974 referendum, a preference for separation was clearly expressed, and Tuvalu became an autonomous territory with its own constitution in 1975 before gaining full independence in 1978 (RNZ summary). McIntyre (2012) treats this separation as one of the few boundary changes in decolonization achieved by mutual agreement. No violent clashes or casualties are reported in the sources consulted, and the demand for separation was resolved through the institutional channel of a referendum.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
      Results of the 1974 ReferendumUnconfirmed (original British colonial authority tally not verified against text)'A clear preference for separation' (RNZ summary; figures not stated)
      CasualtiesNone reportedNo record of violent clashes in sources consulted

      Data records: no Tuvalu entries in COW, UCDP, Powell & Thyne, Archigos, or NAVCO (this pipeline data/TUV.json). Because it was a nonviolent separation resolved through the referendum channel, it is not included even under the NAVCO campaign definition (large-scale nonviolent resistance movements).

      1. RNZ, 'Tuvalu - a brief history' (Pacific collection) · Media · Orientation: New Zealand public broadcaster (Pacific collection overview article) — https://www.rnz.co.nz/collections/nff-tuvalu/about-tuvalu
      2. McIntyre, W. David (2012). The Partition of the Gilbert and Ellice Islands. Island Studies Journal 7(1): 135–146 · Journal Article · Orientation: Decolonization academic journal (based on British colonial administrative documents) — https://islandstudiesjournal.org/article/81307-the-partition-of-the-gilbert-and-ellice-islands

      2013 Constitutional Crisis (Governor's Dismissal of Prime Minister and No-Confidence Vote) Regime Overthrow · 2012–2013

      The Telavi (Willy Telavi) government did not convene parliament for approximately eight months following the death of a minister, and the opposition alleged that the government was seeking to avoid a vote of no confidence (RNZ 2013.08.05). On July 31 (Wednesday), Speaker Kamuta Latasi suspended parliament until a by-election on the grounds of Health Minister Taom Tanualeke's resignation from his seat, preventing the opposition from bringing its motion of no confidence to the floor (ABC 2013.08.01). On August 1, Governor-General Iakoba Italeli dismissed Prime Minister Telavi and appointed opposition leader Enele Sopoaga as acting prime minister by proclamation; Telavi resisted by ordering the dismissal of the governor-general, placing the prime minister and governor-general in a situation where each sought to dismiss the other (ABC 2013.08.01, RNZ 2013.08.02). On August 2 (Friday), parliament voted 8–4–1 to pass the motion of no confidence (8 for, 4 against, 1 abstention, Speaker abstaining), and Sopoaga was elected as the new prime minister (ABC 2013.08.02). The Ministry of Justice referred the legality of the prime minister's dismissal of the governor-general and the governor-general's dismissal of the prime minister to the Chief Justice for determination (RNZ 2013.08.05). Telavi argued that under the constitution, parliament need only meet within twelve months and that the governor-general has no such suspensive power (RNZ 2013.08.07), while opposition legislator Finikaso asserted that if the government and the speaker do not abide by the constitution, reliance must be placed on the judgment of the governor-general, as the Queen's representative (RNZ 2013.08.02) — both accounts are reported side by side. No reports of violence or mobilization were found in confirmed reporting.

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
      Vote of No ConfidenceFor 8, Against 4, Abstaining 1 (Tuvalu Parliament, 15 seats)Same figure (ABC 2013.08.02, citing opposition legislator Finikaso)
      Period without Convening ParliamentUnconfirmed (Government claims that the 'within twelve months' constitutional requirement was met)Approximately 8 months (RNZ 2013.08.05, ABC 2013.08.02)
      CasualtiesNone reportedNo reports of violence or casualties in confirmed reporting

      Data Record: Archigos does not record the leader change as an abnormal transition; Powell & Thyne do not record a coup — because the change occurred through constitutional procedures (the governor-general's suspensive power and parliamentary no-confidence), it falls outside the definitions of coup and abnormal transition. This category, 'regime overthrow,' is a convenience classification on the same criteria as the description of Kiribati's 2021 constitutional crisis.

      2. ABC News (2013.08.02), 'Tuvalu parliament elects new prime minister' · Media · Affiliation: Australian public broadcaster (citing opposition legislator) — https://www.abc.net.au/news/2013-08-02/an-tuvalu-parliament-votes-to-remove-pm-telavi/4861930

      4. RNZ (2013.08.05), 'Legality of new Tuvalu government hinges on court decision' · Media · Affiliation: New Zealand public broadcaster — https://www.rnz.co.nz/news/pacific/219478/legality-of-new-tuvalu-government-hinges-on-court-decision

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      Bougainville Conflict (BRA secessionist armed struggle) Civil War · 1987/1988–1998

      The Bougainville conflict began in 1987 as a dispute over land compensation involving Bougainville Copper Ltd, an Australian subsidiary, and soon revealed the fundamental issue of independence, escalating into armed conflict between the Papua New Guinea government and the Bougainville Revolutionary Army (BRA), representing the island's residents (PA-X compilation). Regan (2010) characterizes this conflict as a 'secessionist conflict with secession' that destabilized Papua New Guinea and Pacific island territories for most of 1988–1997, analyzing a peace process driven by the parties involving 'light international intervention.' After several rounds of pre-negotiation agreements, they reached an agreement on a 'permanent and irrevocable' ceasefire and a framework for normalizing relations between Papua New Guinea and Bougainville (including elections) (PA-X). In 1990, when the Namaliu government negotiated a ceasefire with the BRA and sought to withdraw the defense forces deployed after the 1989 uprising, then-Chief of Police Paul Toghi protested and appealed for the overthrow of the government (Wall 2020, see the subsequent coup entry).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
      DeathsUnconfirmed (original Papua New Guinea government tally not cross-checked)Estimated at approximately 20,000 (PA-X, University of Edinburgh peace agreement database); COW combat deaths: 1,000 (only the 1990–1991 interval)
      Conflict PeriodUnconfirmed1987–1998 (PA-X), 1988–1997 (Regan 2010), 6 active years 1990–1996 (UCDP), 1990–1991 (COW), 1988–1998 (NAVCO)

      Data record: COW civil war is recorded as 1990–1991 (combat deaths 1,000), UCDP as 1990–1996 (territorial dispute, 6 active years), and NAVCO 1.3 as the 1988–1998 'Bougainville rebellion', but the objective is coded as regime change rather than a territorial dispute (disputed_territory false) — differing from the PA-X and UCDP descriptions of an independence demand. The estimated death toll of approximately 20,000 is a figure in a different dimension from the COW combat deaths (1,000), reflecting differences over whether non-combatant deaths are included.

      1. PA-X Peace Agreements Database (University of Edinburgh), 'Bougainville Conflict (1987 - 1998)' · public history DB · orientation: academic peace agreement database — https://www.peaceagreements.org/conflict/40
      2. Regan, Anthony J. (2010). Light Intervention: Lessons from Bougainville. Washington DC: United States Institute of Peace Press (ANU Bell School introduction) · monograph · orientation: scholar who participated in peace process consultations (ANU), published by the US Institute of Peace — https://bellschool.anu.edu.au/dpa/content-centre/research/light-intervention-lessons-bougainville

      March 1990 'coup' attempt by Police Commissioner Tohian Coup · 1990

      When the Namaliu government sought to negotiate a ceasefire with the Bougainville Revolutionary Army and withdraw the defense forces deployed to Bougainville after the 1989 uprising, Police Commissioner Paul Tohian, who had been in charge of the defense forces and police during the state of emergency, strongly opposed this. His position was overturned by the government. He appealed to the defense forces and police to join in overthrowing the government, but he won little support from either commanders or rank-and-file soldiers, and the attempt ended within hours (Wall 2020). Although Wall describes it as the only 'coup' attempt in Papua New Guinea's history, he adds the caveat 'if one can call it that.' The author was an adviser to Prime Minister Namaliu at the time.

      ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigation · other
      DurationUnconfirmedSeveral hours (Wall 2020)
      CasualtiesUnconfirmedNot recorded in sources checked

      Data record: Powell & Thyne coup data records it as 'failed' on 1990-03-14. No Archigos abnormal transfer of power (government retained).

      1. Wall, Jeffrey (2020.03.17), 'Protecting democracy in Papua New Guinea', The Strategist (ASPI) · media · orientation: article for the Australian Strategic Policy Institute, author was an adviser to former Prime Minister Namaliu (account of a party involved) — https://www.aspistrategist.org.au/protecting-democracy-in-papua-new-guinea/

      2012 mutiny by Captain Sasa (Sogavare–O'Neill dual prime minister standoff) Coup · 2011–2012

      In August 2011, while Michael Somare was receiving treatment in Singapore, a vacancy in his seat was declared and he was replaced by Peter O'Neill; in December, the Supreme Court ordered Somare's return as prime minister and member of parliament, but O'Neill, who enjoyed the support of civil servants, police, defense forces, and many MPs, retained the de facto premiership (ABC 2012.01.26). Around 3:00 a.m. on January 26, 2012, a group of soldiers overpowered the guards at Tauroma Barracks, detained their commander, and proceeded to headquarter headquarters, where they placed Defense Forces Commander Francis Agwai under house arrest. Retired Colonel Yaura Sasa claimed that Somare had appointed him commander and asserted that his action was 'not a coup but an intervention in defense of the Constitution and the judiciary'; he demanded that both sides return to parliament within seven days and comply with the Supreme Court order. Deputy Prime Minister Belden Namah stated that 15 of the approximately 30 soldiers supporting Sasa had been arrested, and that Sasa's actions constituted treason (ABC 2012.01.26) — both accounts are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigation · other
      Number of participating troops15 of approximately 30 arrested (announced by Deputy Prime Minister Namah)Unconfirmed
      CasualtiesUnconfirmedNo fatalities recorded in reports checked (Sasa's side acknowledged firing several shots upon entering the barracks)

      Data record: Powell & Thyne records it as 'failed' on 2012-01-26. No Archigos abnormal transfer of power — the August 2011 Somare→O'Neill change (vacancy declared) is also not listed in Archigos abnormal transfers.

      1. ABC News (2012.01.26), 'Rebel soldiers stage mutiny in PNG' · media · orientation: Australian public broadcaster (citing both Sasa and Namah) — https://www.abc.net.au/news/2012-01-26/military-mutiny-underway-in-png/3794314

      2016 University of Papua New Guinea student protests and police firing Protest · 2016

      Students had been protesting for five weeks against Prime Minister Peter O'Neill's handling of corruption allegations and economic management. On June 8, 2016, approximately 1,000 students moved toward a peaceful protest outside parliament but were blocked by police; after police demanded the arrest of the student union president, students refused, and police then fired directly into the crowd, according to student accounts. The government advisory panel stated that it was unclear whether the shots were warning shots or direct fire. Subsequently, riots and looting were reported in Goroka, Mount Hagen, and Lae (ABC/Reuters 2016.06.08). O'Neill said that there were opposition figures with political motives behind the protests and that 'the blood of injured students is on their hands,' while promising to investigate the underlying reasons for the students' grievances (ABC 2016.06.08) — both accounts are presented side by side.

      ItemOfficial announcementIndependent investigation · other
      Injuries5 admitted to hospital, all stable (Papua New Guinea government)At least 17 injured, 10 admitted to Port Moresby General Hospital (ABC/Reuters 2016.06.08)
      DeathsDeath claims are false (Papua New Guinea government)Opposition MP claimed 4 deaths in parliament (ABC report, unconfirmed)

      Data record: Recorded in NAVCO 1.3 'Anti-PM Protest' (2016, nonviolent, aimed at regime change, failed). Mass Mobilization: 1 event in 2016 (protestor violence 1).

      1. ABC News/Reuters (2016.06.08), 'PNG police shoot students during anti-government protest' · Media · Lean: Australian public broadcaster and wire service joint (citing both government and student sides) — https://www.abc.net.au/news/2016-06-08/shooting-on-papua-new-guinea-university-campus/7489416

      January 2024 'Black Thursday' Port Moresby and Lae riots Uprising · 2024

      On January 10, 2024 (Wednesday), hundreds of public servants, including police, soldiers, and prison officers, staged a work stoppage over salary issues and held protests outside the National Parliament. The government explained that a salary error had resulted in a deduction of 300 kina (approximately 120 Australian dollars, roughly half the salary of lower-grade public servants) from two weeks' pay, and the Tax Administration said this was an 'error' in the payroll system and denied reports of a new tax. Immediately after the police strike, arson, looting, and gunfire occurred in Port Moresby and Lai. Prime Minister Marape declared a state of emergency for 14 days on January 11, placed 1,000 defense personnel on standby, and suspended the Commissioner of Police and senior officials from the Ministry of Finance and Treasury. Marape said 'organized arsonists' and 'errant elements within the police' were involved, while the National Capital District Governor Paraka said security forces had incited the looting (ABC 2024.01.12). Counter-narratives regarding the government's claim of 'organizational intervention' could not be verified in this cycle (unverified).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
      FatalitiesUnconfirmed (original government official tally unverified against source text)22 — Port Moresby 15, Lai 7 (ABC 2024.01.12)
      Deduction from Salary300 kina, to be refunded in the next pay cycle (government and Tax Administration explanation of 'payroll error')Same figure (ABC 2024.01.12)

      Data record: An event included in ACLED Papua New Guinea's 55 protests, 204 political violence events, and 325 fatalities in 2024 (annual totals, including inter-tribal violence). The annual 2024 figures cannot be separated for this single event.

      3. 1760–1815 — Literature

      The Wreck of the Antelope and Rival Chiefdoms in Koror and Melekeok 1783

      In August 1783, the British East India Company packet ship Antelope (Captain Henry Wilson) wrecked on Palau's coral reefs. This account is transmitted through George Keate's An Account of the Present State of the Island of Pulo de'aino (London, 1788), based on the journals of Wilson and his officers (Wellcome Collection bibliographic record). The crew spent approximately three months rebuilding the ship with the help of the Palauans, while the Palauan side received firearms in exchange for food and resources. At the time, there were two rival chiefdoms in Palau: Koror under Ibedul and Melekeok under Reklai. Subsequently, many Westerners who drifted ashore became affiliated with one or the other chief and served as 'interpreters to the chiefs' in reality as military strategists and harbor guides (Guampedia; Bruce Campbell). The account in Keate's book concerning direct participation of Antelope crew members in a Koror expedition could not be verified against the original text.

      1. Keate, George (1788). An Account of the Pelew Islands … composed from the journals and communications of Captain Henry Wilson. London: G. Nicol — Wellcome Collection bibliographic record · Source material (18th-century publication record) · Orientation: based on British seafarer's log (perspective of the contact party) — https://wellcomecollection.org/works/aq2yn2f3
      2. Campbell, Bruce L., 'Adventurers: Beachcombers', Guampedia · Public history encyclopedia (Guam Humanities Council affiliate) · Orientation: introductory public history of the region — https://www.guampedia.com/beachcombers/

      5. Modern Events as Recorded in the Literature

      Assassination of First President Remeliik Regime Overthrow · 1985

      First President Haruo Remeliik of the Republic of Palau was shot three times by an unidentified gunman shortly after stepping out of his car and walking toward his home in Koror early on the morning of June 30, 1985, and died approximately one hour later at Koror Hospital (UPI 1985.06.30; announcement by the Palau Guam Liaison Office). At the time, no political leaders had assigned security details. Vice President Alfonso Oiteleong was in New York for a UN meeting, so Attorney General Thomas Remengesau served as acting president, and no state of emergency was declared. On July 20, four men—Melwort Temaituel, son of Governor Arale's father-in-law Roman Temaituel; Leslie Nisang, the governor's nephew; Angelio Sadino; and Francisco Guipan—were arrested and charged with murder and conspiracy to commit murder. Police stated that the crime had been 'planned for quite some time,' and the motive was not established (UPI 1985.07.21). A special presidential election was scheduled for August 28. The International Commission of Jurists (ICJ) 1988 report cited this assassination as one of the constitutional crises that Palau had experienced. Subsequent trial outcomes (a guilty verdict followed by an acquittal on appeal, and a guilty verdict in the 1990s for another individual) could not be verified from primary sources in this investigation.

      Arrests and Charges4 men charged with murder and conspiracy to commit murder (Palau Police; UPI 1985.07.21)Unconfirmed (court records not cross-checked)
      InterrogationsUnconfirmedOver 100 (Palau Police, FBI, Trust Territory Public Safety Bureau; UPI 1985.07.21)

The date of the assassination differs among sources: UPI's June 30 report states 'just after midnight on Saturday,' its July 21 report states 'June 29,' and the ICJ 1988 report states 'July 1985.'

      Repeated Referendums on the Compact of Free Association and the 1987 'Dismissed Workers Committee' Sit-Ins and Violence Protests · 1983–1987

      The 1979 Palauan Constitution prohibited the introduction of nuclear, chemical, and biological weapons without the approval of 75% of voters. The Compact of Free Association with the United States was therefore put to a vote five times: on February 10, 1983 (Option A 62%, Option B 52%), September 1984 (66%), February 1986 (72%), November–December 1986 (65.97%, proclaimed by President Salii), and June 1987 (67%) — in none of these votes did the Compact reach 75% (ICJ 1988). In early 1987, government spending exceeded revenue by more than $5 million, prompting the government to reduce working hours from 40 to 32 hours per week and to place approximately 900 of 1,331 national civil servants on unpaid leave from July 8 to October 1. The 'Furlough Committee,' formed by the furloughed workers, encamped tents around the Capitol building and demanded legislation lowering the 75% requirement to a simple majority, arguing that the government's explanation — that ratification would increase US subsidies and resolve the problem — was false. On July 23, 1987, House Speaker Santos Olisang testified before a US House subcommittee that he had voted in favor 'because our lives were at stake.' The bill was signed on July 19, and both the constitutional amendment vote (5,645 votes in favor, 73.33%) on August 4 and the sixth compact vote (5,964 votes in favor, 73.04%) on August 21 passed by a simple majority (proclaimed by President Salii). In response, 22 plaintiffs, including female elders, filed a constitutional challenge (Ngirmang v. Salii), and between September 4 and 7, wire-cutting occurred at the homes of the plaintiffs and their attorneys, shots were fired at the home of the House Speaker and one plaintiff, and a Molotov cocktail exploded in front of the home of plaintiff Gabriela Ngirmang. On the night of September 7, the father of plaintiff Toshi Kelderman (the father of attorney Roman Bedor) was shot twice by a masked man outside his son's law office and died in hospital. On September 8, all plaintiffs signed a withdrawal, and Judge Hefner recorded that the withdrawal might have been coerced through violence (ICJ 1988, reproduced). The ICJ investigative mission concluded that the withdrawal was not voluntary. The mission's report also included an appendix containing a petition submitted by the Furlough Committee — both the government and the Furlough Committee maintained that ratification would resolve the fiscal crisis, whereas the plaintiffs contested a violation of constitutional procedure.

      5. Modern Events through the Literature

      May and September 1987 Coups (Rabuka) Coup · 1987

      About one month after the Fiji Labour Party–National Federation Party coalition narrowly won the April 1987 general election, ushering in the government of Timothy Bavadra, on May 14, 1987, at 10:04 a.m., Major Nepeveni Rabuka, the third-ranking military officer, led masked soldiers into Parliament and declared a 'military takeover' (Pacific Media Review 2012, citing parliamentary records and Scarr 1988). ConstitutionNet described this as a 'bloodless military coup', and ABC reported that no one was killed. The military explained the action at a press conference as a measure to prevent violence by armed groups; the Fiji Times suspended publication from May 16 for a month, resuming with controlled reporting before closing down in protest against censorship (Pacific Media Review 2012). Rabuka ousted Elizabeth II as head of state and proclaimed a republic later that year through a second coup (ABC 2024). ABC dates the second coup to 'September 1987', while ConstitutionNet dates it to 'October 1987'. The 1990 constitution required the president and prime minister to be indigenous Fijians and reserved 37 of 70 seats for them (ConstitutionNet). The scholar Brij Lal saw the electoral defeat of the governing Alliance Party in 1970–1987 as the primary cause of the coups (cited in Pacific Media Review 2012).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
      DeathsUnconfirmed0 (ABC 2024: 'No one died'), 'bloodless' (ConstitutionNet)
      Time of Parliament OccupationRecorded in parliamentary records (cited in Pacific Media Review 2012)1987.05.14 10:04 (Robie 1989, cited in Pacific Media Review 2012)

      Data Records: Powell & Thyne records only the May 14, 1987 coup as 'successful' and notes no second coup that year. Archigos records Bavadra's irregular exit from office at the hands of the military on 1987-05-14 and Rabuka's tenure from 1987-05-14 to 1987-12-05. NAVCO separately records a 'Movement against the Coalition Government' (violent, successful) and an 'Indian anti-coup movement' (nonviolent, failed) for 1987 (data/FJI.json).

      The May 2000 occupation of Parliament and the November military mutiny Coup · 2000

      Approximately one year after Mahendra Chaudhry, the first Indo-Fijian prime minister, took office in 1999, on May 19, 2000, George Speight and an armed gang occupied Parliament in Suva and held the cabinet hostage for 56 days, excluding Prime Minister Chaudhry and one minister (ABC 2024, ConstitutionNet). The occupying forces demanded the repeal of the 1997 constitution, and a record series published by the ANU Press notes that Suva suffered arson and looting, making this the third coup in approximately 13 years. On May 29, President Mara stepped down, and the military led by Frank Bainimarama seized power and abrogated the constitution (ConstitutionNet). During this period, law and order broke down, and rape, assault, and arson targeted Indo-Fijian residents (ABC 2024). Speight was arrested at a military roadblock in late July, pleaded guilty to treason in February 2002 and was sentenced to death, but President Iloilo commuted the sentence to life imprisonment on the same day, and he was released from prison in September 2024 under a presidential pardon (ABC 2024). On November 2, 2002, soldiers at Queen Elizabeth Barracks in Suva staged a mutiny seeking the replacement of Commander Bainimarama. Amnesty International reported that three soldiers were killed and four arrested men died from beatings (2002), while New Zealand RNZ noted that these retaliatory killings went unpunished (2020). The ringleader, Warrant Officer Shane Stevens, received a life sentence at a military trial in 2002.

NAVCO classifies the 'anti-Chaudhry movement' (2000) as 'non-violent · successful.' The UCDP armed conflict dataset contains no entry for Fiji.

      The 2006 Bainimarama Coup and the 2009 Abrogation of the Constitution Coup · 2006–2009

      In October–November 2006, the conflict between Prime Minister Laisenia Qarase and Commander Voreqe Bainimarama intensified; Bainimarama set a deadline of December 4 and demanded the repeal of the 2000 coup-related legislation and other conditions (ConstitutionNet). The UN Security Council expressed concern on November 29 in a press statement (SC/8881). On December 5, Bainimarama took over the Qarase government militarily, having won two consecutive general elections; on December 6, the military announced that it had taken control of the government. The Security Council expressed 'serious concern' in a second statement on December 7 (SC/8894), and the Commonwealth suspended Fiji from its councils. On January 4, 2007, the military transferred executive power to former President Ratu Sir Kamisese Mara, and on January 5, Mara appointed Bainimarama as acting prime minister. When the Court of Appeal ruled on April 9, 2009 that the dismissal of the 2006 government and the transitional administration were unlawful, President Mara suspended the constitution, dissolved the Court of Appeal, declared a state of emergency, and reappointed Bainimarama as acting prime minister on April 10. Elections were postponed to 2014. The UN Secretary-General demanded an immediate reversal of the measures, and the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights expressed deep concern over press censorship and the dismissal of judges (Security Council Report 2009.04.17). A new constitution was promulgated on September 6, 2013 (ConstitutionNet).

      ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

      DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no mention of casualties in sources examined)

      Data record: Powell & Thyne 2006-12-05 successful coup, Archigos Karase 2006-12-05 removed from office by the military, consistent. Archigos records Bainimarama's tenure as beginning on 2007-01-05 and ending 'in office' on 2015-12-31 (data collection terminated). NAVCO and UCDP have no entry for 2006.

      3. Campbell, I. C. (1980). The Historiography of Charles Savage. Journal of the Polynesian Society 89(2): 143–166. Link — Review of historiography surrounding accounts of the Bau chieftaincy and the introduction of firearms in 1808–1813 (only abstract from search results confirmed; access to full text failed).

      7. Data Sources

      1. Powell, Jonathan M. & Clayton L. Thyne (2011). Global instances of coups from 1950 to 2010: A new dataset. Journal of Peace Research 48(2): 249–259.
      2. Goemans, Henk E., Kristian Skrede Gleditsch & Giacomo Chiozza (2009). Introducing Archigos: A Dataset of Political Leaders. Journal of Peace Research 46(2): 269–283.
      3. Chenoweth, Erica & Christopher Wiley Shay (2020). List of Campaigns in NAVCO 1.3. Harvard Dataverse, doi:10.7910/DVN/ON9XND. / Chenoweth, Erica & Maria J. Stephan (2011). Why Civil Resistance Works: The Strategic Logic of Nonviolent Conflict. New York: Columbia University Press.
      4. Raleigh, Clionadh, Andrew Linke, Håvard Hegre & Joakim Karlsen (2010). Introducing ACLED: An Armed Conflict Location and Event Dataset. Journal of Peace Research 47(5): 651–660.

      Items omitted for lack of verification: exact date of the second 1987 coup (commonly reported as September 25): conflicting between ABC 'September' and ConstitutionNet 'October', recorded only by month.; Charles Savage and the Bau War (1808–1813): excluded from narrative due to unverified original text.; hostage numbers in 2000 (various reports: 27, over 30, 36, etc.): figures omitted from narrative.; date of Commonwealth suspension after the 2006 coup (reported as December 8): Security Council Report records only 'late 2006', omitting the date.; events prior to independence, such as the Suva strike and riots in 1959: not investigated due to time limit for this round.

      Scope of Application of Six-Layer Discourse — The narrative of this monograph series spans from Layer 1 (data and records) to Layer 5 (interpretations through prior research). It does not provide prescriptions, moral evaluations, leader assessments, or predictions. Layer 6 is reserved exclusively for Cheonghae (淸海).
